### [Acoustic Firewalls: Analogue Gravity Perspective on the AMPS Paradox](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.14056v1)

**Authors:** Nikolay S. Akintsov, Artyom P. Nevecheria, Stepan N. Andreev, Qing-Hua Qin  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum gases · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.14056v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** 🔥 `entanglement & information structure` **5/5** · 🔥 `analog quantum simulation` **4/5** · `non-equilibrium dynamics` **2/5** · `non-equilibrium universality` **2/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **1/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **1/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **1/5**

**Summary.** The paper translates the theoretical AMPS paradox concerning black hole information loss into an analogue system using phonons in a Bose-Einstein condensate. By applying entanglement principles across an artificial sonic horizon, the authors predict that post-Page time unitarity forces a measurable depletion of phonon energy density. This predicted signal, scaling as $(\ell_\kappa/\delta r)^2$, offers a concrete, falsifiable test for quantum gravity concepts in cold atom experiments.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly applies advanced concepts of quantum information (entanglement monogamy) and black hole physics into a highly controllable, experimentally accessible platform like BECs, providing a concrete testable prediction for AMO physicists.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the AMPS paradox, which highlights an inconsistency between unitarity, local physics at the horizon, and smooth vacuum evolution during black hole evaporation.

**Main result.** It predicts a falsifiable signature—a specific depletion ratio $R(\delta r) \propto (\ell_\kappa/\delta r)^2$ in phonon energy density—that should appear after an analogue Page time in a BEC.

**Method.** The authors transcribe the AMPS argument to an acoustic system, using entanglement monogamy identities and calculating differences between stress tensors for different vacuum states (Hadamard vs. Boulware-like).

**Model / system.** The model uses analogue gravity realized via a sonic horizon in a Bose-Einstein condensate (BEC), where linearized phonons obey the Klein-Gordon equation in an acoustic metric.

**Key observables.** A dimensionless, differential phonon-calorimetry signal $R(\delta r) = |\Delta\mathcal{E}|/\mathcal{E}^{(0)}$, predicted to scale as $(\ell_\kappa/\delta r)^2$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Healing length ($\xi$), surface gravity ($\kappa$), and the analogue Page time ($t_{	ext{Page}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analogy is purely kinematic, meaning it does not involve true back-reaction or solving Einstein's equations; detection requires averaging over many runs.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the sonic horizon structure, entropy evolution crossing a Page time analogue, and plot the predicted depletion ratio $R(\delta r) \propto (\ell_\kappa/\delta r)^2$ against null results.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from the AMPS paradox to its acoustic analogy, deriving the necessary entanglement identities, calculating the difference in renormalized stress tensors between pre- and post-Page states, and finally presenting the measurable depletion signal.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The monogamy of quantum entanglement, applied by Almheiri-Marolf-Polchinski-Sully (AMPS) to black holes, obstructs a smooth horizon vacuum after the Page time. We transcribe this argument to Hawking-like phonon radiation from a sonic horizon in the Unruh acoustic metric. An exact purity identity shows that post-Page-time unitarity forces the entanglement between an outgoing phonon and its interior partner to vanish, selecting a non-Hadamard (Boulware-like) phonon state, which we define as an acoustic firewall. Its renormalized stress tensor differs from the smooth state by a constant, negative near-horizon flux, and the thermal-atmosphere energy density it removes, measured by a static calorimeter, grows as $(δr)^{-2}$ in the radial coordinate toward the horizon (singular in the free-fall frame), cut off at the healing length. The construction is kinematic and does not resolve the information paradox; it yields one concrete, falsifiable prediction: a differential phonon-calorimetry signal $\mathcal{R}(δr)=|Δ\mathcal{E}|/\mathcal{E}^{(0)}\to(\ell_κ/δr)^{2}$, present only after the analogue Page time in a Bose-Einstein condensate.

</details>

**Type:** theory · **Category:** statistical mechanics · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.13391v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

**Summary.** This paper develops the mathematical machinery to analyze when the conditional probability law of coupled stochastic processes factorizes given an intermediate 'boundary' path. It establishes that factorization is equivalent to specific conditions on likelihood ratios and mutual information, providing a unified language applicable across various physical models from general diffusions to quantum-inspired systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous mathematical framework connecting concepts like Markov boundaries, information theory (mutual information), and stochastic control energy in non-equilibrium settings, which is crucial for understanding open quantum systems dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central problem is determining the necessary and sufficient conditions for the conditional law of histories (paths) to factorize when conditioning a joint stochastic process on an intermediate 'boundary' path.

**Main result.** Factorization of the boundary-conditioned path law is equivalent to specific mathematical conditions, such as multiplicative separability of the Radon–Nikodym derivative or vanishing mixed covariance terms in specialized regimes.

**Method.** The analysis employs advanced measure theory, utilizing Girsanov's theorem for likelihood ratio calculations and information-theoretic tools like conditional mutual information and relative entropy.

**Model / system.** The study considers coupled stochastic systems decomposed into exterior ($Y$), boundary ($B$), and interior ($X$) variables, modeled by general continuous processes, including Itô diffusions.

**Key observables.** Path likelihoods (Radon–Nikodym derivatives), conditional mutual information $I(X; Y | B)$, and the quadratic control energy derived from log-likelihood expectations.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The time interval $[0, T]$, the structure of the coupling between variables ($Y, X$), and the nature of the conditioning path $B$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Assumes Polish state spaces to guarantee the existence of regular conditional probabilities; relies on domination by product reference laws for factorization theorems.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the core object (regular conditional probability on a path space) to establishing necessary and sufficient conditions for its factorization, providing equivalent criteria across different mathematical frameworks (e.g., general Borel laws vs. Itô diffusions).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We consider coupled stochastic systems decomposed into exterior, boundary, and interior variables, with the boundary variables sometimes carrying the directed structure of a sensor and actuator. The central question is when the conditional law of histories factorises, and how this path space statement is detected by log likelihoods, by Girsanov changes of measure, and by information theoretic quantities used in nonequilibrium statistical physics. The basic object is a regular conditional probability on a path space. Under domination by clamped reference laws, the boundary property becomes multiplicative separation of a Radon--Nikodym derivative, or equivalently additive separation of a path log likelihood. For Itō diffusions this log likelihood is computed by Girsanov's theorem; its expectation is the quadratic control energy appearing in the Föllmer entropy identity and in the stochastic control formulation of Schrödinger bridge problems. When exact factorisation fails, the remaining coupling is measured by conditional mutual information, namely the relative entropy between the true boundary-conditioned path law and the product of its conditional marginals. This gives a common language for boundary screening, path likelihood inference, controlled changes of path law, and the thermodynamic value of mutual information.

</details>

For small values of β, the connected correlations are close to zero, indicating that the sys- tem approaches a nearly separable product state consistent with the mean-field description. <figure class="paper-figure-card">
<img src="2607.13572_figures/2607.13572_fig2.jpg" alt="Figure 2">

The magnetization gener- ally decreases as β increases, reflecting the suppression of classical local spin order in the correlated quantum regime. The sharper drops observed for even N reflect the absence of geometrical frustration, while the odd rings retain a larger residual magnetization because a perfect alternating antiferromagnetic arrangement is incompatible with the periodic boundary condition.</figcaption>
</figure>
<figure class="paper-figure-card">
<img src="2607.13572_figures/2607.13572_fig3.jpg" alt="Figure 3">

The even rings show the largest entropy increase, consistent with the stronger suppression of their local magnetization.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Summary.** This work explores the transition between quantum and classical physics in spin chains using an interpolating Hamiltonian. By analyzing observables like magnetization and entropy, the authors quantify how genuine quantum correlations (captured by connected terms) stabilize the system compared to mean-field approximations. This provides a quantitative framework for understanding decoherence effects in condensed matter models.

**Why it may be interesting.** The use of a controlled interpolation parameter $\beta$ to bridge quantum dynamics (where correlations matter) and classical/mean-field descriptions is highly relevant for understanding open quantum systems where environmental coupling drives decoherence.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the quantum-classical crossover behavior in finite spin-1/2 rings subjected to Heisenberg exchange, Dzyaloshinsky-Moriya (DM) interaction, and an external magnetic field.

**Main result.** The parameter $\beta$ interpolates between a fully correlated quantum regime ($\beta=1$) and a product-state mean-field limit ($\beta=0$), showing that quantum correlations significantly modify observables like magnetization and entropy.

**Method.** The analysis employs an interpolating Hamiltonian $\hat{H}_\beta$ solved using the dissipative Gisin–Schrödinger dynamics, which is analyzed both in spectral decomposition (quantum) and via numerical methods (mean-field).

**Model / system.** The system is a finite ring of $N$ spin-$1/2$ particles. The Hamiltonian includes nearest-neighbor Heisenberg exchange ($J$), DM interaction ($\vec{D}$), and a Zeeman term, controlled by the interpolation parameter $\beta$.

**Key observables.** Local magnetization ($M_\beta$), connected spin correlations, single-site von Neumann entropy ($S_{\text{VN}}$), and the saturation field ($B_c$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The crossover parameter $\beta \in [0, 1]$, the ring size $N$, and the external magnetic field $B$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** For $\beta < 1$, the state-dependent Hamiltonian loses the simple spectral decomposition available in the purely quantum case ($\beta=1$).

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 shows connected correlations growing negatively with increasing $\beta$; Figure 2 demonstrates that magnetization decreases as $\beta$ increases; and Figure 3 illustrates entropy changes.

**Paper structure.** The paper first defines the interpolating Hamiltonian, then analyzes the dynamics in the purely quantum limit ($\beta=1$) using spectral methods. It contrasts this with the mean-field limit ($\beta=0$), before analyzing the crossover behavior across $\beta$ and calculating critical fields like $B_c$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study finite spin-$\tfrac12$ rings with nearest-neighbor Heisenberg exchange, Dzyaloshinsky--Moriya interaction, and an external magnetic field. We introduce an interpolation parameter between the fully quantum Hamiltonian and a state-dependent mean-field description. Using dissipative Gisin--Schrödinger dynamics, we analyze the resulting quantum--classical crossover through local magnetization, connected spin correlations, single-site entropy, and the saturation field of the fully polarized state.

</details>

**Summary.** The research reports on achieving high-precision quantum simulation of interacting magnon dynamics in a 2D XY magnet using superconducting qubits. By combining analog evolution with digital gate control, the authors measure key response functions to map out temperature-dependent spectra and scattering mechanisms. This work validates the use of hybrid quantum hardware for probing complex many-body physics relevant to materials science.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly bridges superconducting quantum hardware capabilities with fundamental condensed matter physics problems, providing a blueprint for simulating complex many-body dynamics beyond the reach of current theoretical numerical methods alone.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central challenge is combining high-fidelity quantum evolution with sophisticated control to accurately simulate complex, interacting dynamics in many-body quantum systems for materials discovery.

**Main result.** The work demonstrates high-precision simulation of interacting magnon dynamics, revealing strong mode dependence of decay rates and enabling the study of scattering mechanisms inaccessible in simpler regimes.

**Method.** The authors employ an interleaved analog-digital protocol on a superconducting processor to measure time-dependent correlation functions ($\chi_\mu(t)$), comparing these measurements against advanced numerical techniques like Matrix Product States (MPS) and BP-PEPS simulations.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is a 2D XY spin-1/2 magnet, realized on an analog-digital superconducting processor of up to 97 qubits. The physics involves quantized spin waves called magnons.

**Key observables.** Magnon spectra, temperature-dependent decay rates ($\Gamma$), linear and nonlinear response functions ($\chi_\mu(t)$), and mode dependence of scattering mechanisms (e.g., near van Hove singularities).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature ($T$), energy density ($\epsilon$), qubit count (up to 97 qubits), and simulation fidelity metrics.

**Assumptions / limitations.** MPS predictions become inaccurate away from low temperatures or small systems; the analysis relies on comparing experimental measurements to approximate numerical simulations like MPS/BP-PEPS.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate Hamiltonian learning circuits, measured time-dependent correlation functions ($\chi_\mu(t)$) matching theory, magnon dispersions showing mode dependence, and power-law fits for temperature dependence of decay rates.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the need for high-fidelity simulation to detailing the interleaved analog-digital measurement protocol. It then presents results on linear response (spectra/lifetimes), followed by nonlinear measurements (self-scattering), and finally discusses computational limitations using tensor network methods (BP-PEPS) regarding scaling complexity.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum simulation promises to advance materials discovery by accurately simulating complex states of matter, their microscopic excitations, and macroscopic response functions. The central challenge in resolving the underlying interacting dynamics is to combine high-fidelity evolution with the sophisticated control necessary to manipulate individual quasi-particles in quantum many-body states. Here, we report on high-precision simulation of both linear and non-linear response functions in a 2D XY spin-1/2 magnet using an analog-digital superconducting processor of up to 97 qubits. By interleaving digital gates with analog evolution precisely characterized via Hamiltonian learning, we selectively excite magnons at tunable energy densities. Measuring first the linear magnon response -- a central probe in neutron-scattering experiments -- we extract temperature-dependent spectra and lifetimes. Our results reveal stark variations in magnon decay rates across the Brillouin zone, with enhancement near van Hove singularities and suppression for edge-localized modes. Next, we perform a suite of nonlinear measurements, including the study of self-scattering mechanisms, as well as pump-probe spectroscopy to directly characterize the magnon interactions. While matrix-product state simulations capture the dynamics well in either small systems or at low temperatures, their predictions become inaccurate away from these limits. This work demonstrates precise simulation of the interacting dynamics in quantum magnets, and provides key insights into quasi-particles and their microscopic scattering mechanisms.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a method to rapidly drive open quantum systems toward non-equilibrium steady states (NESS) by engineering transient nonreciprocal dissipation. Using a two-mode bosonic model, the authors show that this engineered channel acts as a 'shortcut,' accelerating relaxation rates robustly and independently of the direction of nonreciprocity. This technique offers a powerful tool for fast state preparation in quantum information applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, controllable thermodynamic technique for rapid cooling and state preparation in continuous-variable systems, which is highly relevant to quantum optics and quantum information processing protocols.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge addressed is dynamically accelerating the convergence of open quantum systems toward a non-equilibrium steady state (NESS), moving beyond traditional studies focused only on thermal equilibrium.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that activating a transient, engineered nonreciprocal dissipative channel provides a robust shortcut to rapidly accelerate the system's approach to the target NESS.

**Method.** The analysis employs both mean-field dynamics (tracking expectation values) and rigorous quantum fluctuation analysis using the evolution of the covariance matrix within the phase-space representation.

**Model / system.** The model is a two-mode interacting bosonic system coupled to local thermal baths. The time evolution is governed by a Lindblad master equation, with nonreciprocity engineered via specific coupling terms in the dissipation channel.

**Key observables.** Relaxation speedup (measured via trace distance), mean field expectation values ($\langle a_1 angle, \langle a_2 angle$), and the evolution of the covariance matrix $\mathbf{V}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The nonreciprocity strength ($\Gamma$) controlled by phase conditions ($	heta$), system coupling ($\lambda$), and bath temperatures ($n_1, n_2$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Initial analysis uses mean-field theory, but the full treatment requires analyzing the second-order covariance matrix to capture quantum fluctuations accurately.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate that activating the nonreciprocal coupling transiently expedites convergence to NESS (Fig. 1a), and plots show the rapid decay of state norms/trace distance during pulsed regions compared to natural decay rates.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the challenge of accelerating NESS convergence, developing the theoretical framework using master equations and phase-space methods, demonstrating the acceleration via nonreciprocal coupling, and concluding with implications for quantum state preparation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Driven by recent discoveries regarding the quantum Mpemba effect, the anomalous relaxation dynamics of open quantum systems have garnered significant attention. While expediting thermalization to equilibrium has been extensively studied, dynamically accelerating the convergence toward nonequilibrium steady states remains a formidable challenge. In this article, we find a transient engineered nonreciprocal dissipative channel can provide a shortcut that accelerates convergence to the target reciprocal nonequilibrium steady state for the considered two-mode model and initial states. Using interacting bosonic modes, we demonstrate that the temporal activation of a nonreciprocal channel efficiently suppresses prolonged inter-mode energy oscillations, enforcing a rapid, unidirectional thermal dump into the environment. Counterintuitively, we find that this relaxation speedup is robust and independent of the direction of the nonreciprocity. Our results provide a powerful thermodynamic technique for rapid state preparation and cooling in continuous-variable quantum systems, particularly critical for low-temperature quantum information processing.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates the fate of excitations following a quench across a quantum critical point using a large-scale quantum annealer. It distinguishes between robust defect creation governed by the Kibble-Zurek mechanism and subsequent post-critical dynamics. The key finding is that while defect counting works well, magnetization reveals that disorder localizes the domain structure, suggesting these two physical processes are distinct.

**Why it may be interesting.** The work provides a concrete experimental platform (quantum annealers) to probe complex non-equilibrium dynamics beyond simple critical exponents, linking quantum annealing hardware capabilities directly to fundamental concepts in condensed matter physics like defect formation and relaxation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to separate the physics of defect creation near a quantum critical point (Kibble-Zurek mechanism) from the subsequent, nonintegrable dynamics of confined excitations.

**Main result.** While defect density follows expected Kibble-Zurek scaling, post-critical observables like magnetization and domain statistics reveal that this evolution is interrupted by localization effects, particularly when disorder is present.

**Method.** The research combines quantum simulation on large-scale annealers with theoretical analysis using Matrix Product States (MPS) simulations to compare defect counting versus full dynamical evolution.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is a longitudinally biased one-dimensional quantum Ising chain, implemented physically on a 5,564-qubit quantum annealer. The dynamics are driven across a quantum critical point via controlled annealing schedules.

**Key observables.** Kink density ($\langle n_{ex} angle$), longitudinal magnetization ($\langle\sigma_zangle$), and minority-domain statistics (e.g., average domain size $\langle L angle$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The ratio $A = \hat{\xi}/\xi_{\parallel}$ controls the crossover between the Kibble-Zurek and Landau-Zener regimes; disorder strength is also a critical parameter.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that dynamics can be separated into near-critical defect creation followed by post-critical mesonic evolution, which may be interrupted by static disorder.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the data collapse of kink density and magnetization onto scaling functions of $A$, comparing quantum simulation results against both uniform and disordered MPS simulations to highlight localization effects.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the framework using quantum annealers for non-equilibrium dynamics. It then analyzes defect creation via KZM/LZ crossover, contrasting this with post-critical observables like magnetization. Finally, it uses theoretical modeling (mesonic states) and disorder simulations to show that localization governs the late-time domain structure.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum phase transitions provide a controlled route for generating many-body excitations, but the dynamics after the critical point can be as important as the initial defect creation. Recent progress in quantum annealing has made it possible to access coherent nonequilibrium dynamics in programmable Ising systems with thousands of superconducting qubits. Here we use this capability to study a longitudinally biased quantum Ising chain, where Kibble--Zurek defect creation is followed by nonintegrable post-critical dynamics. The longitudinal bias confines kink--antikink excitations into mesonic bound states, so that the final spin configurations encode both the production of defects near the critical point and the subsequent evolution of the confined excitations. Using energy-scale rescaling and zero-noise extrapolation, we find that the defect density follows the expected biased Kibble--Zurek/Landau--Zener crossover and agrees with matrix-product-state simulations with uniform bias. In contrast, magnetization, spatial profiles, and minority-domain statistics reveal that mesonic evolution is interrupted by localization of the post-critical domain pattern. Matrix-product-state simulations with disorder reproduce this separation between robust defect creation and localized post-critical dynamics. Our results show that large-scale quantum annealers can probe the fate of critical excitations beyond defect counting.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces COMPASS, an adaptive biorthogonal neural network framework designed for simulating Non-Hermitian many-body systems. It stabilizes ground state finding by combining energy and variance minimization into a progressive optimization scheme. This allows researchers to study exotic phenomena like spectral instability shielding via frustration and novel topological structures in NH quantum magnets.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, generalizable numerical tool (COMPASS) that extends quantum simulation techniques beyond the Hermitian domain, allowing direct investigation of complex physics relevant to open or dissipative quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing a robust computational framework to study Non-Hermitian (NH) many-body systems, specifically bridging concepts of frustration and non-Hermiticity.

**Main result.** The proposed COMPASS framework successfully simulates NH systems directly, revealing that gap frustration shields against spectral instability and generating novel topological features like the diabolic ring.

**Method.** It employs a Complementary Optimization Method for Progressive Adaptive State Search (COMPASS) using adaptive biorthogonal Neural Quantum States (NQSs). This method alternates between energy minimization and variance minimization to ensure stable convergence to ground-state eigenpairs.

**Model / system.** The framework is applied to 1D and 2D NH many-body systems, including frustrated magnetism models based on extensions of the J1-J2 spin chain. The formalism requires a biorthogonal approach using left ($\Psi_L$) and right ($\Psi_R$) eigenstates.

**Key observables.** Ground-state eigenpairs ($E$), energy expectation value $\langle H angle$, energy variance ($\sigma^2$), and the fidelity of the biorthogonal overlap $|\langle\Psi_{L0}|\Psi_{R0}angle|$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Frustration coupling strength, PT-symmetry breaking parameters (e.g., $\gamma/J_1$), system size ($N=1D$ up to 200, $N=2D$ up to 100).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework assumes that physically-informed ansatz selection is crucial for reliable NH simulations; it avoids Hermitian embeddings or adiabatic continuation.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the COMPASS optimization process (adaptive growth, loss functions), PT-symmetry breaking in 1D Ising chains, and the emergence of Exceptional Points (EPs) when simulating frustrated NH models.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the COMPASS method using adaptive biorthogonal NQSs. It details the two-phase optimization process ($\mathcal{L}_e$ then $\mathcal{L}_v$) for finding ground states in NH systems. Finally, it applies this to frustrated magnetism to study spectral topology and EP emergence.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this work, we introduce a complementary optimization method for progressive and adaptive state search (COMPASS) based on biorthogonal adaptive recurrent neural quantum states. Our approach combines an adaptive autoregressive architecture with a biorthogonal variational Monte Carlo scheme as well as a complementary optimization scheme that alternates between energy and variance minimization. This enables the stable convergence to ground-state eigenpairs, while avoiding Markov chain sampling through exact autoregressive generation. We demonstrate that for parity-time(PT)-symmetric Hamiltonians, unconstrained complex ansatze can spontaneously break PT symmetry during optimization, even in the unbroken phase, leading to spurious imaginary energies. Real-valued ansatze, on the other hand, naturally constrain the optimization to the correct physical manifold. Conversely, for generic non-Hermitian (NH) Hamiltonians without symmetry protection and complex spectra, complex ansatze are essential for capturing complex ground-state properties. Our results establish that physically-informed ansatz selection is crucial for reliable NH simulations. By combining adaptive architectures, biorthogonal optimization, and symmetry-aware modeling, this framework enables a direct study of 1D and 2D NH many-body systems without Hermitian embeddings or adiabatic continuation. Applying this framework to systems with frustrated magnetism, we show that gap frustration provides a quantitative shield against NH spectral instability, with the frustration gap setting a critical threshold for PT-symmetry breaking. Also, complexifying the frustration coupling itself generates a new topologically nontrivial network of diabolic level crossings, controlled by the phase of the complex coupling, that has no Hermitian analog. We term this novel spectral topology in NH frustrated systems the diabolic ring.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper numerically examines how a Fermi--Pasta--Ulam lattice cools when coupled to an external bath whose temperature is lowered at a specific rate. It finds that the cooling process deviates significantly from equilibrium below a stochastic threshold, leading to a measurable residual zero-point energy ($E_0$). This residual energy exhibits a predictable power-law scaling with both system size and the applied cooling rate.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work explores non-equilibrium dynamics in classical many-body systems, which shares conceptual links with open quantum system thermodynamics and relaxation processes studied in AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the non-equilibrium cooling process and associated glassy behavior of a Fermi--Pasta--Ulam (FPU) system when coupled to an external bath whose temperature is reduced at a controlled rate.

**Main result.** The residual zero-point energy ($E_0$) maintained by the FPU system below equilibrium scales with both system size ($N$) and cooling rate ($\xi$) according to $E_{0} \sim (\xi N)^{2/3}$.

**Method.** Numerical simulations using the Verlet method are employed to track the time evolution of energy components during the controlled cooling process.

**Model / system.** The system is an FPU lattice interacting via a Lennard-Jones potential, coupled to a gas bath. The total Hamiltonian includes both the FPU dynamics and the coupling interaction potential.

**Key observables.** Zero-point energy ($E_0$), specific energy ($u = E/N$), and the deviation of measured energy from expected equilibrium values.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Cooling rate ($\xi$), system size ($N$), and the stochastic threshold below which non-equilibrium effects dominate.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on simulating the cooling process by scaling gas particle velocities at discrete steps, assuming a weak stochastic threshold dictates the onset of glassy behavior.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the time evolution of kinetic energy during cooling and show how specific energy deviates from linear expectations as temperature drops, confirming power-law dependencies for $E_0$ on $N$ and $\xi$.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the setup of the FPU system coupled to a bath, describes the numerical simulation procedure involving controlled cooling rates ($\xi$), presents diagnostics like zero-point energy ($E_0$), and concludes with the derived power-law scaling relationship.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this work, we numerically studied the cooling process of a Fermi--Pasta--Ulam system, which occurs when the FPU system is placed in contact with a gas whose temperature $T$ is reduced with a certain cooling rate $ξ$. It was found that the existence of a weak stochastic threshold has a significant impact on the cooling process, because below such a stochastic threshold the specific FPU energy is larger than its temperature, i.e., the FPU system falls out of equilibrium. The difference remains finite in the limit $T \to 0$, so that the FPU system maintains a residual amount of energy $E_0$ at vanishing temperature. Our numerical simulations reveal that this energy exhibits a power--law dependence on both the system size and the cooling rate, scaling approximately as $E_{0} \sim (ξN)^{2/3}$.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents an efficient theoretical framework to calculate the reduced density matrix for open quantum systems, applicable to both bosons and fermions. By modeling the environment coupling carefully, the authors derive exact solutions that rigorously satisfy the Lindblad master equation. This offers a straightforward tool for simulating complex dissipative dynamics in many-body physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This is highly relevant as it provides a powerful, non-standard computational tool for simulating complex open quantum dynamics (quantum transport/control) without resorting to full Liouvillian space diagonalization.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is developing a computationally efficient method to derive the reduced density matrix for open quantum many-body systems undergoing Markovian evolution.

**Main result.** The authors introduce a framework that yields exact solutions satisfying the Lindblad master equation for both bosonic and fermionic cases, bypassing costly Liouvillian diagonalization.

**Method.** The method models the environment as a copy of the primary system with a monotonically decaying coupling to enforce unidirectional energy flow, allowing direct calculation of the reduced density matrix via tracing out bath degrees of freedom.

**Model / system.** The study addresses open quantum systems (bosonic and fermionic) interacting with an environment. The dynamics are governed by the Lindblad master equation, which is satisfied exactly by the proposed framework.

**Key observables.** Reduced density matrix components ($\hat{ho}_S(t)$), dissipative dynamics.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dissipation parameters ($\gamma$), bath temperature/occupancy ($ar{n}$), coupling strengths ($g_{ij}(t)$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The Markovian approximation is assumed, and the method relies on engineering system-bath coupling to ensure unidirectional energy flow.

**Figures summary.** Not specified in the provided notes; the structure is based on mathematical derivations involving equations (Eq. 1) through (Eq. 40).

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the general Hamiltonian and master equation for open systems, deriving explicit formulas for bosonic and fermionic cases using specific coupling schemes, and culminating in a generalized, efficient method that satisfies the Lindblad form.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Markovian dynamics of open quantum many-body systems are typically governed by the Lindblad master equation, yet obtaining the reduced density matrix for bosonic and fermionic systems remains a formidable numerical challenge due to the exponential growth of the Hilbert space. Here, we introduce a computationally efficient framework for constructing the reduced density matrix by modeling the environment as a copy of the primary system with a monotonically decaying coupling that enforces unidirectional energy flow. Our method directly yields exact solutions that rigorously satisfy the Lindblad master equation for both bosonic and fermionic cases, bypassing the need for costly Liouvillian diagonalization. We validate our approach through applications to paradigmatic models, demonstrating accurate reproduction of dissipative dynamics across a broad parameter regime. This work provides a straightforward and powerful tool for simulating Markovian open-system evolution, with immediate applicability to quantum transport and control problems in many-body physics.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper proposes that multi-mode Schrödinger Cat States can serve as a robust platform for universal quantum computation. By engineering non-local dissipation and utilizing the Zeno limit approximation, the authors derive protocols to achieve arbitrary single-qubit rotations and an entangling two-qubit gate. This establishes SCS states as a viable architecture for future bosonic quantum processors.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses quantum computation using bosonic systems, providing concrete protocols for implementing fundamental gates using engineered dissipation, which is highly relevant to open quantum system research in AMO/Quantum Optics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is establishing a framework for universal quantum computation using multi-mode Schrödinger Cat States (SCS) stabilized by engineered non-local dissipation.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that arbitrary single-qubit control and an entangling $XX(\pi/2)$ gate can be implemented, thereby realizing a universal gate set for bosonic quantum computation.

**Method.** The analysis relies on deriving effective low-dimensional dynamics by projecting the full system evolution onto the dark mode subspace using the Zeno limit approximation.

**Model / system.** The physical platform is modeled as a 1D chain of Kerr anharmonic oscillators coupled via engineered non-local dissipation, whose dynamics are governed by a Lindblad master equation in Fourier space.

**Key observables.** Gate fidelity dynamics, entanglement generation (e.g., logarithmic negativity), and the evolution of the density matrix $ho_{\phi\phi}(t)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dissipation rates ($\gamma$), Kerr non-linearity strength ($U$), coupling strengths ($\lambda$), and the effective Hamiltonian parameters derived from these constants.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation heavily relies on the strong dissipation regime (Zeno limit) to define a stable, low-dimensional logical subspace.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the physical setup involving Kerr oscillators driven by pumping and coupled through lossy reservoirs. Subsequent figures plot fidelity dynamics for single and two-qubit operations over time.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the basic concept of SCS stabilization to deriving the effective Hamiltonian, demonstrating individual qubit control (X and Z rotations), and finally coupling two such arrays to achieve a universal entangling gate set.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Schrödinger cat states provide a hardware-efficient platform for bosonic quantum error correction by encoding logical information in protected manifolds of harmonic oscillators. While previous work has demonstrated the dissipative stabilization of multi-mode Schrödinger cat states as robust quantum memories, a framework for universal quantum computation has remained unavailable. Here we extend this approach by introducing a universal gate set for dissipatively stabilized multi-mode cat qubits. Using a chain of Kerr non-linear oscillators coupled through engineered non-local dissipation and an effective low-dimensional description, we show how arbitrary single-qubit control can be achieved through arbitrary rotation around the $X$-axis and $π/2$-rotation around the $Z$-axis. We further show how coupling two such stabilized arrays through just one oscillator on each respective array enables coherent entangling operations through implementation of the $XX(π/2)$ gate. Numerical simulations demonstrate high-fidelity gate dynamics and entanglement generation under realistic parameters. Finally, we analyze the effects of induced and intrinsic photon loss, disorder, and the validity regime of the effective low-dimensional theory. Our results establish dissipatively stabilized multi-mode Schrödinger cat states as a potential architecture for universal bosonic quantum computation.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a Lie algebraic framework to analyze and simulate non-Markovian quantum dynamics. By mapping the density matrix evolution into a high-dimensional linear time-varying system, it applies the Magnus expansion technique. The key contribution is providing detailed error analysis showing how truncation errors depend on non-Markovian effects and the choice of stochastic calculus (Itô vs. Stratonovich) during measurement.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides advanced mathematical tools (Lie algebra, Magnus expansion) to tackle the notoriously difficult problem of non-Markovian dynamics in quantum computing contexts, offering rigorous error analysis that is crucial for developing reliable quantum simulators.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the challenge of accurately simulating non-Markovian quantum dynamics, particularly when measurement noise is involved.

**Main result.** It demonstrates that the Magnus expansion can efficiently simulate these complex dynamics, and it rigorously quantifies how truncation errors depend on the underlying Lie algebraic structure and whether the stochastic process is modeled using Itô or Stratonovich calculus.

**Method.** The core methodology involves vectorizing the density matrix to transform the evolution into a high-dimensional linear time-varying equation in Liouville space, which is then solved using the Magnus expansion technique derived from Lie algebra theory.

**Model / system.** The model describes an open quantum system interacting with an environment. The dynamics are governed by equations that transition from standard Lindblad forms to complex stochastic differential equations (SDEs) when measurement/filtering is included.

**Key observables.** Truncation errors of the first- and second-order Magnus expansions, and the comparison between Itô and Stratonovich noise models in quantum filtering.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The step size ($h$) for numerical integration, and the time scale relative to non-Markovian interaction timescales.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on approximating higher-order terms of the Magnus expansion using Taylor series expansions around a midpoint, and assumes bounded time-varying amplitudes in the Liouville equation representation.

**Figures summary.** Numerical simulations compare ideal evolution trajectories against results from first-order Runge-Kutta (RK) methods and second-order Magnus expansions, showing good agreement only for very short simulation times.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds theoretically by representing non-Markovian dynamics as linear time-varying equations in the Liouville space using a Lie algebraic basis. It then applies and analyzes the convergence properties of the Magnus expansion for both deterministic and stochastic (Itô/Stratonovich) regimes, concluding with numerical validation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this paper, we study the non-Markovian quantum dynamics in quantum computations from the perspective of a Lie algebraic approach based on numerical analysis. By vectorizing the density matrix of quantum states, the non-Markovian evolutions can be represented with high-dimensional linear time-varying equations, where the time-varying parameters arise from the non-Markovian interactions between the quantum system and environment. We study the Magnus expansion of such linear time-varying quantum dynamics and clarify how the truncation errors for the first- and second-order Magnus expansions are influenced by the non-Markovian properties. Besides, when the quantum states are measured for filtering, the dynamics can be modeled as time-varying stochastic differential equations due to the existence of measurement noise. The Magnus expansions based on quantum stochastic filtering are different when the quantum measurement noises are modeled in an {Itô} or Stratonovich approach, rendering different truncation errors. Based on this, numerical simulations further demonstrate the efficiency of Magnus expansions in simulating non-Markovian quantum dynamics without or with stochasticity, and how the truncation errors are influenced by the Lie algebras in the Liouville space.

</details>

**Authors:** Sandeep Jangid, Soumyabrata Saha, Kapil Sharma, Jitendra Kethepalli, Benjamin Guiselin, Jacopo De Nardis, Tridib Sadhu

**Summary.** This paper provides an exact solution to the macroscopic fluctuation theory governing single-file diffusion using the Brownian Hard Rod model. The authors achieve this by applying a canonical transformation that maps the complex interacting system onto a solvable point-particle description. This yields explicit, universal large-deviation statistics for observables like tracer position and integrated current in both annealed and quenched environments.

**Why it may be interesting.** The techniques used—mapping complex interacting systems (like BHR) onto exactly solvable point-particle models via canonical transformations—are highly relevant for understanding non-equilibrium dynamics in quantum many-body systems where exact solutions are rare.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to develop an exactly solvable macroscopic fluctuation theory (MFT) for single-file diffusion, a process common in low-dimensional systems.

**Main result.** They show that the MFT for the extended Brownian Hard Rod gas is exactly solvable via a canonical transformation, yielding explicit large-deviation statistics for key observables.

**Method.** The core method involves mapping the fluctuating hydrodynamic equation governing the density field onto an exactly solvable point-particle system using a canonical transformation. Large deviation functions and scaled cumulant generating functions are calculated in both annealed and quenched ensembles.

**Model / system.** The primary model is single-file diffusion, modeled by a one-dimensional gas of extended Brownian Hard Rods (BHR). The theory is generalized to lattice gases with finite-volume exclusion.

**Key observables.** Tracer-position ($z_t$), integrated-current ($Q_t$), and the local-height observable $h(z)_t$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Hard rod length ($a$), bare diffusion coefficient ($D_0=1$), time scaling ($\sqrt{t}$), and ensemble type (annealed vs. quenched).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the large-time asymptotic behavior of fluctuations, characterized by saddle-point approximations in the path integral formalism.

**Figures summary.** Figures schematically illustrate BHR trajectories, show LDFs for tracer-position and integrated-current comparing theory to simulation, and display optimal density profile evolution associated with specific SCGF variables.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from establishing the continuum model (BHR) and its hydrodynamic description. It then introduces the canonical transformation that renders the MFT solvable. Finally, it derives explicit formulas for the SCGFs of tracer-position and integrated-current, comparing results across different ensembles and showing connections to local-height statistics.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Single-file diffusion is a ubiquitous phenomenon in low-dimensional systems, arising in transport inside narrow channels. Its natural continuum model is a one-dimensional gas of extended Brownian hard rods (BHR). Perhaps owing to the perceived intractability of this problem, much of the literature has traditionally focused on lattice exclusion models, where integrability methods have yielded remarkable, albeit limited, exact results. A major recent advance comes from a formal solution of macroscopic fluctuation theory (MFT) for the exclusion process. Yet, despite the formal solution, only a handful of properties have been made explicit. We show that the corresponding MFT of the extended BHR gas is in fact exactly solvable through a canonical transformation. We demonstrate this by explicit computation of the large-deviation statistics of the tracer-position and integrated-current in both annealed and quenched ensembles. We further show that an analogous canonical transformation applies to the MFT of lattice gases with finite-volume exclusion, yielding corresponding tracer and current statistics. We validate our results using rare-event simulations for both the continuum and the lattice models.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a rigorous framework to calculate temporal entanglement entropy in 2D CFTs by analytically continuing replica twist correlators from Euclidean to Lorentzian time. Holographically, this translates into finding complex geodesics in AdS space that match the field theory results across various physical quenches and states.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful extension of entanglement concepts into time-dependent regimes (Lorentzian signature), which is crucial for understanding non-equilibrium dynamics in strongly coupled quantum systems relevant to AMO physics and open quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is formulating and calculating timelike entanglement entropy (TEE) and its Rényi extension in 2D Conformal Field Theory (CFT) and establishing its holographic dual description.

**Main result.** The authors provide a systematic field-theoretic construction using analytic continuation of replica twist correlators, which yields explicit agreement with complex geodesic calculations in holography for various physical scenarios.

**Method.** The methodology involves analytically continuing Euclidean replica twist correlators to time-ordered, timelike-separated insertions in CFT. Holographically, this translates to identifying and selecting specific complex geodesics in the bulk AdS geometry that minimize the real part of their length.

**Model / system.** The work applies to 2D CFTs considering temporal subregions of arbitrary extent, with holographic duals modeled by Anti-de Sitter (AdS) spacetimes, including specific backgrounds like AdS-Vaidya geometry. The analysis covers quantum operator quenches and excited states.

**Key observables.** Timelike Entanglement Entropy ($S(T)$), Rényi entanglement entropy, complex-valued entropies (pseudoentropies), and the imaginary part of the entropy.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Rényi index $n$, time separation $\Delta t$, and parameters defining the specific AdS background geometry.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The approach relies on analytic continuation from Euclidean to Lorentzian signature and utilizes a geodesic approximation in the bulk spacetime, which is necessary because no real geodesic connects timelike-separated points on the boundary.

**Figures summary.** Figure 11 (referenced in notes) compares numerical solutions for correlators against analytical forms derived from AdS and BTZ geometries, demonstrating consistency under specific limiting procedures.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically develops the field-theoretic definition of TEE via replica twist correlators, extends this to Rényi indices $n>1$, derives the corresponding complex extremal surface prescription in holography, and validates these results across multiple physical examples like quenches and excited states.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We formulate timelike entanglement entropy and its Rényi extension in two-dimensional conformal field theory through the analytic continuation of replica twist correlators to time-ordered, timelike-separated insertions. This field-theoretic construction grounds and generalizes recent developments, and applies to temporal subregions of arbitrary extent. Within three-dimensional holography, the semiclassical boundary correlator identifies boundary-anchored complex geodesics as the relevant bulk saddles and selects the one with the smallest real part of the length. This provides a direct boundary derivation of the proposed complex extremal surface prescription and extends to Rényi index $n>1$, for which we explicitly construct the corresponding complex cosmic brane geometry in the vacuum. We develop these ideas in several representative settings, including locally and globally excited states and quantum operator quenches, making manifest the precise agreement between boundary twist correlator and bulk complex geodesic calculations. For AdS-Vaidya, our approach predicts a different result from earlier piecewise geodesic constructions, while reproducing the field theory answer. Across these examples, the operator ordering uniquely determines the imaginary part of the complex-valued entropy, which is quantized in units of $cπ/6$ and sensitive to the effective causal structure but not to the underlying dynamics.

</details>

**Summary.** The research experimentally benchmarks trigonometric continuous-variable quantum gates using trapped ions. By characterizing these elementary cosine gates at the level of individual operations, the authors demonstrate their utility as fundamental building blocks. This advances the feasibility of implementing complex bosonic simulations and hybrid quantum algorithms on current hardware platforms.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly bridges advanced quantum information theory (CV gate sets) with state-of-the-art trapped-ion hardware, providing crucial empirical data on non-polynomial gates essential for simulating complex many-body physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to experimentally benchmark trigonometric continuous-variable (CV) gate primitives, which are necessary for simulating complex bosonic dynamics.

**Main result.** Trigonometric CV gates are established as reusable building blocks for hybrid quantum algorithms and bosonic Hamiltonian simulations requiring non-polynomial operations.

**Method.** The authors performed gate-level characterization by measuring Fock-space transition probabilities on the QSCOUT trapped-ion platform, comparing finite-step circuits against ideal evolution.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a trapped-ion chain ($	ext{}^{171}	ext{Yb}^+$) utilizing collective motional modes (qumodes) alongside hyperfine states acting as qubits. The gates are implemented via hybrid qubit-qumode operations.

**Key observables.** Fock-space transition probabilities, Wigner function, Negativity, and Characteristic functions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Gate parameters ($c$), Trotter step number, thermal initialization effects, and motional dephasing rates.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The study compares finite-step circuit approximations against ideal trigonometric evolution while accounting for experimental imperfections like thermal noise and dephasing.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate measured detection probabilities for radial motional modes in three- and four-ion chains, relating to the implementation of collective motional gates.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from discussing CV-DV universality (Section II) to introducing the trapped-ion platform (Section III), analyzing ideal one- and two-qumode cosine gates using phase-space diagnostics (Section IV), detailing finite-step circuit realization (Section V), presenting experimental measurements (Section VI), and concluding (Section VII).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Hybrid continuous-discrete-variable quantum processors can represent bosonic degrees of freedom directly in oscillator modes, or qumodes, while using qubits for control, readout, and nonlinear operations. Recently proposed trigonometric continuous-variable (CV) gate sets promote periodic functions of oscillator quadratures to elementary operations, making them natural primitives for compact variables, rotor models, lattice gauge theories, and anharmonic dynamics. Here we experimentally demonstrate and benchmark one-qumode cosine gates, and perform a mode-resolved marginal benchmark of two-qumode cosine-gate implementations, on the QSCOUT trapped-ion quantum platform. Our implementation uses collective motional modes of three- and four-ion $^{171}{\rm Yb}^{+}$ chains and realizes finite-step trigonometric-gate circuits through hybrid qubit-qumode operations and conditional phase-space displacements. In contrast to previous theoretical and compilation work, we focus on the gate-level characterization of the trigonometric primitives. We measure Fock-space transition probabilities, study their dependence on gate parameters and Trotter step number, and compare with simulations incorporating thermal initialization and motional dephasing. We also derive ideal gate matrix elements and phase-space diagnostics, connecting the measurements to the non-Gaussian structure generated by these gates. These results establish trigonometric CV gates as reusable building blocks for bosonic Hamiltonian simulations and hybrid quantum algorithms requiring intrinsically non-polynomial operations.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper addresses the experimental difficulty of implementing transverse interaction catalysts in quantum annealing. It proposes and rigorously analyzes self-consistent field protocols that emulate these effects using only measurements in the transverse basis. The results confirm that these approximations are highly accurate, making the technique a viable alternative for current quantum annealers.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, implementable theoretical framework for simulating complex quantum interactions (like those found in spin glasses) on near-term hardware by mapping difficult terms onto controllable single-parameter controls.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is emulating the effects of fully-connected transverse interactions, which act as catalysts in quantum annealing but are experimentally difficult to implement directly.

**Main result.** A self-consistent transverse field protocol can successfully emulate these catalyst dynamics, establishing it as a viable alternative for near-term quantum annealers by quantifying and minimizing approximation errors.

**Method.** The authors introduce several self-consistent protocols (SCE, SCD, SCM) that replace the direct interaction term with an iteratively determined transverse field $\Gamma(t)$, analyzed via large-$N$ limit calculations and numerical simulations.

**Model / system.** The work focuses on quantum annealing applied to spin glass models, specifically using the $p$-spin model Hamiltonian. The dynamics are governed by time-dependent Hamiltonians involving both $\sigma_z$ (problem) and $\sigma_x$ (transverse field/catalyst) terms.

**Key observables.** Average z-magnetization ($m_z(t)$), average x-magnetization ($\langle\hat{\sigma}_x^iangle$), and the error metric ($\Delta z$) comparing different simulation protocols.

**Important parameters / regimes.** System size ($N$), annealing parameters ($s, \lambda$), waiting time interval ($w$), number of measurements ($k$), and total evolution time ($T$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The core assumption is that the dynamics can be accurately captured by replacing the interaction term with a self-consistently determined transverse field. The protocol's performance degrades at large $T$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate error comparisons ($\Delta z$) between Exact Diagonalization (ED) and various self-consistent protocols (SCE, SCD, SCM) across different parameter regimes, showing how errors scale with system size or approximation parameters.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the problem of emulating transverse catalysts. It then develops and compares multiple self-consistent protocols (SCE, SCD, SCM), rigorously proving their equivalence to the original dynamics in the large-$N$ limit for SCE. Finally, it analyzes the error scaling of these approximations across different physical regimes, including phase transitions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Fully-connected transverse interactions have been considered as catalysts for quantum annealing that could mitigate exponentially small gaps and circumvent first-order phase transitions, but their experimental implementation remains challenging. In this work, we introduce a procedure for emulating their effects via a self-consistent transverse field. All that is required beyond conventional transverse-field annealing is the ability to make measurements in the transverse ($\hatσ^x$) basis. We show that this protocol yields identical dynamics in the large-system limit, and study the approach to that limit in numerical simulations of the (uniform) $p$-spin model. However, realizing the protocol in practice requires us to consider a series of approximate variants, each of whose errors we quantify and demonstrate can be made sufficiently small. Lastly, we show how to map the protocol onto annealing platforms that vary only a single control parameter. Even after the multiple stages of approximation, our procedure can generate dynamics that agree well with the original transverse-interaction catalyst, establishing self-consistent transverse fields as a viable alternative on near-term quantum annealers.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a phase-independent generalization of 'magic' using stabilizer Rényi entropy to quantify non-stabilizerness. It applies this tool to tree-level gluon scattering, demonstrating that the resulting measure is robust against local basis choices and yields specific quantitative predictions for different scattering channels.

**Why it may be interesting.** Although focused on high-energy physics (gluon scattering), the core mathematical technique—defining a robust, physically meaningful resource measure invariant under local gauge/phase transformations—is highly relevant for analyzing entanglement and complexity in open quantum systems or condensed matter models where such ambiguities arise.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to generalize the concept of 'magic' (non-stabilizerness) by formulating a measure that is invariant under local phase ambiguities in the computational basis.

**Main result.** They find specific quantitative results for gluon scattering, showing that the phase-independent magic can be larger than limits set by simpler processes and that color dependence cancels out entirely.

**Method.** The authors define and calculate a generalized, phase-independent stabilizer Rényi entropy ($\langle M_q angle$) by averaging the standard measure over the local basis-phase $U(1)^k$ orbit.

**Model / system.** The primary physical testbed is tree-level gluon scattering amplitudes, where outgoing helicities are interpreted as qubits forming a multi-qubit Hilbert space. The analysis compares results for $2	o 2$, $3	o 2$, and $2	o 3$ processes.

**Key observables.** Phase-independent stabilizer Rényi entropy ($\langle M_q angle$), Magic (non-stabilizerness), Gluon scattering amplitudes.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Scattering process multiplicities ($2	o 2$, $3	o 2$, $2	o 3$); the index $q$ of the Rényi entropy; kinematic configurations (e.g., symmetry of outgoing momenta).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to tree-level amplitudes, and a key step involves approximating the phase average over the basis-phase orbit.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 plots $M_2$ vs. $r$ for two-qubit states; Figure 2 shows enhancement of $M_2$ with an admixture coefficient $c$; Figure 3 compares the standard $M_2$ to the phase-averaged $\langle M_2 angle$, confirming it is a lower bound.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the standard SRE, then identifies its dependence on local basis phases, and finally applies the corrected, phase-independent measure to analyze specific gluon scattering amplitudes in different processes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Magic, also known as non-stabilizerness, measures the usefulness of a quantum state for quantum computation. While magic is defined relative to a choice of computational basis, in some physical settings the available data determine this basis only up to local phase conventions. In this paper, we generalize the notion of magic and formulate it in a phase-independent manner, and hence define a generalized stabilizer Rényi entropy. As a case study, we consider higher-multiplicity tree-level gluon scattering, interpreting the outgoing helicities as qubits. In this setting, the helicity data naturally determine a local basis for each qubit but leave a phase ambiguity. For $3\to 2$ scattering, we find that the final-state phase-independent magic is generically larger than the maximum attainable in $2\to 2$ scattering. For $2 \to 3$ scattering, we find a nonzero minimal value approached in the soft limit. Moreover, when the three outgoing momenta become symmetric, the magic approaches a local minimum only a few percent above the soft-limit value. In all cases considered, the color dependence cancels from the phase-independent stabilizer Rényi entropy.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates superconductivity induced in a charge-transfer insulator ($	ext{T-NbSe}_2$) placed near a superconductor ($	ext{H-NbSe}_2$). By creating tunable junctions using STM, the researchers demonstrated that strong coupling leads to robust superconducting proximity effects characterized by Yu-Shiba-Rusinov-like bound states. This establishes a model platform for studying correlated superconductivity.

**Why it may be interesting.** The observation of YSR states in a proximity setup involving correlated insulators provides an experimental realization for studying superconducting coupling mediated by localized magnetic moments, which is highly relevant to open quantum systems modeling.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to understand the microscopic interplay between superconductivity and strongly correlated insulating states using proximity effects.

**Main result.** In a strong-coupling regime, the junction exhibits superconducting gaps comparable to the source material and displays pronounced particle-hole symmetric in-gap bound states consistent with YSR excitations.

**Method.** Ultralow-temperature scanning tunnelling microscopy (STM) was used to construct atomically sharp lateral junctions between two phases of NbSe2 and perform site-dependent spectroscopy.

**Model / system.** The system utilizes monolayer H-NbSe2 (a CDW metal with Ising superconductivity) and T-NbSe2 (a strongly correlated charge-transfer insulator). Junctions are formed either by weak structural displacement or strong tip-pulse induced phase transition.

**Key observables.** Superconducting gap ($\Delta$), in-gap resonance peaks (YSR-like excitations), and Kondo resonances observed via STS.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Low temperatures (e.g., 350 mK, 4.2 K); comparison between weak vs. strong coupling regimes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The YSR state formation requires the confluence of a finite density of states at $E_F$, local magnetic moments, and strong exchange coupling in the interfacial region.

**Figures summary.** Figures detail structural characterization (CDW superlattices) and junction formation mechanisms (sliding vs. phase transition). STS spectra illustrate gap reduction (weak coupling) versus YSR peak emergence (strong coupling).

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically characterizes the two types of junctions (Type-1/Weak, Type-2/Strong), first establishing the structural basis using STM imaging, followed by detailed electronic spectroscopy (STS) to compare gap behavior and identify emergent bound states.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Proximity-induced superconductivity in strongly correlated insulators provides a versatile route for engineering quantum states of matter and artificial systems with tailored functionalities. However, microscopic interplay between superconductivity and correlated insulating states remains poorly understood. Here we use ultralow-temperature scanning tunnelling microscopy (STM) to systemically investigate superconducting proximity effects in a charge-transfer insulator. Via STM tip manipulation, atomically sharp lateral junctions composed of superconducting monolayer H-NbSe2 and charge-transfer insulating monolayer T-NbSe2 are constructed, enabling direct access to tunable coupling regimes. In the weak-coupling regime, there is a robust proximity-induced superconducting gap in T-NbSe2, with a reduced gap value relative to that of H-NbSe2. Upon entering the strong-coupling regime, T-NbSe2 exhibits a superconducting gap comparable to that of H-NbSe2, accompanied by pronounced particle-hole-symmetric in-gap bound states, consistent with Yu-Shiba-Rusinov-like excitations. These findings establish monolayer H/T-NbSe2 lateral junctions as a model platform for elucidating superconducting proximity effects in strongly correlated charge-transfer insulators.

</details>

**Summary.** The researchers engineered a novel on-chip device combining a Quantum Point Contact with an electronic resonator to study Kondo spins. By controlling the ER's spin parity, they achieved noninvasive detection of two-impurity Kondo effects via characteristic double-peak Zero-Bias Anomalies. This work clarifies that the Kondo physics is distinct from the origin of the anomalous 0.7 conductance plateau.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides an exquisite experimental platform for probing fundamental many-body quantum phenomena—specifically, the competition between different types of spin correlations (Kondo vs. $0.7$ anomaly) in nanoscale conductors—which is highly relevant to understanding correlated electron physics in mesoscopic systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is experimentally distinguishing the Kondo screening state from other complex, coexisting many-body spin states within strongly correlated quasi-one-dimensional nanoconstrictions like Quantum Point Contacts (QPCs).

**Main result.** The authors demonstrated a noninvasive quantum sensing method by using an integrated electronic resonator (ER) to controllably induce and detect two-impurity Kondo effects, while also showing that the 0.7 anomaly counteracts these fluctuations.

**Method.** They utilized lithographically defined all-on-chip devices combining a QPC with an ER in a high-mobility GaAs/AlGaAs heterostructure, measuring linear and nonlinear conductance (Zero-Bias Anomalies) as a function of gate voltages.

**Model / system.** The system involves coupling the localized spin moment within a QPC to a tunable spin state residing in an adjacent electronic resonator (ER). This setup allows for the study of two-impurity Kondo physics and its competition with the anomalous 0.7 conductance plateau.

**Key observables.** Zero-Bias Anomaly (ZBA) peaks, which change from single-peak to double-peak depending on the ER occupancy parity; linear conductance oscillations related to ER filling; and the magnitude of the $0.7$ anomaly.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperature ($T$), magnetic field ($B$), gate voltages controlling QPC/ER occupancy, and the ratio of coupling strengths between impurities.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The study assumes that the observed ZBA features are robust indicators of Kondo screening, despite the presence of other strong correlation effects like the 0.7 anomaly.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the device structure (QPC + ER), show characteristic linear and nonlinear conductance maps, detail how alternating single/double-peak ZBAs correlate with ER filling parity, and demonstrate the suppression of these features upon increasing temperature or magnetic field.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical platform (ER-QPC) to address the ambiguity in spin state identification. It then systematically measures transport properties, using the change in ZBA structure (single vs. double peak) as a diagnostic tool to prove nonlocal control over Kondo screening and differentiate it from the physics causing the 0.7 anomaly.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The precise nature of Kondo spins has remained enigmatic when extended to multiple spin impurities or, more intriguingly, when the localized spin itself may already be the consequence of many-body interactions in a presumably-delocalized open nanoconstriction, such as a quantum point contact (QPC). It is experimentally challenging to distinguish the Kondo state from other coexisting many-body spin states in such a strongly correlated system. Here we lithographically define an all-on-chip electronic resonator (ER) and a QPC in a high-mobility GaAs/AlGaAs heterostructure transistor. Local Kondo screening of the QPC spin and nonlocal spin singlet across the ER-QPC integration are controllable in response to ER occupancy parity. We also show that the 0.7 anomaly, another strongly-correlated state in QPCs, not only has a different physical origin but furthermore counteracts the Kondo spin singlet. These results demonstrate a noninvasive quantum method for sensing spontaneous magnetic impurities within an open nanoconstriction.

</details>
