**Summary.** The paper uses advanced Quantum Monte Carlo simulations to map the phase diagram of 1D bosons with soft-core interactions mimicking Rydberg dressing. It reveals a complex sequence of phases—including TLL, quasi-supersolid, and cluster states—driven by the interplay between interaction range and density. This work is crucial for understanding strongly correlated quantum matter in synthetic atomic systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a detailed theoretical map of quantum phases in low-dimensional systems using modern atomic platforms (Rydberg atoms), bridging concepts from TLL theory, supersolidity, and cluster physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the complex phase diagram of one-dimensional bosons interacting via soft-core Rydberg-dressed potentials.

**Main result.** The system exhibits a rich sequence of phases, transitioning from Tomonaga-Luttinger liquid (TLL) behavior through quasi-supersolid regimes to distinct cluster phases under strong interactions.

**Method.** Exact path-integral Quantum Monte Carlo (QMC) simulations were employed in continuous space to calculate correlation functions and determine the Luttinger parameter K.

**Model / system.** The system models 1D spinless bosons interacting via a soft-core potential derived from Rydberg dressing. The Hamiltonian is analyzed for its competition between roton-driven ordering and particle-hole backscattering processes.

**Key observables.** Luttinger parameter K, single/pair correlation functions ($g_1, g_2$), static structure factor $S(q)$, and cluster proportions ($	ext{n}_{	ext{cluster}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interaction strength ($U_0$), density ($n$), Fermi momentum ($k_F$), and the resulting Luttinger parameter K (which dictates regimes like $K>1$, $K<1$, $K<1/2$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on QMC simulations, which are compared against theoretical frameworks like TLL theory and Bogoliubov approximations.

**Figures summary.** Figures map the quantum phase diagram across interaction strength and density, illustrating transitions between LL-like ($K>1$), HR-like ($K<1$), quasi-supersolid ($K < 1/2$), beyond-TLL, and cluster lobes.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical context using Rydberg gases, details the QMC methodology for calculating correlation functions, analyzes the resulting equation of state $n(\mu)$, and systematically maps out the phase diagram by tracking changes in the Luttinger parameter K across different interaction regimes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Rydberg and Rydberg-dressed atomic gases have recently emerged as a promising quantum simulator for a variety of models in condensed matter physics. Here we investigate one-dimensional bosons with soft-core Rydberg-dressed interactions using exact path-integral quantum Monte Carlo simulations. The finite-range and the negative Fourier component of the interaction potential generate a roton mode at finite momentum, while particle-hole backscattering processes enhance the susceptibility of one-dimensional systems at twice the Fermi momentum. The competition between the corresponding length scales yields a rich phase diagram, featuring a conventional Tomonaga-Luttinger liquid (TLL) regime, a beyond-TLL regime, and commensurate cluster phases. In the TLL regime, the system transitions from Lieb-Liniger-like bnehavior with Luttinger parameter $K>1$ to hard-rod-like behavior with $K<1$, with a quasi-supersolid phase emerging for $K < 1/2$. For strong interactions and high densities, deviations from TLL theory appear as precursors for the onset of cluster phases, where particles aggregate into stable clusters of several particles. The properties of each phase is discussed in detail.

</details>

The close overlap between the experimental and theoretical coincidence counts demonstrates good agreement with...</figcaption>

**Summary.** This paper introduces an optimized Quantum Phase Estimation (QPE) algorithm that reduces circuit complexity from $O(n^2)$ to $O(n)$ for structured unitary operators. The method is realized deterministically on a four-qubit photonic platform using polarization and path modes. This advancement significantly improves the feasibility of implementing complex quantum algorithms in physical hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it tackles a core computational bottleneck in quantum algorithms—the complexity of QPE—by providing both a theoretical simplification and a robust, deterministic photonic realization.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to improve Quantum Phase Estimation (QPE) by reducing its circuit complexity from a standard $O(n^2)$ scaling down to an optimal linear $O(n)$ scaling for specific structured unitary operators.

**Main result.** The authors successfully developed and demonstrated an optimized QPE algorithm that achieves linear complexity while maintaining full determinism, which was experimentally validated on a photonic platform.

**Method.** The optimization involves exploiting the inherent structure of the target unitary operator to eliminate the computationally expensive Inverse Quantum Fourier Transform (IQFT) stage, replacing controlled operations with simpler controlled-Z gates.

**Model / system.** The work is implemented on a four-qubit photonic system utilizing a photon pair. The qubits are encoded using a hybrid scheme combining polarization degrees of freedom and spatial path modes for the quantum walk architecture.

**Key observables.** Coincidence signatures at output ports used to uniquely identify and measure the estimated eigenphases ($\phi$), with successful observation matching theoretical predictions for specific eigenvalues (e.g., 1, i, -1, -i).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The complexity reduction is demonstrated for a special class of diagonal unitary operators $U = P(\pi) \otimes P(\pi/2) \otimes \cdots$, and the initial experimental validation was performed at a 2-bit precision level ($m=2$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The efficiency gain relies critically on the structured, diagonal nature of the unitary operators being analyzed. Experimental limitations include source non-idealities and interferometer instabilities.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the comparison between the standard QPE circuit (requiring controlled-$U_{2k}$ operations) and the optimized circuit (using controlled-Z gates), showing the elimination of the IQFT network for a specific unitary case.

**Paper structure.** The paper first reviews the general QPE protocol and its $O(n^2)$ complexity. It then introduces the mathematical insight that allows simplification for structured unitaries, leading to an optimized $O(n)$ algorithm. Finally, it details the physical implementation on photonics and presents experimental validation of phase estimation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum algorithms solve certain computational problems faster than the best known classical algorithms. Many algorithms, including Shor's for factoring, Grover's for unstructured search, and the HHL for solving linear systems, rely on quantum phase estimation (QPE) as a fundamental subroutine. The QPE protocol proceeds through the initialization of a control register in a uniform superposition, controlled unitary evolution encoding the eigenphase, and a final inverse quantum Fourier transform followed by measurement to extract the phase. Here, we address a special class of unitary operators that frequently appear in quantum Fourier transform-based protocols, cyclic group representations, and periodically evolving quantum systems. We introduce a QPE algorithm that successfully reduces the circuit complexity from $\mathcal{O}(n^2)$ to $\mathcal{O}(n)$ for a special class of unitary operators and implement it on a four-qubit photonic system. The four-qubit system is realised using a photon pair, with two qubits encoded in its polarization degree of freedom and the remaining two in its path modes. In contrast to previous photonic implementations of QPE based on dual-rail encoding and KLM protocol, where controlled operations are inherently probabilistic and thus reduce the overall success probability of phase estimation, our scheme is fully deterministic. Moreover, it is scalable to higher-dimensional unitaries, provided the underlying structure of the unitaries is preserved.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops optimal control strategies for implementing fast, high-fidelity entangling gates in trapped ion quantum processors. By moving beyond simple harmonic approximations and utilizing anharmonic dynamics, the authors design driving pulses resilient to common noise sources like detuning and temperature variations. This advances the feasibility of building scalable quantum computation hardware based on trapped ions.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the practical challenges of scaling quantum computation using trapped ions by providing advanced pulse shaping techniques that explicitly incorporate and mitigate environmental decoherence sources, which is central to modern AMO research.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is designing fast, high-fidelity entangling quantum gates for trapped ions that are robust against environmental noise sources like detuning and temperature fluctuations.

**Main result.** Optimal control schemes exploiting anharmonic motional dynamics allow for the realization of high-fidelity gates, showing resilience to noise particularly when the Lamb-Dicke parameter is small.

**Method.** The authors employ optimal control techniques (like GRAPE) to find time-dependent driving pulses that minimize gate infidelity while accounting for ensemble averaging over noise distributions.

**Model / system.** The system uses trapped ions as qubits, with interactions mediated by collective motional degrees of freedom. The dynamics are governed by a complex Hamiltonian incorporating both qubit and motional terms, moving beyond simple harmonic approximations to include anharmonicity.

**Key observables.** Gate fidelity ($F$) and ensemble-averaged infidelity ($ar{I}$), measured as a function of gate duration ($T_G$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Lamb-Dicke parameter ($\eta$), Rabi frequency ($\Omega$), detuning ($\Delta$), and the trap period ($T$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on numerically optimized control schemes, and while anharmonicity is exploited, initial approximations (like those used in standard Mølmer-Sørensen schemes) are shown to be insufficient for high performance.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare gate infidelity versus gate duration for different $\eta$, contrast optimized drives against conventional ones, and illustrate the effect of noise averaging on fidelity across various parameter regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the physical Hamiltonian and identifying limitations in harmonic approximations to formulating the optimal control problem. It then details the use of gradient-based optimization methods (like GRAPE) to design pulses that achieve target unitaries while minimizing infidelity under noise models.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Entangling gates of trapped ions are typically mediated by collective motional degrees of freedom. Weak coupling between qubit and motional degrees of freedom and the resulting harmonic dynamics give access to a broad range of gate schemes, but also impose strict limitations on achievable gate times. In this paper, we devise optimally designed driving schemes for the realization of fast, high-fidelity entangling gates mediated by anharmonic dynamics. The driving can also be optimized to achieve resilience to multiple system imperfections, and the anharmonicity in the motional dynamics can be used to enhance such resilience.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors report on the design and experimental realization of a versatile, laser-machined rf Paul trap capable of holding large arrays of ions (100+). By stacking precisely machined fused silica wafers with independently biased electrodes, they achieve unprecedented control over trapping potentials, enabling stable confinement in complex 1D and 2D geometries. This platform significantly advances the hardware required for scaling up quantum simulation experiments using trapped ions.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work represents a significant engineering advance in the physical platform for quantum simulation. The ability to precisely engineer trap potentials using macro-scale machining opens up new regimes of control over ion crystal geometry compared to previous traps.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The development of a versatile, macroscopic radiofrequency (rf) trap capable of confining large arrays of ions in various one-dimensional and two-dimensional geometries for quantum simulation.

**Main result.** The fabricated laser-machined trap successfully supports robust confinement for ion crystals exceeding 100 ions, while demonstrating tunable control over trapping potentials, micromotion compensation, and low heating rates.

**Method.** The authors utilized precision laser machining of stacked fused silica wafers to create ten independently biased electrodes, allowing precise electrical control over the confining potential.

**Model / system.** The system is a macroscopic rf Paul trap designed for trapped ions (specifically $^{171}	ext{Yb}^+$). The trapping potential is modeled using finite-element method (FEM) simulations and analyzed via pseudopotential approximations to predict secular frequencies.

**Key observables.** Radial and axial secular frequencies ($\omega_y, \omega_z$), micromotion modulation index ($eta$), motional heating rate ($\dot{ar{n}}$), and the stable geometry of ion arrays (1D chains, 2D crystals).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The trap supports confinement for $N \approx 500$ to $1000$ ions; frequencies can exceed $2\pi 	imes 3.5$ MHz; residual micromotion is shown to be $\lesssim 0.02$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary limitations are expected to be off-axis micromotion and background gas collisions at room temperature, despite theoretical estimates for very large ion numbers.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the trap's CAD assembly (stacked wafers), predicted equilibrium geometries for various configurations (1D/2D), measured secular frequencies versus RF voltage, and measurements of micromotion modulation.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the design and fabrication process using laser-machined fused silica. It presents theoretical modeling via FEM and pseudopotential analysis to predict potential shaping. Experimental sections demonstrate trapping capability across various geometries (1D/2D), measure key motional parameters like micromotion and heating rates, and confirm operational stability.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Large ion crystals in diverse geometries are a key resource for quantum simulation experiments. In this work, we introduce a macroscopic rf trap that supports a wide variety of one-dimensional ion configurations as well as lateral two-dimensional crystals with more than 100 ions. Our design is based on precision-machined fused silica wafers that are stacked to form the trap structure. Ten independently biased electrodes provide flexible control over the axial potential, enabling long one-dimensional crystals, isospaced ion strings, split-well chains, and two-dimensional arrays with tunable aspect ratios. We present the design and fabrication process for this trap and demonstrate the ability to tune the radial secular frequencies, detect and compensate micromotion, rotate the principal axes, and characterize trapped ion heating rates. All trap design and documentation files are freely available alongside this work, to facilitate adoption and further development within the ion trap community.

</details>

Dashed lines are fits to the elastic peak and a constant background. Black arrow indicates the energy of the l scan shown in (d). (d) Neu- tron scattering intensity as a function of l at Q = (1/6, 1/6, l) for ℏω = 0.3 meV. Red...</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This work uses polarized neutron scattering to probe the spin supersolid state in a near-Ising antiferromagnet. The research confirms that the supersolid order survives even when strongly anisotropic, revealing that low-energy dynamics are dominated by longitudinal fluctuations rather than transverse ones. This provides crucial experimental data constraining theoretical models of frustrated quantum magnets.

**Why it may be interesting.** The finding that low-energy excitations are predominantly longitudinal, contrasting with conventional expectations for such systems, provides stringent constraints on microscopic theoretical models (like XXZ) describing frustrated magnetism and quantum phase transitions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the nature of transverse order and longitudinal fluctuations within the spin supersolid state of a near-Ising antiferromagnet.

**Main result.** The system exhibits evidence for a persistent supersolid ground state in the near-Ising regime, characterized by predominantly longitudinal low-energy spin dynamics despite the presence of a small transverse ordered moment.

**Method.** Neutron scattering experiments (both unpolarized and polarized) were used to measure magnetic structure factors and analyze temperature dependence across successive BKT transitions.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the S=1/2 triangular-lattice easy-axis antiferromagnet K2Co(SeO3)2, modeled by an XXZ Hamiltonian near the Ising limit.

**Key observables.** Longitudinal ($S_{zz}$) and transverse ($S_{\perp}$) ordered moments; low-energy spin fluctuation spectra; flipping ratio (FR); interlayer correlation parameters ($A_{01}, A_{02}, A_{03}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Near-Ising regime, $T \approx 0.35	ext{ K}$ (for transverse order onset), and the small ratio of transverse to longitudinal moment ($\sim 11\%$ or $\sim 32\%$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on separating spin correlations into longitudinal and transverse components using polarized neutron scattering techniques.

**Figures summary.** Figures show temperature dependence of magnetic intensity (NSF/SF channels) highlighting transitions, and plots of the flipping ratio (FR) showing changes below $	ext{TBKT},3$. Other figures detail spectral weight suppression in inelastic measurements.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the development of supersolid order through successive BKT transitions using polarized neutron scattering to quantify longitudinal vs. transverse moments, followed by detailed analysis of low-energy spin dynamics.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the polarization of the ordered moments and low-energy spin fluctuations in the spin supersolid state of the S = 1/2 triangular-lattice easy-axis antiferromagnet K2Co(SeO3)2 using neutron scattering. The supersolid order develops through successive BKT transitions: the longitudinal order appears at higher temperature, while the transverse component associated with the Bose-Einstein condensate emerges only below a lower-temperature transition accompanied by a change in the interlayer correlations. At the lowest measured temperature, the transverse ordered moment reaches only 11% of the longitudinal component. Moreover, the low-energy spin fluctuations are found to be predominantly longitudinal in character. These results provide direct evidence that the supersolid ground state survives even in the near-Ising regime and exhibits longitudinal low-energy dynamics, imposing stringent constraints on microscopic theories of triangular-lattice XXZ antiferromagnets.

</details>

**Summary.** This work explores Fractional Chern Insulators in twisted multilayer MoTe2 by tuning parameters like layer sliding distance and electric fields. The key finding is that the stability of these highly correlated topological states depends critically on the system's underlying quantum geometry, quantified by a specific trace condition. This establishes sliding as an effective knob for controlling exotic many-body phases.

**Why it may be interesting.** The explicit connection between geometric properties (quantum geometry/trace condition) and the stabilization/destruction of exotic correlated topological phases like FCIs provides a powerful, measurable diagnostic tool applicable to moiré heterostructures relevant to quantum simulation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates strongly correlated many-body states, specifically Fractional Chern Insulators (FCIs), in alternating twisted multilayer MoTe2.

**Main result.** FCIs are found to be stabilized only in specific parameter regimes, a contrast explained by the quantum geometry quantified by the trace condition, which can destroy FCI phases even when bands are topological.

**Method.** The research employs numerical calculations involving exact diagonalization of the many-body Hamiltonian projected onto the topmost hole band.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled using alternating twisted trilayer (AT3L) and tetralayer (AT4L) MoTe2 moiré systems, incorporating kinetic energy, interlayer tunneling, and an external perpendicular electric field.

**Key observables.** Chern number, quantum geometric metrics (Fubini-Study metric), the trace condition (T), bandwidth (W), and various energy gaps ($G_1, G_2$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Sliding distance ($\delta$) of the top layer relative to others, perpendicular electric field strength ($D$), and twist angle ($	heta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Electrons are assumed to be spin-valley polarized; a continuum model is used for low-energy description.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate band structures (Fig. 2), the evolution of diagnostic quantities like the trace condition and gaps with sliding distance $\delta$ (Fig. 4), and phase diagrams showing FCI vs CDW regimes based on applied fields (Fig. 5).

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the system by first establishing the Hamiltonian, then calculating band structures and quantum geometry metrics as a function of tunable parameters ($\delta$, $D$), culminating in identifying the trace condition as the key discriminator for FCI stability.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study strongly correlated many-body states in alternating twisted trilayer and tetralayer MoTe$_{2}$. By sliding the top layer with respect to others and applying a perpendicular electric field, a variety of band structures can be realized. In many cases, the topmost hole band has unity Chern number and its quantum geometric properties can be tuned to some extent. Exact diagonalizations suggest that fractional Chern insulators are stabilized in certain parameter regimes but not in some regimes even when the band is topological. This contrast is attributed primarily to different quantum geometries as quantified by the trace condition. Our results demonstrate that sliding can serve as a useful knob for probing many-body states in moiré systems.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors report on a high-performance, fibre-integrated single-photon source utilizing Zn-indiffused PPLN waveguides pumped via degenerate SPDC. The system demonstrates excellent purity and brightness across telecom wavelengths, establishing it as a robust platform for wavelength-multiplexed quantum networking applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses quantum networking hardware by demonstrating a highly efficient, broadband, integrated photonics source, which is crucial for scalable quantum communication protocols.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To characterize a fibre-integrated heralded single-photon source based on spontaneous parametric down-conversion (SPDC) using Zn-indiffused PPLN ridge waveguides at telecom wavelengths.

**Main result.** The source achieved high performance metrics, including an absolute brightness of $9.1	imes 10^{9}$ pairs/(s mW), a low heralded second-order correlation ($g_{	ext{h}}^{(2)}(0) \approx 5.53	imes 10^{-4}$), and a competitive internal heralding efficiency of $(58 \pm 5)\%$.

**Method.** The characterization involved measuring the broadband spectrum, quantifying pair correlations using the coincidences-to-accidentals ratio (CAR), and calculating the heralded second-order correlation function.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a fibre-pigtailed MgO:PPLN ridge waveguide module operating in Type-0 degenerate SPDC. The analysis utilized numerical modeling via a fully vectorial optical mode solver (FIMMWAVE) to predict theoretical pair generation rates and modal profiles.

**Key observables.** Absolute brightness ($9.1	imes 10^{9}$ pairs/(s mW)), internal heralding efficiency ($(58 \pm 5)\%$), coincidences-to-accidentals ratio ($> 9	imes 10^4$), and heralded second-order correlation ($g_{	ext{h}}^{(2)}(0) = (5.53 \pm 0.46)	imes 10^{-4}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Broadband emission spanning $\approx 70 	ext{ nm}$ across the C- and L-bands; theoretical pair generation rate ($	ext{R}_{	ext{SPDC}}/	ext{P}$) $\approx 2.7 	imes 10^{10}$ pairs $	ext{s}^{-1} 	ext{mW}^{-1}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The reported performance metrics are based on a comprehensive characterization that accounts for optical losses and the specific waveguide structure.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the fibre-pigtailed module, schematic of the Zn-indiffused ridge waveguide, mode analysis showing index contrast profiles at different wavelengths, and experimental setups for SPDC generation and bandwidth measurement.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first reporting initial source performance metrics, followed by detailed characterization of the waveguide structure and mode modeling (including SHG measurements), culminating in the description of the full experimental SPDC measurement process to validate theoretical limits.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We report a fibre-integrated heralded single-photon source based on a commercial Zn-indiffused MgO:PPLN ridge waveguide operated in Type-0 degenerate spontaneous parametric down-conversion (SPDC). The device emits a broadband spectrum spanning approximately 70 nm across the telecom C-and L-bands, with an absolute brightness of $9.1\times 10^{9}$ pairs/(s mW). After accounting for optical losses, we determine an internal heralding efficiency of 58(5)%. The source maintains high purity, exhibiting a coincidences-to-accidentals ratio exceeding $9\times 10^{4}$ and a heralded second-order correlation of $g_{\text{h}}^{(2)}(0) = (5.53 \pm 0.46)\times 10^{-4}$. Low multi-photon noise and broadband emission demonstrate the viability of such platforms for wavelength-multiplexed quantum networking.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper demonstrates that wireless microwave interconnects can successfully excite superconducting resonators at millikelvin temperatures, offering a path to overcome wiring density limitations in quantum computing. By comparing results to traditional wired links, the authors validate the feasibility of free-space coupling while also characterizing unavoidable parasitic losses from stray radiation within the cryostat. The findings establish critical design guidelines for integrating wireless components into future scalable quantum hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the physical engineering challenges of scaling quantum processors by providing empirical data on microwave coupling in extreme cryogenic environments, which is crucial for building large quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Scalability of quantum computing is bottlenecked by dense electrical interconnects, necessitating a reliable wireless link solution for cryogenic hardware.

**Main result.** Wireless excitation preserves the intrinsic superconducting resonator response compared to wired links, establishing a framework for co-designing such interconnects despite residual stray radiation losses.

**Method.** The authors experimentally compare microwave resonator characterization using both direct wiring and free-space wireless coupling within a dilution refrigerator environment.

**Model / system.** The system involves superconducting microwave resonators (DUT) patterned on NbN film, measured at millikelvin temperatures. The interconnect uses planar antennas and metalens optics to guide microwaves through cryostat apertures.

**Key observables.** Resonant frequency ($f_r$), internal quality factor ($Q_i$), transmission coefficient ($	ext{|S}_{21}|$), and temperature dependence of these parameters.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Operating temperatures ranging from $20 	ext{ mK}$ to $3.0 	ext{ K}$; input power in the few-photon regime (approx. $-116 	ext{ dBm}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The study assumes that wireless coupling can be engineered to match the intrinsic response of superconducting hardware, though residual stray radiation remains a challenge.

**Figures summary.** Figures show schematic setups for wireless links, $	ext{|S}_{21}|$ spectra comparing wired vs. wireless modes across temperatures, and analyses detailing stray loss mechanisms and the effect of RF absorbers on signal quality.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the interconnect bottleneck to demonstrating successful wireless excitation, characterizing the resulting losses (stray radiation), and finally proposing a co-design framework incorporating packaging and material science solutions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Scalable quantum computing is limited by the dense network of electrical interconnects linking cryogenic quantum processors to room-temperature control electronics. To overcome this bottleneck, considerable effort has focused on cryogenic CMOS electronics and microwave-to-optical transduction, aiming to reduce wiring complexity and thermal loading. Wireless interconnects have recently emerged as a promising complementary approach, yet their compatibility with superconducting quantum hardware remains largely unexplored. Here, we demonstrate the wireless excitation of a superconducting microwave resonator of the type routinely employed for qubit readout, operating at millikelvin temperatures inside a dilution refrigerator. By directly comparing wired and wireless operation within the same cryogenic environment, we show that wireless coupling preserves the intrinsic resonator response while revealing parasitic electromagnetic pathways arising from stray radiation within the cryostat enclosure. These results establish a framework for the co-design of wireless interconnects, cryogenic packaging and superconducting quantum hardware.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study explores the complex quantum phases of ultra-cold dipolar mixtures trapped in checker-board optical bilayers. By analyzing the interplay between long-range dipole interactions and particle hopping, the authors map out multiple crystalline ground states. The key finding is that tunneling can drive solid-into-solid phase transitions between these distinct crystal structures.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses many-body physics in ultracold atomic systems using dipolar interactions, which is a major frontier in AMO physics. The prediction of super-exchange induced phase transitions provides concrete, testable predictions for optical lattice experiments.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the complex interplay between intra- and inter-component anisotropic long-range interactions within dipolar mixtures.

**Main result.** The system exhibits a rich landscape of crystalline phases, including solid-into-solid transitions induced by super-exchange processes as external parameters are varied.

**Method.** The research combines second-order perturbation theory with Cluster-Gutzwiller (CGW) calculations to analyze the ground state properties and phase diagrams.

**Model / system.** The system models ultra-cold dipolar mixtures confined in separated, checker-board optical bilayers. The physics is governed by an extended Bose-Hubbard Hamiltonian incorporating dipole interactions and hopping terms.

**Key observables.** Crystalline phases (Checker-board, Two-row stripe, Intermediate Crystalline, Phase-Separated), phase transition lines, and energy shifts ($\Delta E$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Inter-layer separation ($h$), inter-component interaction strength ($V$), dipole orientation angle ($	heta$), and hopping amplitude ($J$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Assumes equal populations ($\mu_1 = \mu_2$) and uniform on-site interactions ($V_{1,1} = V_{2,2} = V_{1,2} = V$); CGW methods are used for melting transitions while super-exchange is needed for solid-solid ones.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the setup (bilayer scheme), phase diagrams showing transitions between CB, 2ST, CRS, and PS phases based on $h$, and how these transitions depend on dipole orientation ($	heta$) and hopping ($J$).

**Paper structure.** The paper analyzes the ground state by first establishing the Hamiltonian in the bilayer setup. It then maps out phase diagrams showing transitions (CB $\leftrightarrow$ 2ST $\leftrightarrow$ CRS $\leftrightarrow$ PS) as $h$ changes, followed by analyzing how finite hopping ($J$) induces solid-into-solid transitions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Ultra-cold dipolar mixtures in component-dependent optical potentials constitute an interesting platform for the study of the interplay between intra- and inter-component anisotropic long-range interactions. We study the particular case of binary dipolar mixtures placed in separated bilayers which are displaced in an anti-magic wavelength configuration. Using a combination of second-order perturbation theory and cluster-Gutzwiller calculations, we unveil a rich landscape of possible crystalline phases for the two components, showing that, interestingly, inter-site hopping may result, via super-exchange, in solid-into-solid transitions between different crystalline phases. These crystalline phases and the corresponding transitions can be experimentally realized using e.g. lanthanide mixtures in optical lattices.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates whether current quantum computing platforms can solve real-world industrial scheduling problems by comparing results from quantum, quantum-inspired, and classical methods. The authors conclude that while these techniques are useful for early prototyping and guiding solver selection, achieving high performance requires careful co-design between the problem formulation and the specific hardware architecture used.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on scheduling, the rigorous comparison of different physical implementations (annealers vs. gate models) and the explicit discussion of hardware constraints provide valuable insights into near-term quantum algorithm implementation challenges relevant to any complex Hamiltonian simulation or optimization task.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Assessing the practical industrial utility of quantum optimization algorithms for complex combinatorial problems, specifically using an industrial variant of the Job-Shop Scheduling Problem (JSSP).

**Main result.** Hardware-software co-design is crucial for achieving good solution quality and scalability; current quantum methods are best suited for supporting solver selection and proof-of-concept development rather than guaranteeing global optimality.

**Method.** The study benchmarks quantum, quantum-inspired (e.g., QAOA), and classical solvers against an exact MILP baseline by formulating the scheduling problem as QUBOs.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled using formulations for JSSP variants (Single/Multi-Constraint Models) solved on three platforms: IBM Quantum (gate-based), D-Wave Quantum Annealer, and Fujitsu Digital Annealer.

**Key observables.** Runtime, solution quality (optimality gap), and scalability across different hardware backends.

**Important parameters / regimes.** QUBO size (number of qubits/constraints), time budget for classical solvers, and the specific formulation used (Single vs. Multi-Constraint).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The optimal QUBO solution does not guarantee the global optimum because the complex scheduling task is artificially split into assignment and ordering stages.

**Figures summary.** Not specified by notes; focus is on comparative benchmarking of performance metrics across platforms.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically compares multiple optimization approaches (quantum, quantum-inspired, classical) applied to a real-world scheduling problem, emphasizing the necessary co-design between formulation and hardware.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum Processing Units promise speed-ups for selected computational problems, including combinatorial optimisation, but their industrial utility remains an open challenge. We study an industrial variant of the Job-Shop Scheduling Problem using quantum, quantum-inspired, and classical methods across three platforms: IBM Quantum, the D-Wave Quantum Annealer, and the Fujitsu Digital Annealer. By tailoring formulations to hardware-specific constraints, we show that hardware-software co-design is essential for solution quality and scalability. We benchmark all approaches against an exact classical solver and a MILP formulation, evaluating runtime, solution quality, and scalability. Our results indicate that quantum and quantum-inspired optimisation can support industrial solver selection, integration in classical workflows, modelling decisions, and early proof-of-concept development, while suggesting a potential path towards improved approximations for industrial scheduling.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously compares three novel W-like quantum states against the standard W state using multiple entanglement measures. It proves mathematically that these new states exhibit superior entanglement properties and robustness against particle loss. Consequently, they are shown to be necessary resources for achieving perfect superdense coding and teleportation protocols.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a detailed resource comparison within quantum information theory, identifying optimal entangled states for fundamental protocols like teleportation and dense coding that surpass standard benchmark states.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper compares three specific W-like quantum states ($|\vartheta'angle, |\eta'angle, |\xi'angle$) against the standard W state to determine which is superior for quantum communication tasks.

**Main result.** The three W-like states are shown to possess higher entanglement robustness and are suitable resources for perfect teleportation and superdense coding, capabilities that the standard W state lacks.

**Method.** The investigation uses rigorous mathematical proofs involving multiple entanglement measures (vNEE, tangle, negativity) derived from Schmidt Decomposition analysis.

**Model / system.** The system involves three-qubit quantum states. The comparison is between the general W state and specific parameterized W-like states ($\{|\varthetaangle, |\etaangle, |\xiangle\}$).

**Key observables.** Von Neumann entanglement entropy (vNEE), 2-tangle ($	au$), Negativity, and their averages across different qubit subsystems.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The condition for maximal vNEE components ($S(ho_\mu) = \ln 2$) is a key constraint used to define the optimal states.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the W-like states are LU equivalent to their derived Schmidt Decomposition forms, and it relies on specific local unitary operations for demonstrating protocols.

**Figures summary.** Tables compare entanglement measures ($	au_{xy}$, $	ext{A}_{
u nee}$, etc.) across GHZ, W, and the three optimized W-like states; other tables summarize single-qubit entropies and average negativity.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes necessary conditions for maximal entanglement components. It then proves that specific parameterized states uniquely maximize various entanglement measures compared to the W state. Finally, it demonstrates the practical utility of these optimized states by showing they enable perfect teleportation and superdense coding.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In [Phy. Rev. Lett., 98, 260501 (2007)] and previous papers, the W-like states were used in many quantum communication schemes, distillation, teleportation and superdense coding. So far, no one has compared the W-like states with the W state. We investigate the properties of three W-like states named as $|\vartheta ^{\prime }\rangle $, $|η^{\prime }\rangle $ and $|ξ^{\prime }\rangle $. We point out that $|\vartheta ^{\prime }\rangle $ (resp. $|η^{\prime }\rangle $ and $|ξ^{\prime }\rangle $) has the maximal von Neumann entanglement entropy (vNEE) $S(ρ_{A})=\ln 2$ (resp. $S(ρ_{B})=\ln 2$ and $S(ρ_{C})=\ln 2$) and the maximal tangle $τ_{A(BC)}=1$ (resp. $τ_{B(AC)}=1$ and $τ_{C(AB)}=1$) while the W state does not. We also indicate that if some one of three qubits is traced out, then the remaining two qubits of $|\vartheta ^{\prime }\rangle $ (resp. $|η^{\prime }\rangle $ and $|ξ^{\prime }\rangle $) are more entangled than the two qubits of the W state by several entanglement measures. It means that the three W-like states have higher robustness against some particle loss than the W state. We show that $|\vartheta ^{\prime }\rangle $, $|η^{\prime }\rangle $ and $|ξ^{\prime }\rangle $ can be suitable for perfect teleportation and superdense coding but the W state cannot.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors introduce Temporal Fourier Optics (TFO) to overcome the challenge of spectral broadening caused by dissipation in optical measurements. By treating this broadening as a compensable temporal attenuation, they develop a TPSF reconstruction method. This technique successfully reveals hidden hybridized light-matter states in plasmonic and molecular systems, offering a general tool for spectroscopy across various wave physics domains.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful, experimentally accessible tool—TFO—to probe fundamental quantum phenomena like strong light-matter coupling in dissipative environments, extending beyond standard complex-frequency formalisms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Intrinsic dissipation and measurement-induced spectral broadening obscure underlying hybridized light–matter states, making their direct observation challenging in spectroscopy.

**Main result.** The Temporal Fourier Optics (TFO) framework successfully reconstructs these hidden upper and lower polaritonic branches across diverse platforms, revealing strong coupling regimes otherwise unresolved by conventional methods.

**Method.** A temporal point-spread-function (TPSF) reconstruction method is implemented, which compensates for effective temporal decay before performing a Fourier transformation to recover intrinsic spectral responses.

**Model / system.** The work applies the framework to deterministic single-molecule Au nanosphere dimers and open Au@Ag plasmonic nanocavities coupled to J-aggregate excitons. The underlying physics is governed by wave propagation principles analogous to spatial diffraction.

**Key observables.** Reconstructed upper and lower polaritonic branches, intrinsic spectral responses, scattering cross sections.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Strong coupling regime ($	ext{g} > (\gamma_{	ext{cav}} - \gamma_e)/4$), temporal decay rate ($\gamma$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Spectral broadening is mathematically equivalent to an effective temporal attenuation that can be inverted via the TFO framework.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 conceptually illustrates the space–time Fourier correspondence linking spatial diffraction and temporal spectral broadening; other figures likely show reconstruction results on various platforms.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the theoretical foundation by drawing an analogy between spatial PSF (diffraction) and temporal TPSF (dissipation). It then details the mathematical framework for compensation, followed by experimental validation across multiple plasmonic and molecular systems to demonstrate hidden state recovery.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Spectral measurements provide fundamental insights into wave systems by revealing resonances, mode hybridization, and light-matter interactions. However, intrinsic dissipation and measurement-induced spectral broadening often conceal the underlying hybridized light-matter states that give rise to measured spectra. Here, we establish a space-time Fourier correspondence that interprets spectral broadening as an effective temporal attenuation, giving rise to a temporal Fourier optics framework for recovering hidden spectral information. Implemented through a temporal point-spread-function (TPSF) reconstruction method, the framework compensates the effective temporal decay before Fourier transformation, directly reconstructing intrinsic spectral responses from experimentally measured spectra without repeated frequency synthesis or model-dependent fitting. We experimentally validate the approach in deterministic single-molecule Au nanosphere dimers and open Au@Ag nanorod- and nanotriangle-based plasmonic nanocavities coupled to J-aggregate excitons. Across these diverse platforms, TPSF consistently reconstructs hidden upper and lower polaritonic branches, thereby revealing the underlying hybridized light-matter states and strong coupling that remain unresolved in conventional scattering spectra. The reconstructed spectra agree closely with the recently developed complex-frequency formalism while offering a considerably simpler and more experimentally accessible implementation. Beyond strong light-matter coupling, temporal Fourier optics establishes a general framework for uncovering physical states hidden by dissipation, opening new opportunities for spectroscopy, imaging, sensing, and inverse wave measurements across photonics and wave physics.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors report the direct observation of photon-induced vortex-antivortex pair dynamics in superconducting films, interpreting the resulting quantized voltage signals as discrete phase-slip events. By analyzing the statistical fluctuations of these VAPs, they demonstrate that the device achieves photon-number resolution by linking absorbed photons directly to countable flux quanta changes.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides an experimental realization of quantum light detection based on fundamental superconducting phase dynamics (phase slips), offering a novel pathway for high-speed quantum optical detectors distinct from standard resistive or quasiparticle tunneling mechanisms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to directly observe photon-induced vortex-antivortex pair (VAP) dynamics in superconducting films and demonstrate a mechanism for photon-number resolution.

**Main result.** The detection signal is governed by quantized voltage pulses resulting from discrete phase-slip events, allowing for the direct counting of absorbed photons under specific operating conditions.

**Method.** Measurements involve irradiating a superconducting film with laser pulses while monitoring the resulting quantized voltage signals using SQUID readout.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a bilayer superconducting film patterned into a strip (VBD) operated in a current-carrying state. The mechanism relies on non-equilibrium phase dynamics where photon absorption triggers VAP nucleation, leading to $2\pi$ phase slips.

**Key observables.** Quantized voltage pulses whose time integral equals integer multiples of the magnetic flux quantum ($\Phi_0$), and the second-order correlation function ($g^{(2)}_{	ext{VAP}}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Bias current ($I_b$), base temperature ($T_b$), input photon number ($\mu_{	ext{in}}$), and the superconducting phase slip mechanism.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The detection process is fundamentally governed by discrete phase-slip events rather than conventional resistive transitions, and PNR capability emerges when VAP generation fluctuations are statistically suppressed (approaching Poissonian statistics).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate comparisons between resistive vs. phase-slip detection modes; show the temperature dependence of critical current ($I_c$); display pulse height distributions showing discrete peaks; and finally, present $g^{(2)}$ measurements confirming Poissonian statistics for photon counting.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the physical platform (VBD) and contrasting its mechanism with conventional detectors. It then details the measurement of quantized voltage signals ($\Phi_0$ quantization). Finally, it uses correlation functions ($g^{(2)}$) to demonstrate that under optimized conditions, the VAP generation statistics match the incident photon statistics, confirming PNR.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Nucleation of vortex-antivortex pairs (VAPs) is believed to play a central role in the photon detection mechanism of superconducting detectors; however, their direct dynamic observation has remained challenging. Here, we report the direct observation of photon-induced VAP dynamics in a current-carrying superconductor as quantized voltage signals following photon absorption. The observed signals are interpreted as discrete phase-slip events, where each vortex traversal induces a 2-pi phase change of the superconducting order parameter, resulting in a quantized voltage pulse whose time integral is given by the magnetic flux quantum. We analyze the resulting quantized signals as a function of bias current, base temperature, and input photon-number states, and find that the number of VAPs generated per absorbed photon becomes effectively stabilized under specific conditions. Under these conditions, we demonstrate photon-number-resolving capability by directly counting phase-slip-induced voltage quanta. Our results reveal a detection mechanism governed by phase dynamics rather than conventional resistive transitions. We further show that photon-number resolution emerges when the fluctuation of photon-induced vortex-antivortex pair generation becomes statistically suppressed. These findings establish a new route toward photon-number-resolving detection based on phase-slip counting and open opportunities for high-speed superconducting detectors for quantum optics and photonic quantum technologies.

</details>

**Summary.** This work introduces a model where synaptic memory persists even after neuronal firing stops. The key finding is that the residual synaptic configuration alone contains sufficient information to predict if the network will spontaneously reactivate (regenerate activity) or die down. This suggests short-term memory is encoded in this latent, quantifiable synaptic capacity rather than just ongoing spikes.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on neuroscience, the concept of predicting complex, non-equilibrium dynamics (like activity continuation) solely from a static 'memory' snapshot mirrors challenges in characterizing quantum states or emergent behavior in many-body systems without full time evolution.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates whether the residual synaptic state alone contains enough information to predict if a neuronal network will spontaneously regenerate activity after a period of complete silence, or if it will simply terminate.

**Main result.** The latent excitatory recruitment (LER) capacity, derived from analyzing the initial synaptic memory snapshot, was shown to be a near-perfect predictor of whether multi-cycle regeneration occurs without needing to simulate subsequent network dynamics.

**Method.** The authors developed a novel snapshot-based framework that analyzes the residual synaptic configuration ($\mathbf{L}(t)$) at the first silent state to predict future dynamical outcomes.

**Model / system.** The model is a minimal Synaptic-Lifetime Network (SLN), which is a discrete-time excitatory–inhibitory neural network incorporating finite-lifetime synapses. The system's state is defined by both neuronal activity ($\mathbf{X}(t)$) and the synaptic memory matrix ($\mathbf{L}(t)$).

**Key observables.** Latent Excitatory Recruitment (LER) capacity, density of active neurons ($ho(t)$), and the comparison between one-cycle vs. multi-cycle activity realizations.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Network size ($N=2000$), mean degree ($\langle k_{in} angle = 200$), synaptic lifetimes ($T_E, T_I$), and network weights ($W_E=1, W_I=-4$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the system can be characterized by a single snapshot $\langle X(t), L(t) angle$, simplifying the need for full time-series simulation to predict future behavior.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate examples comparing one-cycle and multi-cycle dynamics, tracking activity levels and quantifying the number of 'fresh' versus 'ghost' excitatory neurons over time.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical model (SLN with synaptic memory), defines the core problem (predicting regeneration from silence), develops the diagnostic tool (LER capacity predictor), presents results showing its accuracy, and concludes by discussing implications for understanding short-term memory encoding.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Transient synaptic memory has emerged as a potential mechanism for maintaining short-term information even in the absence of persistent neuronal activity. However, it remains unclear whether the hidden synaptic state alone contains sufficient information to predict the future evolution of neuronal networks after activity has ceased. Here, we introduce a minimal neuronal network model with finite-lifetime synapses and investigate the mechanism underlying spontaneous activity regeneration following complete neuronal silence. We show that the residual synaptic configuration at the first silent state already determines whether network activity terminates after a single activation cycle or spontaneously regenerates an additional cycle. By analyzing this synaptic-memory snapshot, we identify the Latent Excitatory Recruitment (LER) capacity, quantified by the cumulative number of fresh excitatory neurons, as a near-perfect predictor of multi-cycle dynamics without continuing the subsequent network simulation. Remarkably, these distinct dynamical outcomes emerge in an otherwise homogeneous neuronal network, demonstrating that transient synaptic memory alone is sufficient to generate diverse future dynamics. Our findings provide a mechanistic explanation for activity regeneration from a residual synaptic state and suggest that short-term memory is encoded not only in ongoing neuronal activity but also in the latent synaptic configuration that preserves the network's capacity to recruit new neuronal assemblies. More broadly, the proposed snapshot-based framework offers a new perspective for predicting and potentially controlling the future evolution of neuronal networks.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper derives a general, necessary, and sufficient statistical condition for any system to exhibit 1/f noise. By analyzing various physical models, including diffusion equations and electrical circuits, the authors conclude that this ubiquitous noise requires its dynamics to be governed by exactly two separated characteristic timescales. This provides a powerful, universal diagnostic tool for understanding complex relaxation phenomena.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical noise sources, the mathematical framework involving spectral density analysis and the identification of universal scaling laws (like $1/f$) is highly relevant to characterizing non-equilibrium dynamics in open quantum systems or complex many-body relaxation processes.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to determine a necessary and sufficient condition for the occurrence of ubiquitous 1/f noise, which has an uncertain underlying physics.

**Main result.** A stationary 1/f noise requires the system's dynamics to be characterized by exactly two separated constant timescales ($f_{	ext{low}}$ and $f_{	ext{high}}$), with no other constant parameters in between.

**Method.** The analysis uses frequency domain techniques (Fourier transforms) applied to models like diffusion equations and Ornstein-Uhlenbeck processes, relating spectral intensity to the system's correlation functions.

**Model / system.** The study analyzes several models, including a one-dimensional diffusion equation for a quantity $q(x, t)$ and an electrical circuit modeled by the telegraph equation. The core mathematical tool is analyzing how white noise input translates into colored noise output.

**Key observables.** Spectral intensity of the noise current or flux ($I(\omega)$), specifically showing $\propto 1/\omega$ scaling for $1/f$ noise.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Two separated constant frequencies ($	ext{flow} \ll f_{	ext{high}}$) and two characteristic timescales ($	au_{	ext{small}}, 	au_{	ext{large}}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the noise is stationary. In some derivations, approximations like neglecting shunt conductance or taking limits at infinite frequency are employed.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate spectral intensities for diffusion setups and electrical circuits, showing transitions to $1/\omega$ scaling as separation between timescales increases.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the condition through multiple physical examples (diffusion, electrical circuit) before stating a general statistical criterion based on the existence of two separated time scales ($	au_{	ext{small}}, 	au_{	ext{large}}$).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

While $1/f$ noise is ubiquitous and has been found in various systems, its physics remains uncertain. From an analytical study of an ordinary diffusion equation, we find an additional example of the $1/f$ noise. The formula for this example, together with existing knowledge about scaling in fluid turbulence, implies a necessary and sufficient condition for the occurrence of any stationary $1/f$ noise. That is, the noise needs to be characterized by two constant frequencies of $f_{\rm low} \ll f_{\rm high}$. For a frequency range from $f = f_{\rm low}$ to $f_{\rm high}$, it is further needed that, except for the mean amplitude of the noise, there is no other constant parameter. Then, at $f_{\rm low} \ll f \ll f_{\rm high}$, the noise scales asymptotically as $1/f$. Being statistical and simple, our condition applies to any system and hence explains the ubiquity of the $1/f$ noise. It is also applicable to some systems with noise of $α\ne 1.0$ for $1/f^α$, via intermittency analogous to that of the turbulence.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes a nonlinear voter model where social interactions occur on a spatially constrained network. By incorporating rules that delete links when rewiring fails, the authors show that global consensus is fragile. Instead, the system settles into fragmented absorbing states whose structure depends critically on the interplay between local geometry and interaction parameters.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical opinion dynamics, the framework of non-equilibrium phase transitions, absorbing states, and structural changes due to local constraints (like link deletion) mirrors concepts in quantum many-body systems undergoing localization or fragmentation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates opinion formation dynamics in a spatially constrained coevolving nonlinear voter model, focusing on how local geometric constraints alter the nature of absorbing states.

**Main result.** Local geometric accessibility fundamentally reshapes the structure of absorbing states, leading to distinct regimes characterized by reduced consensus regions and spatially segregated fragmentation rather than robust global consensus.

**Method.** The authors employ extensive numerical simulations across parameter space $(p, q)$ on a Random Geometric Graph (RGG), utilizing techniques like finite-size scaling and route closure equations to characterize absorption processes.

**Model / system.** The model is a Nonlinear Voter Model implemented on an RGG embedded in 2D space. Dynamics involve state flips and rewiring attempts, with the crucial modification being link deletion when local rewiring fails due to spatial constraints.

**Key observables.** Magnetization ($\langle|m|angle$), active link density ($\langlehoangle$), largest connected component fraction ($\langle S_{max}/Nangle$), mean degree ($\langle kangle$), and the spatial enrichment ($\Delta sp$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The interaction probability ($p$) and the rewiring/coevolution parameter ($q$); system size ($N$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that deviations from standard CNVM due to link deletion are key; finite-size effects are noted when interpreting consensus regimes.

**Figures summary.** Figures map out empirical regimes (P1-P5) based on multiple observables, showing size dependence of magnetization loss (Fig. 2), and distinguishing between topological vs. real-space fragmentation using spatial enrichment ($\Delta sp$) (Fig. 3).

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the model by first defining the constrained dynamics, then mapping out empirical regimes via multiple observables, developing phenomenological descriptions for mean degree evolution, and finally applying advanced techniques like finite-time scaling to characterize absorption boundaries.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate a spatially constrained coevolving nonlinear voter model. Using a random geometric graph, we constrain the interaction range of voter dynamics. If local rewiring is not possible, the discordant link is deleted. Our results reveal absorbing states that differ not only in magnetization and activity, but also in mean degree and spatial state organization. By exploring dynamical and structural observables, we found that distinct regimes from the existing coevolving nonlinear voter model are characterized by a reduced consensus region, spatially segregated fragmentation, and isolated node formation. Additionally, we develop a phenomenological description of the evolution of the mean degree and terminal non-conserved quantities that is consistent with the numerical results. Our model highlights how local geometric accessibility reshapes the structure of the absorbing states.

</details>

**Summary.** This work develops a theoretical model for circadian rhythm synchronization in biological oscillators that incorporate intrinsic noise. It establishes that the transition to robust, synchronized rhythms is governed solely by the single-oscillator coherence ($R_1$). The findings provide a powerful framework for understanding how molecular parameters can be tuned to maintain collective order despite underlying stochasticity.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on biology, the mathematical framework—analyzing phase transitions in coupled stochastic systems based on a single-particle metric ($R_1$)—is highly relevant to non-equilibrium statistical mechanics and open quantum systems exhibiting collective behavior.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research aims to understand how intrinsic stochastic noise affects synchronization in biological oscillators, particularly developing a tunable mechanism for achieving robust collective rhythms.

**Main result.** Synchronization is shown to depend only on the single-oscillator coherence ($R_1$), leading to a phase transition predicted at $R_1 \gtrsim 1.96$, independent of specific biochemical rates.

**Method.** The study uses stochastic modeling (master equation) and analyzes both mesoscopic and macroscopic limits, characterizing dynamics via coherence measures and scaling laws around the critical point.

**Model / system.** The system models the KaiABC circadian clock in cyanobacteria, focusing on the phosphorylation of KaiC proteins. The dynamics are governed by rate equations incorporating coupling strength derived from KaiA sequestration.

**Key observables.** Single-oscillator coherence ($R_1$), population-level coherence ($R_N$), and timing error (coefficient of variation of the period $T$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Topological parameter ($ho$), thermodynamic force ($\mu$, related to ATP/ADP ratio), and coupling strength ($c_{seq}A$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on modeling stochastic transitions, using a diffusion approximation for large N, and assuming instantaneous KaiA sequestration upon entering specific state regions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the single-molecule phase space, population synchronization dynamics showing scaling laws above/below the transition, and how experimental modifications tune parameters leading to changes in collective behavior.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops a novel mechanism linking synchronization to $R_1$, analyzes the resulting phase transition using deterministic limits, examines mesoscopic vs. macroscopic scaling properties, and discusses compensatory mechanisms that restore coherence after disruption.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

While synchronization has been well-studied in deterministic oscillators, most underlying oscillators are stochastic in both natural and man-made systems. Yet, the effects of intrinsic stochasticity remain poorly understood. Here, we develop a new mechanism for synchronizing circadian KaiC molecules that have topologically protected cycles. We find a phase transition to synchronization that depends only on the single-oscillator coherence, across a range of molecular changes that determine this coherence. Examining both mesoscopic and macroscopic numbers relevant for cellular and in vitro conditions respectively, we find different scaling properties above and below the phase transition. Our results shed light on several existing experiments and further predict that external changes can be offset by compensatory changes that improve the single-oscillator coherence - demonstrating a tunable pathway between stochastic single oscillators and their robust collective rhythms.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops a novel theoretical framework by applying Loewner theory to derive a nonlinear Generalized Langevin Equation. It reformulates the Fluctuation-Dissipation Relation using conformal mappings, showing that the resulting system exhibits a characteristic $t^{-1}$ energy dissipation scaling law. This connects complex analysis tools to fundamental problems in non-equilibrium statistical mechanics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work connects advanced complex analysis (Loewner evolution) directly to non-equilibrium statistical mechanics concepts like FDRs and energy dissipation, providing a novel mathematical framework for open quantum systems dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to derive a nonlinear generalized Langevin equation (GLE) and analyze the role of entropy in colored noise environments using advanced mathematical tools.

**Main result.** The Loewner-theoretic approach reformulates the GLE derivation, leading to specific forms for the Fluctuation-Dissipation Relation (FDR) that predict an energy dissipation scaling law $R \sim t^{-1}$.

**Method.** It employs a decomposition method based on conformal transformations governed by the chordal Loewner equation and modifies the Mori-Zwanzig formalism.

**Model / system.** The model is a one-dimensional nonlinear GLE, applied conceptually to processes like cell migration, which involves analyzing stochastic dynamics in colored noise environments.

**Key observables.** Response function $R(t, t+h)$, energy dissipation scaling law ($R \sim t^{-1}$), and Loewner entropy ($S_{Loew}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The memory kernel structure and the nature of the colored noise environment are central.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key assumptions include approximating the autocorrelation function using backward Loewner evolution properties and assuming the system's nonlinear response is forced in a conformal field.

**Figures summary.** Figures show time-dependent behavior and log-log plots confirming the predicted $t^{-1}$ scaling for two different forms of the response function derived from the theory.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the GLE via Loewner theory, deriving multiple FDR expressions (Kubo vs. Loewner), analyzing the role of Loewner entropy in these relations, and finally confirming the predicted $t^{-1}$ scaling through numerical simulations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this study, the formal derivation of a one-dimensional nonlinear generalized Langevin equation is demonstrated using a decomposition method based on the conformal transformation governed by the chordal Loewner equation. Here, we used a modified Mori-Zwanzig method whose operator is substituted by that is derived from the discrete Loewner evolution. By this approach, the different types of fluctuation-dissipation relation (FDR) were reformulated using mathematical terms affected by the conformal maps. Dealing with a memory kernel that models the cell migration experiment, the numerical simulation was performed to obtain the specific scaling law of energy dissipation that is common among the two obtained types of FDRs. In addition, the concept of Loewner entropy is used for the estimation of the canonical ensemble in the colored noise environment throughout the theoretical analyses.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper explores how basis-independent coherence survives environmental decoherence in three-qubit systems under pure dephasing. It finds that non-Markovian effects provide superior protection compared to Markovian ones, and collective coupling enhances robustness by utilizing decoherence-free subspaces. The findings establish basis-independent measures as a more reliable indicator of intrinsic quantum resource preservation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the robustness of intrinsic quantum resources (coherence) against realistic noise models, which is central to designing fault-tolerant quantum technologies.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how basis-independent quantum coherence dynamics evolve for tripartite states when subjected to pure dephasing environments.

**Main result.** Non-Markovian environments show remarkable robustness, leading to nearly frozen intrinsic coherence, while collective dephasing enhances preservation compared to local dephasing.

**Method.** The analysis uses the basis-independent quantum coherence measure (based on Jensen-Shannon divergence) applied to master equations describing open quantum system evolution.

**Model / system.** The model considers tripartite pure and mixed states subjected to two types of pure dephasing: local (uncorrelated environmental fluctuations) or common/collective (correlated environmental fluctuations).

**Key observables.** Basis-independent quantum coherence, decoherence dynamics.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Markovian vs. non-Markovian regimes; Local vs. Common dephasing coupling strengths ($\gamma_i(t)$ and $\alpha(t)$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to pure dephasing environments, though the framework can be extended to other noise models.

**Figures summary.** Figures schematically illustrate local vs. common dephasing mechanisms; subsequent figures show coherence decay dynamics for mixed states under various environmental regimes (Markovian/non-Markovian).

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically compares basis-independent versus basis-dependent coherence measures, analyzing the time evolution of specific tripartite states ($|GHZangle$, $|Wangle$, etc.) across local and common dephasing channels in both Markovian and non-Markovian limits.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum coherence is a fundamental quantum resource whose preservation under environmental interactions is essential for quantum information processing. While most studies have focused on basis-dependent coherence measures, the dynamics of intrinsic coherence quantified by basis-independent measures remain largely unexplored. In this work, we investigate the dynamics of basis-independent quantum coherence for several representative tripartite pure and mixed states subjected to local and common dephasing environments in both Markovian and non-Markovian regimes. We show that Markovian local dephasing leads to state-dependent coherence degradation, whereas collective dephasing significantly enhances coherence preservation through decoherence-free sectors. More importantly, non-Markovian environments give rise to nearly frozen coherence dynamics for both pure and mixed states, demonstrating the remarkable robustness of intrinsic coherence against dephasing environment. A comparison with the measure of relative entropy of coherence reveals that basis-independent coherence measure exhibits substantially greater resilience and qualitatively different dynamical behaviour than its basis-dependent counterpart. These results provide new insights into the preservation of intrinsic multipartite coherence in open quantum systems and highlight basis-independent coherence as a robust quantum resource for realistic noisy quantum technologies.

</details>
