**Summary.** This paper develops an advanced theoretical method, Dynamical Formulation of Stationary Scattering (DFSS), to find exact analytical solutions for light scattering from diffraction gratings. By mapping the wave problem onto a non-Hermitian Hamiltonian evolution, it successfully calculates scattering amplitudes for both TE and TM waves beyond the limitations of the standard paraxial approximation. This provides crucial tools for designing and analyzing complex optical structures.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful theoretical framework (DFSS) to solve complex wave scattering problems that are typically intractable or limited by approximations like the paraxial regime. The extension to magnetic gratings is particularly valuable for quantum optics applications.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To determine exactly solvable scattering problems for both TE and TM waves interacting with diffraction gratings, extending beyond previous approximations.

**Main result.** The authors establish that the scattering problem can be solved analytically by mapping it to quantum dynamics via a fundamental transfer matrix derived from an effective non-Hermitian Hamiltonian. This allows for explicit analytic expressions for diffracted beam amplitudes valid beyond the paraxial approximation.

**Method.** The core method is the Dynamical Formulation of Stationary Scattering (DFSS), which maps the wave scattering problem onto determining the time-evolution operator associated with a non-Hermitian effective Hamiltonian.

**Model / system.** The system is an optical diffraction grating, modeled by spatially varying relative permittivity ($\hat{\varepsilon}$) and permeability ($\hat{\mu}$). The analysis covers generalized gratings that can be potentially magnetic, unlike previous studies limited to nonmagnetic cases.

**Key observables.** TE and TM wave scattering amplitudes and diffracted beam intensities (e.g., reflection $	au_{0\pm}^2$ and transmission $|	au_{1\pm}|^2$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Incident wavenumber ($k$), incidence angle ($	heta_0$), grating parameters ($\ell, a_0, \zeta, \kappa, K$). The analysis is applicable across various regimes including Bragg and Raman-Nath.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method utilizes the DFSS framework, which is stated to be valid beyond the paraxial approximation. Some specific calculations rely on approximations valid when incidence angles are near grazing or normal incidence ($\cos 	heta \approx 0$ or $\cos 	heta_0 \approx 0$).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the dependence of squared reflection and transmission coefficients ($|	au_{m\pm}|^2$) on the incident angle ($	heta_0$), showing behavior at grazing angles and the existence of isolated zeros in reflection.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory by first establishing the governing wave equation and relating it to a transfer matrix $\mathbf{x}_M$. It then derives this matrix using an exponential form involving the effective Hamiltonian, culminating in explicit analytic expressions for scattering amplitudes applicable to both TE and TM waves.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In J.~Phys.~A {\bf 31}, 3493 (1998), Berry provides an analytic solution of the problem of determining the diffracted beam intensities for atoms incident upon a grating given by the potential, $v(x,y):=iV_0(e^{i\mathfrak{K}\,y}-1)$ for $0\leq x\leq\ell$ and $v(x,y):=0$ otherwise, where $V_0, \mathfrak{K}$, and $\ell$ are positive real parameters. This result, which relies on the paraxial approximation, readily applies to the diffraction of transverse electric (TE) waves by the nonmagnetic optical grating given by the relative permittivity, $\hat\varepsilon(x,y):=1-v(x,y)/k^2$, where $k$ is the incident wavenumber. We show that Berry's grating belongs to a larger class of possibly magnetic diffraction gratings whose scattering problem for both TE and transverse magnetic (TM) waves are exactly solvable. Our analysis makes use of a recently developed dynamical formulation of stationary scattering that is based on the idea of mapping the scattering problem to the quantum dynamics generated by an effective non-Hermitian Hamiltonian operator. {We use this formulation to derive explicit analytic expressions for the diffracted beam amplitudes that are valid beyond paraxial approximation.} As a concrete example, we offer the exact solution of the scattering problem for TE and TM waves incident upon a generalization of Berry's grating.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper analyzes entanglement harvesting from detectors coupled to the energy density of a scalar field, focusing on resolving persistent ultraviolet divergences. By employing distributional methods and analyzing switching correlations, the authors demonstrate conditions under which the calculated entanglement measure remains finite. This advances the theoretical understanding of quantum information extraction in curved or interacting quantum fields.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous mathematical tools for handling divergences in quantum field theory calculations relevant to open quantum systems, which is crucial for modeling realistic detector interactions.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study addresses persistent ultraviolet (UV) divergences encountered when calculating entanglement harvesting from particle detectors coupled to the renormalized energy density of a massless scalar field.

**Main result.** The analysis shows that these UV divergences are controlled by the switching correlation at coincident interaction times, and pointlike detectors remain finite if their switchings do not overlap.

**Method.** The authors employ distributional methods in position space, utilizing Wick's theorem for Gaussian states to calculate reduced detector states perturbatively up to $O(\lambda^2)$.

**Model / system.** The model involves two Unruh-DeWitt detectors coupled via an interaction Hamiltonian density proportional to the renormalized energy-momentum tensor of a massless scalar field in Minkowski spacetime.

**Key observables.** Negativity ($N$), which quantifies entanglement, and correlation terms derived from the energy density ($\mathcal{E}_\phi$) and two-point functions ($\Xi_\phi$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Detector coupling strength ($\lambda$), Gaussian widths ($\sigma$), detector separation ($d$), and field parameters like temperature or vacuum state type.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The initial field state $\hat{ho}_\phi$ is assumed to be a zero-mean Gaussian state, and the analysis often requires assuming non-overlapping supports for switching functions to guarantee finite results.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates Negativity versus detector gap ($\Omega$) for various switching delays ($\delta t$), showing an optimal entanglement range before it vanishes.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by setting up the interaction Hamiltonian, calculating the reduced density matrix perturbatively, and then systematically analyzing the resulting correlation terms using distributional calculus to isolate and control UV divergences.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study entanglement harvesting with particle detectors coupled to the renormalized energy density of a massless scalar field. Our analysis identifies the distributional mechanism underlying the persistent ultraviolet divergences previously observed for quadratic detector couplings and discusses that the energy density coupling exhibits the same underlying structure. We show that these divergences are entirely controlled by the switching correlation at coincident interaction times. Consequently, pointlike detectors are UV finite whenever the switchings do not overlap. For Gaussian-smeared detectors, the harvesting correlations are automatically finite in 1+1 and 2+1 dimensions, while in higher dimensions the remaining divergences are removed by non-overlapping switchings. Finally, we derive general expressions for arbitrary zero-mean Gaussian states and illustrate the formalism with a thermal field state.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents $	exttt{iNORG}$, an open-source solver package utilizing the Natural Orbitals Renormalization Group (NORG) method for Dynamical Mean-Field Theory. It efficiently solves strongly correlated electron models by intelligently compressing the computational Hilbert space using natural orbitals. This advancement provides a powerful, accurate tool for studying complex materials physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter, the underlying mathematical machinery—basis transformation via optimal single-particle density matrices and iterative subspace projection (Krylov/Lanczos)—is highly relevant to open quantum systems where system-bath coupling requires efficient representation of reduced dynamics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central challenge is developing efficient and robust quantum impurity solvers for Dynamical Mean-Field Theory (DMFT) calculations applied to strongly correlated electron systems.

**Main result.** The authors introduce $	exttt{iNORG}$, an open-source package implementing the Natural Orbitals Renormalization Group (NORG) method, which achieves high accuracy with reduced computational cost by optimizing the bath representation.

**Method.** The core methodology is NORG, which iteratively optimizes a natural orbital basis derived from the Single-Particle Density Matrix to compress the Hilbert space by freezing inactive degrees of freedom. This is combined with a Krylov-subspace approach for efficient Green's function calculation.

**Model / system.** The package is designed for solving strongly correlated electron systems, specifically modeling the Anderson Impurity Model (AIM) and multi-orbital Bethe lattice models using Hamiltonians involving local interactions ($U$) coupled to a non-interacting bath.

**Key observables.** Natural orbital occupation numbers ($n_k$), Green's functions $G(\omega)$, and ground state properties of the impurity model.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Coulomb repulsion ($U$, $U'$), chemical potential ($\mu$), hybridization function parameters, and temperature (currently limited to zero-temperature calculations).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that freezing inactive orbitals introduces an error proportional to the occupation number deviation from 0 or 1; current limitations restrict use to single-site models and zero temperature.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the necessity of efficient solvers for DMFT. It details the NORG method, explaining how natural orbitals are used to compress the Hilbert space. The implementation leverages a Krylov-subspace approach for Green's function calculation, which is then demonstrated using examples on AIM and Bethe lattice models.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In the context of dynamical mean-field theory (DMFT) calculations for strongly correlated electron systems, quantum impurity solvers play a central computational role in treating correlated lattice models and realistic materials. Consequently, developing efficient and robust quantum impurity solvers remains a key challenge. In this paper, we present an open-source quantum impurity solver package based on the natural orbitals renormalization group (NORG) method, dubbed $\texttt{iNORG}$. This software delivers high accuracy with reduced computational cost by optimizing the bath representation using natural orbitals and incorporating advanced features such as efficient Hilbert space selection and efficient algorithms for computing Green's functions. We first introduce the basic principle of the NORG method and then discuss the implementation details. The software framework, major features, and installation procedure for $\texttt{iNORG}$ are explained as well. Finally, several simple examples are presented to demonstrate the usage of $\texttt{iNORG}$.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper investigates the Brownian Castle (BC) interface growth model, finding it belongs to a new universality class distinct from standard models like Ballistic Deposition. Through extensive simulations, the authors determine specific scaling exponents and show that the height fluctuations follow non-Gaussian statistics characterized by Cauchy-Lorentz tails. This work advances the understanding of complex, driven interfaces in statistical physics.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical interface growth, the analysis of non-Gaussian statistics (Cauchy-Lorentz tails) and deviations from standard scaling laws provides insights into complex, non-equilibrium dynamics relevant to open quantum systems or driven many-body processes where simple thermal assumptions fail.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study characterizes a new universality class associated with interface growth dynamics by analyzing modified Family-Vicsek scaling properties and probability distributions.

**Main result.** The Brownian Castle (BC) interface exhibits modified scaling exponents ($eta \approx 0.47$, $\alpha \approx 1.01$) and shows that the height change distribution follows a Cauchy-Lorentz form, suggesting large jumps.

**Method.** Numerical simulations are used to extract growth and roughness exponents via fitting double logarithmic plots, complemented by data collapse analysis and statistical testing of probability distributions.

**Model / system.** The system is the Brownian Castle (BC) interface growth model, a variation on Ballistic Deposition that allows for material removal. It is compared against established models like BD, which falls into the KPZ universality class.

**Key observables.** Interface width $w(L, t)$, growth exponent ($eta$), roughness exponent ($\alpha$), and probability distributions of height changes ($p(dh)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** System size ($L$) up to 512 sites, time steps up to $10^6$, and the derived scaling exponents $eta=0.47 \pm 0.01$ and $\alpha=1.01 \pm 0.01$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The standard scaling relationship $z = \alpha/eta$ may break down due to finite size effects, which is noted when comparing derived exponents to the exponent observed during data collapse ($z=3$).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate schematic growth rules for BD vs. BC, show interface width evolution over time and system size (determining $eta$ and $\alpha$), and demonstrate near-perfect data collapse using a specific scaling function.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the BC model variation, presents numerical simulations to calculate scaling exponents ($\alpha, eta$) via fitting, analyzes data collapse for consistency checks, and finally examines probability distributions (heights and height changes) to characterize the underlying stochastic process.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Brownian Castle is a new interface growth model that is a variation on the well-known ballistic deposition model that results in an entirely new universality class. We present numerical verification that the interface width for BC interfaces displays modified Family-Vicsek scaling properties up to finite size corrections. Specifically, we find a growth exponent of $β=0.470 \pm 0.012$ and a roughness exponent of $α=1.01 \pm 0.018$. The scaling is modified at short times with a size scaling exponent that described the early time dependence of interface width on length. The probability distribution of heights for the BC interface shows significant deviations from simple Gaussian behavior and the probability distribution of height changes shows a Cauchy-Lorentz form consistent with expectations for a process involving relatively large jumps.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper models how high-energy phonons, which can damage superconducting qubits, are suppressed through a process called down-conversion within an adjacent normal metal film. By solving coupled kinetic equations for electron and phonon populations, the authors determine that this mitigation mechanism is most effective when the normal metal layer has a thickness near one micron.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses energy dissipation mechanisms in superconducting circuits, which is crucial for maintaining quantum coherence. The detailed kinetic modeling of phonon relaxation provides insights into open quantum system dynamics driven by the environment (phonons).

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is mitigating quasiparticle poisoning in superconducting devices caused by pair-breaking phonons.

**Main result.** Phonon down-conversion efficiency, which reduces the damaging high-energy phonons, is found to be nearly optimal over a finite range of normal metal thicknesses, typically around one micron.

**Method.** The study employs kinetic equation modeling solved in the steady state to track phonon and electron distributions. Analytical and numerical solutions are used to calculate the down-conversion efficiency based on material parameters.

**Model / system.** The system involves a superconducting device coupled via a normal metal film to a substrate, where high-energy phonons scatter off electrons, transferring energy and lowering the phonon energy below the pair-breaking threshold.

**Key observables.** Down-conversion efficiency ($R_{qp}$ or $R_\lambda$), electron distribution function ($f(E)$), and phonon distribution functions ($n(\Omega)$, $n_s(\Omega)$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Material parameters like electron-phonon interaction strength, phonon transmission coefficients ($	au_{sm}, 	au_{ms}$), film thickness ($d_m$), and substrate thickness ($d_s$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** Approximations include neglecting spatial gradients, assuming particle–hole symmetry for electrons, using the Debye model for phonons (ignoring anharmonic effects), and neglecting electron-electron scattering.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the physical setup (superconducting qubit chip with normal metal islands) and show the steady-state evolution of electron and phonon distributions under various injection conditions.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops a kinetic equation model for coupled phonon/electron dynamics, solves for the steady state using limiting cases (no-metal, perfect absorption), derives explicit formulas for down-conversion efficiency via asymptotic analysis, and discusses parameter dependence on geometry.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Thanks to low dissipation, superconducting devices are promising for a number of applications, such as detectors and implementations of quantum computation. However, their working can be adversely impacted by quasiparticles, which is why so-called quasiparticle poisoning mechanisms and their mitigation are under intense investigation. Here we focus on one poisoning mechanism, namely pair-breaking phonons, and its mitigation through down-conversion by a normal-metal film - the process in which scattering of high-energy phonons by electrons lowers the energy of the former below the pair-breaking threshold. To study the down-conversion, we introduce a model based on kinetic equations, which we solve both analytically (approximately) and numerically in the steady state. We use the solution the estimate a properly-defined down-conversion efficiency which depends on material parameters (such as the strength of electron-phonon interaction and the phonon transmission coefficient at interfaces) and film and substrate thicknesses. Interestingly, we find that the efficiency is nearly optimal over a finite range of metal thicknesses, with the minimum near-optimal thickness being typically of the order of a micron.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper develops an exact theoretical framework for analyzing how multiple active agents interact when their movement is dictated by a shared, memory-retaining 'trail field.' It derives precise formulas for collective first-passage statistics, notably proving that under saturating interaction conditions, the order of exploration does not affect the final absorption probabilities.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical random walks, the mathematical techniques used—analyzing non-equilibrium dynamics governed by environmental memory and deriving exact scaling laws—are highly relevant to open quantum systems where environment coupling leads to persistent correlations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study characterizes collective first-passage statistics for multiple interacting random walkers whose movement is influenced by a shared, history-dependent environmental 'trail field.'

**Main result.** For saturating interactions (SATW$\phi$), the splitting probabilities are exactly identical whether the walkers start simultaneously or sequentially, establishing an exact framework for such memory effects.

**Method.** The analysis involves deriving exact analytical expressions by representing the collective trail field using squared Bessel processes and generalizing Ray-Knight theorems to $N$ particles.

**Model / system.** The model consists of N one-dimensional random walkers (SIRWs) whose jump probabilities depend on a shared local time profile, distinguishing between saturating ($	ext{SATW}_\phi$) and non-saturating interaction regimes.

**Key observables.** Persistence exponents ($	heta_{k,N}$) and splitting probabilities ($P_{+k|x_0}$), which quantify the probability of $k$ walkers reaching one boundary before another.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of walkers ($N$), the interaction strength parameter ($\phi$), and the dimensionality ($d=1$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The rigorous analytical results are established for the saturating class ($	ext{SATW}_\phi$); deviations occur in non-saturating cases.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate schematic dynamics, numerical verification of persistence exponents across different models, and comparisons showing that splitting probabilities match theory for SATW$\phi$ but show protocol dependence for TSAWs.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the physical problem to deriving exact expressions for observables like survival probabilities and splitting probabilities. It then demonstrates the key invariance property for SATW$\phi$ by relating sequential and simultaneous protocols via local time additivity, concluding with general scaling arguments for exponents.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Memory encoded in the environment mediates interactions between active agents, from trail-following organisms to synthetic active matter depositing persistent tracks. Although such memory is known to strongly affect transport, its consequences for collective first-passage phenomena remain largely unexplored. Here we study $N$ one-dimensional random walkers interacting through a shared trail field. We characterize the $k^{\rm th}$ (among $N$) arrival time at a fixed target, and the probability that exactly $k$ walkers in $[0,1]$ reach one boundary before the other. For the broad class of self-interacting walkers with a saturating response to the trail, we derive exact expressions for the corresponding persistence exponents and splitting probabilities. Strikingly, despite the strong history-dependent correlations generated by the common environment, splitting probabilities are exactly identical whether walkers explore simultaneously or one after another. This invariance breaks down for nonsaturating trail interactions. Our results follow from an exact representation of the collective trail field and establish a framework for first-passage phenomena in systems coupled through persistent environmental memory.

</details>

### [Quantum observables for probabilistic classical particles](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13937v1)

**Authors:** Christof Wetterich

**Summary.** This paper develops a novel framework to describe probabilistic classical particles using quantum mechanical formalism. It shows that robust 'quantum observables' can be defined for these systems, demonstrating how standard quantum mechanics (like the hydrogen atom spectrum) emerges as a specific subsystem of this larger statistical structure. This offers a theoretical bridge between classical probability theory and fundamental quantum principles.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a deep theoretical link between classical statistical mechanics and quantum theory by reformulating observables using non-commuting operators, which is highly relevant for understanding open quantum systems or measurement processes.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The classical observables of position and momentum are inadequate for describing particles in microphysical situations with substantial probability distribution dispersion.

**Main result.** The paper proposes a quantum formalism where statistical observables, represented by non-commuting operators, can describe probabilistic classical particles, showing how standard quantum mechanics emerges as a subsystem description.

**Method.** It uses a quantum formalism applied to the Liouville equation for classical statistics, introducing complex wave functions derived via Fourier transforms and analyzing coupled particle-mirror dynamics.

**Model / system.** The model involves probabilistic classical particles governed by the Liouville equation. The analysis is formalized using a complex Schrödinger equation describing a system composed of the original particle and its time-reversed 'mirror' counterpart.

**Key observables.** Quantum observables (statistical observables), expectation values of position ($\langle x angle$), momentum ($\langle p angle$), and conserved statistical quantities like $\langle H^2 angle$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis is general but demonstrates specific results for the harmonic potential and the Coulomb potential.

**Assumptions / limitations.** It assumes that a quantum system can emerge as a subsystem of a larger classical statistical framework, though it does not claim this applies to all physical reality.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the inadequacy of classical observables, then introducing the quantum formalism via the Liouville equation mapping, developing the coupled particle-mirror Hamiltonian, and finally demonstrating recovery of known quantum results (e.g., Hydrogen atom spectrum) in specific limits.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The classical observables of position and momentum are not well adapted to particles in a microphysical situation where typical probability distributions are characterized by a substantial dispersion. We propose the use of more robust quantum observables for probabilistic classical particles. The quantum observables are statistical observables which do not take fixed values for a given classical position and momentum. Solutions of the Liouville equation are discussed in the quantum formalism for classical statistics. Statistical observables are represented by non-commuting operators. No classical correlation function is defined for these observables and Bell's inequalities do not apply. We demonstrate for a general potential how a quantum system emerges from classical statistics. For the particular cases of a harmonic potential and a Coulomb potential we investigate subsystems which describe all features of a quantum particle. This covers the discrete energy spectrum of the hydrogen atom and quantum harmonic oscillator. We discuss the interference for the double-slit experiment. Conserved statistical observables may also be relevant for the probabilistic dynamics of dust or planets.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper details the advanced instrumentation capabilities of the F2 Sample Pre-selection and Characterization Station at SECUF. By integrating multiple extreme conditions—such as high pressure, low temperature, and strong magnetic fields—the facility allows for comprehensive characterization of novel quantum materials. The reported achievements underscore its role in discovering exotic states like high-Tc superconductivity under immense pressure.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on materials science instrumentation, the reported discoveries (superconductivity, quantum spin states) are central to strongly correlated electron physics, which often involves complex many-body interactions relevant to theoretical modeling.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To provide comprehensive sample synthesis, processing, pre-screening, and characterization services to prepare high-quality specimens for frontier research under extreme physical conditions.

**Main result.** The facility has enabled discoveries including novel quantum spin supersolid states, pressure-induced high-temperature superconductivity in nickelates, and giant anomalous Hall angles.

**Method.** A suite of advanced experimental techniques is utilized, combining electrical transport measurements (resistivity, heat capacity) with structural characterization (XRD, SEM) under extreme conditions like ultrahigh pressure and ultralow temperature.

**Model / system.** The research platform is the Synergetic Extreme Condition User Facility (SECUF), specifically detailing the F2 Sample Pre-selection and Characterization Station. This station integrates multiple physical controls: ultrahigh pressure, ultralow temperature, strong magnetic fields, and ultrafast optical fields.

**Key observables.** Resistivity, heat capacity, magnetic susceptibility, structural parameters (lattice constants, crystal structure), anomalous Hall angle, and superconducting transition signatures.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperatures down to $<10$ mK; Magnetic fields up to $\pm 16$ T; Pressures up to 70 GPa.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that the combination of extreme physical conditions can unlock novel quantum phases and material properties inaccessible under ambient conditions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the comprehensive instrumentation overview, including PPMS, MPMS, DR, NMR, SCXRD, XRD, SEM, Raman, and FT-IR systems, alongside details of instrument upgrades like high-pressure cells.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the specifications of ten core instrument systems within the F2 Station. It then highlights several breakthrough scientific achievements enabled by these combined capabilities, such as superconductivity in nickelates and anomalous Hall effects.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Synergetic Extreme Condition User Facility (SECUF) is a comprehensive, state-of-the-art user facility designed to provide integrated extreme physical conditions-including ultrahigh pressure, ultralow temperature, strong magnetic fields, and ultrafast optical fields-for frontier research in condensed matter physics and materials science. Within SECUF, the F2 Sample Pre-selection and Characterization Station plays a pivotal supporting role. Its mission is to provide comprehensive sample synthesis, processing, pre-screening, and characterization services to prepare high-quality specimens for subsequent experiments under extreme conditions. This paper details the specifications and performance of ten core instrument systems within these units. Furthermore, we highlight several breakthrough scientific achievements enabled by the F2 Station, encompassing the discovery of novel quantum spin supersolid states, pressure-induced high-temperature superconductivity in nickelates, giant anomalous Hall angles, and molecular water in lunar soil. We also outline ongoing technical developments that expand the station's capabilities, such as integrated high-pressure cells and self-built ancillary measurement systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This work uses THz spectroscopy on $	ext{Gd}_2	ext{Ru}_2	ext{O}_7$ to simultaneously measure magnetic and lattice responses. By modeling the coupled evolution of these two spectral features, the authors successfully quantify an internal magnetic field and reveal a subtle Gd-ordering transition previously hidden from bulk measurements.

**Why it may be interesting.** The technique provides a powerful, non-invasive way to probe coupled quantum phenomena (spin-lattice coupling) by separating and analyzing distinct energy scales within a single spectroscopic measurement.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to understand how magnetic order couples to lattice vibrations (spin-lattice coupling) in $	ext{Gd}_2	ext{Ru}_2	ext{O}_7$ and reveal an ordering transition that is difficult to detect using standard bulk thermodynamic measurements.

**Main result.** By simultaneously measuring magnetic excitations and optical phonons via THz spectroscopy, the authors quantified an internal exchange field ($B_{	ext{int}}$) consistent with cluster-multipolar order and provided independent spectroscopic evidence for a low-temperature Gd-ordering transition.

**Method.** Magneto-Terahertz Time-Domain Spectroscopy (THz-TDS) was employed to measure the complex optical conductivity ($\sigma(
u)$) across both magnetic and phonon frequency ranges as a function of temperature and external magnetic field.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the pyrochlore ruthenate $	ext{Gd}_2	ext{Ru}_2	ext{O}_7$, which contains localized $4f$ moments ($	ext{Gd}^{3+}$) coupled to itinerant $	ext{Ru}$ magnetism. The analysis models spectral features using uncoupled Lorentz oscillators superimposed on a quadratic background.

**Key observables.** Low-frequency magnetic resonance parameters ($\Omega_m, 
u_m, 	au_m$), optical phonon parameters ($\Omega_p, 
u_p, 	au_p$), and the integrated spectral weights ($W_m, W_p$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Temperatures ranging from $1.6	ext{ K}$ to $200	ext{ K}$, external magnetic fields up to $7	ext{ T}$, and derived internal exchange field values ($\approx 5-6 	ext{ T}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the spectral response in each region can be modeled by an uncoupled Lorentz oscillator on a smooth quadratic background, allowing for independent fitting of magnetic and phonon components.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the temperature and field dependence of resonance parameters ($\Omega, 
u, 	au$) for both magnetic and phonon modes, showing correlated anomalies at characteristic ordering temperatures. Integrated spectral weights ($W_m, W_p$) are plotted as functions of T and B.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes a unified framework by analyzing the temperature and field dependence of two distinct spectroscopic signals—the low-frequency magnetic resonance and the optical phonon mode—to correlate them with known magnetic transition points in $	ext{Gd}_2	ext{Ru}_2	ext{O}_7$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Magnetic order in materials combining localized rare-earth moments with itinerant transition-metal sublattices generates internal fields whose lattice imprint is rarely accessed directly. In Gd pyrochlore ruthenate, we find that a single terahertz spectrum resolves an exchange-split Gd$^{3+}$ mode and an optical phonon. Their coupled evolution quantifies the internal field, oriented as predicted for cluster-multipolar order, and reveals a Gd-ordering transition elusive to bulk thermodynamic probes, establishing a unified framework for accessing magnetic and lattice responses in correlated quantum materials.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically establishes that magnetic quadrupole (MQ) currents can be generated and accumulated in noncentrosymmetric antiferromagnets, providing a novel pathway for controlling magnetization. Using advanced group theory and multipole expansions, the authors prove that this effect is intrinsically linked to the breaking of spatial inversion symmetry. This work provides a crucial theoretical framework for designing MQ-based spintronic devices.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter magnetism, the rigorous application of group theory to classify emergent transport responses (like quadrupole currents) provides powerful theoretical tools applicable to understanding symmetry breaking in complex quantum materials relevant to AMO physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates magnetic quadrupole (MQ) current generation and accumulation in noncentrosymmetric antiferromagnets as a mechanism for controlling magnetization.

**Main result.** MQ currents are generically allowed in noncentrosymmetric systems because the relevant conductivity tensor vanishes in centrosymmetric materials. The authors establish a framework showing MQ accumulation at sample edges, analogous to the spin Hall effect.

**Method.** The analysis employs symmetry group theory and multipole representation techniques (Kubo formula) to classify the components of the MQ conductivity tensor ($\sigma_{n\alpha i;j}$).

**Model / system.** The focus is on noncentrosymmetric antiferromagnets, where the magnetic quadrupole moment constitutes the lowest-rank symmetry-allowed magnetic multipole for magnetization control.

**Key observables.** MQ conductivity tensor ($\sigma_{n\alpha i;j}$), MQ current ($J_{n\alpha i}$), and MQ accumulation near sample edges.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis depends critically on the crystal point group symmetry, specifically comparing centrosymmetric vs. noncentrosymmetric subgroups (e.g., $mmm$ to $mm2$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The study operates within the linear-response regime initially but extends to analyze non-equilibrium accumulation effects.

**Figures summary.** Notes mention figures illustrating the layer dependence of spin response in the mm2 phase and general references to tables summarizing multipole parities and conductivity components for various CPGs.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically establishes symmetry conditions for MQ current generation by constructing the multipole representation of the MQ conductivity tensor, demonstrating its necessity in parity-broken systems, and predicting edge accumulation effects.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Magnetization control via magnetic octupole injection has recently been proposed for a new class of centrosymmetric antiferromagnets, namely $d$-wave altermagnets, where the magnetic octupole is the lowest-rank magnetic multipole allowed by symmetry and serves as an alternative carrier to spin injection. In contrast, in noncentrosymmetric antiferromagnets, the magnetic quadrupole (MQ) constitutes the lowest-rank symmetry-allowed magnetic multipole, suggesting that MQ currents can provide an efficient route toward magnetization control through MQ injection. Here, we establish the symmetry conditions for MQ-current generation by constructing the multipole representation of the MQ conductivity tensor and show that MQ currents are generically allowed in noncentrosymmetric crystallographic point groups. As a representative example, we demonstrate MQ-current generation in the linear-response regime associated with symmetry lowering from the centrosymmetric point group ($mmm$) to its noncentrosymmetric subgroup ($mm2$). Furthermore, we reveal MQ accumulation near sample edges, analogous to spin accumulation induced by the spin Hall effect. This edge accumulation provides direct evidence of MQ-current generation and constitutes a key prerequisite for realizing MQ injection and MQ-based magnetization control in noncentrosymmetric antiferromagnets.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a novel two-stage inverse design technique to create 2D photonic networks that are inherently stealthy hyperuniform. By optimizing the network topology directly, the authors achieve isotropic complete photonic band gaps with unprecedented width and robustness across large ensembles of disordered structures.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on photonics, the methodology—using inverse design coupled with hyperuniformity constraints ($	ilde{\chi}_V(k)=0$) to engineer macroscopic properties in a disordered medium—is highly relevant for designing artificial quantum materials or metamaterials where disorder must be controlled precisely.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is achieving isotropic complete photonic band gaps (PBGs) in disordered 2D photonic networks that persist robustly as the system size approaches the thermodynamic limit.

**Main result.** The authors developed a novel two-stage inverse design procedure that successfully creates such networks, yielding PBG widths comparable to anisotropic crystals and demonstrating robustness across ensembles up to 100,000 vertices.

**Method.** They employ a two-stage inverse design optimization process targeting the stealthy hyperuniformity of the entire two-phase network structure directly, rather than just its progenitor point pattern.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled as a 2D photonic network composed of high-dielectric disks at vertices and thin rectangular walls along bonds. The analysis uses the Density of States (DoS) ensemble method to probe large-scale behavior.

**Key observables.** Complete Photonic Band Gap (PBG), Stealthy Hyperuniformity ($	ilde{\chi}_V(k)$), Gap-to-Midgap Ratio, and Defect State Density.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dielectric contrast of 13.0; target stealthy hyperuniformity values like $\chi_1(K_1) = 0.37$ and $\chi_2(K_2) = 0.24$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on the ensemble-based DoS method to approximate behavior in the thermodynamic limit, overcoming limitations of single-realization studies.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare the Density of States (DoS) for TE and TM modes using the new inverse design method against prior art, showing robust band gap suppression. Spectral density plots confirm the hyperuniform nature across large ensembles.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first identifying the limitations of previous methods based on point patterns, then introducing the two-stage optimization protocol (global topology followed by network refinement), and finally validating the results using ensemble DoS calculations to prove thermodynamic stability.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a two-stage inverse design procedure for producing disordered stealthy hyperuniform trivalent photonic networks in two dimensions with isotropic complete photonic band gaps (PBGs) blocking light regardless of direction or polarization (TE or TM) over a wide frequency range. Most ordinary disordered systems fail to maintain complete PBGs as system size increases. The only known exceptions that remain open in the largest simulations have been generated by mapping stealthy hyperuniform point patterns into trivalent networks. However, the resulting networks are not truly stealthy hyperuniform two-phase media. Although their PBGs remain open, they are relatively narrow due to limited overlap between the TE and TM band gaps and broad band tails caused by localized defect states. By contrast, our two-stage inverse design aims to make the final network itself stealthy hyperuniform, achieving unprecedented near-optimal overlap between the TE and TM band gaps and a small defect state density at the band edges. We obtain not only single realizations with large PBGs, but a striking homogeneity across a large ensemble, effectively probing a network with 100,000 vertices. This ensemble-based band gap is comparable in width to the complete PBG of an anisotropic honeycomb photonic crystal with the same network parameters and nearly an order of magnitude wider than the previously widest known isotropic complete PBGs. Our designs can be fabricated using additive manufacturing, offering new pathways to manipulate electromagnetic waves for photonic technologies.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically analyzes three-body quantum systems across the dimensional transition between Efimov and unatomic regimes. It finds that at the critical dimension, the system's momentum tail requires a unique logarithmic scaling structure governed by an additional three-body contact parameter. This work establishes the critical dimension as a key point for probing emergent scaling properties in few-body quantum gases.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a rigorous theoretical framework for understanding how fundamental many-body physics transitions between different scaling laws (log-periodic vs. power-law) in ultracold atomic systems, offering new experimental signatures based on momentum measurements.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how the momentum distribution and contact parameters of a mass-imbalanced three-body quantum system evolve as the spatial dimension is tuned across the critical point separating Efimov and unatomic regimes.

**Main result.** At the critical dimension, the asymptotic momentum tail exhibits a unique logarithmic scaling structure requiring an additional three-body contact parameter, which can lead to the suppression of the three-body contribution at finite momenta.

**Method.** The authors use theoretical methods involving solving Faddeev equations in an effective noninteger spatial dimension D and analyzing the large-momentum asymptotic behavior of the spectator function.

**Model / system.** The model is a mass-imbalanced three-body quantum system, analyzed by mapping its physics onto an effective continuous spatial dimension D, allowing for a dimensional crossover between different physical regimes.

**Key observables.** Single-particle momentum distribution (large-momentum tail), two-body contact parameter ($C_2$), and the newly introduced three-body contact parameter ($C''_3$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The critical dimension $D_c$, the mass imbalance ratio, and the intermediate scaling regime width $\Delta = D_c - D$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on mapping the system to an effective noninteger spatial dimension D and using asymptotic expansions for momentum space functions.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the scaling parameter $s_n$ vs. dimension D across Efimov, critical, and unatomic regimes; another figure quantifies the width of the intermediate scaling regime dependence on mass imbalance; a third shows the logarithmic asymptotic form at $D_c$.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the system's behavior in three distinct dimensional regimes (Efimov, Unatomic, Intermediate) by deriving the momentum distribution components and identifying the necessary contact parameters required to maintain physical consistency across the transition point.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We investigate the single-particle momentum distribution and contact parameters of mass-imbalanced three-body systems at the critical dimension Dc, where the transition between discrete and continuous scale invariance takes place as the spatial dimension is tuned between three and two dimensions. We show that the asymptotic momentum distribution at Dc is governed by a distinct logarithmic scaling structure, which differs fundamentally from both the log-periodic behavior of Efimov states and the power-law scaling of the unatomic regime. This structure requires the introduction of an additional three-body contact parameter associated with a quadratic logarithmic contribution, leading to a finite and well-defined description of the momentum tail at the transition. This additional three-body parameter depends sensitively on the mass imbalance, changing sign across different mass configurations and vanishing for identical particles. As a consequence, the three-body contribution to the momentum distribution can be suppressed at a characteristic momentum scale, leaving the asymptotic tail entirely determined by the two-body contact. We further analyze the narrow intermediate region connecting the Efimov and unatomic regimes, here identified as an intermediate scaling regime, whose extent and properties are strongly controlled by the mass ratio. These results establish the critical dimension as a regime with emergent scaling properties and provide experimentally accessible signatures for probing the transition between discrete and continuous scale invariance in few-body quantum systems.

</details>

### [Quantum memory advantage for quantum process tomography](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13476v1)

**Authors:** Carlos Bravo-Prieto, Weiyuan Gong, Antonio Anna Mele  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.13476v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `quantum measurements` **3/5** · `entanglement & information structure` **2/5** · `measurement-induced transitions` **2/5** · `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5**

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>
<div class="paper-figures-scroll">
<figure class="paper-figure-card">
<img src="2607.13476_figures/2607.13476_fig1.jpg" alt="Figure 1">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 1.</b> Figure 1. Overview of the proof strategy. We first construct a local family of channels near the completely depolarizing channel. Each channel is indexed by a matrix perturbation of the normalized Choi operator. We then represent every round of an adaptive incoherent protocol by a single-round tester. Along any transcript, the adaptivity protocol yields a deterministic sequence of tester elements. The key technical ingredient is a transcript-wise likelihood tilt bound over local Schatten neighborhoods, which yields posterior anti-concentration: after fewer than order d3 ind3 out/ε2 channel uses, the posterior cannot concentrate in the Choi trace ball that would be forced by any diamond-norm...</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously proves that utilizing quantum memory offers a significant computational advantage in Quantum Process Tomography (QPT). It establishes that protocols without memory require query complexity scaling as $d_{in}^3 d_{out}^3/\varepsilon^2$, while those with memory achieve a much better scaling of $d_{in}^2 d_{out}^2/\varepsilon^2$. This separation quantifies the necessity and power of quantum coherence in characterizing unknown quantum channels.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides fundamental resource quantification for quantum characterization tasks, directly impacting the feasibility limits of experimental quantum computing hardware.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central problem is Quantum Process Tomography (QPT): learning an unknown quantum channel from black-box access.

**Main result.** The paper establishes a rigorous separation between QPT protocols with and without quantum memory, showing that the required query complexity scales differently in both cases.

**Method.** The authors use information-theoretic techniques, constructing local families of channels to derive lower bounds on sample complexity, which are matched by non-adaptive upper bounds.

**Model / system.** The model involves an unknown quantum channel $\Phi: \mathcal{L}(A) 	o \mathcal{L}(B)$ acting between finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces $A$ and $B$. The comparison is drawn between protocols that use memory (coherent) versus those that reset after each measurement (incoherent).

**Key observables.** The estimation error is measured in the diamond norm ($\|\cdot\|_{\diamond}$), and the complexity is quantified by the required number of queries ($T$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dimensions $d_{\mathrm{in}}$ and $d_{\mathrm{out}}$, and the target accuracy $\varepsilon$. The scaling difference between memory-assisted ($\Theta(d_{in}^2 d_{out}^2/\varepsilon^2)$) and non-memory protocols ($\Theta(d_{in}^3 d_{out}^3/\varepsilon^2)$) is key.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes finite-dimensional Hilbert spaces and focuses on classical adaptivity for the incoherent case, while the coherent case utilizes quantum memory.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 provides an overview of the proof strategy, linking local channel families to single-round testers and culminating in a likelihood-ratio estimate leading to the lower bound $T = \Omega(d_{in}^3 d_{out}^3 / \epsilon^2)$.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops necessary mathematical tools (e.g., random-basis reconstruction, concentration inequalities) before applying them to establish a lower bound for adaptive incoherent protocols, which is then matched by an upper bound.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum process tomography, the task of learning an unknown quantum channel from black-box access, is a central problem in quantum information. In this setting, protocols with quantum memory can coherently store and jointly process quantum information obtained from multiple channel uses, whereas protocols without quantum memory must measure after each use and retain only a classical transcript of the measurement outcomes. A fundamental open question is whether quantum memory provides a query-complexity advantage even when protocols without quantum memory may adapt their experiments based on all previous outcomes with unbounded classical computational power. In this work, we show that it does. We determine the optimal query complexity of quantum process tomography without quantum memory up to a constant factor to be $Θ(d_{\mathrm{in}}^3 d_{\mathrm{out}}^3/\varepsilon^2)$, where $d_{\mathrm{in}}$ and $d_{\mathrm{out}}$ are the channel input and output dimensions, respectively, and $\varepsilon$ is the target diamond-norm accuracy. More precisely, we prove that any incoherent protocol for this task, including adaptive protocols, requires $Ω(d_{\mathrm{in}}^3 d_{\mathrm{out}}^3/\varepsilon^2)$ queries, even when each channel use may be assisted by arbitrary fresh ancilla, and we present a non-adaptive, ancilla-free incoherent protocol achieving the matching upper bound $O(d_{\mathrm{in}}^3 d_{\mathrm{out}}^3/\varepsilon^2)$. Our results thereby generalize the optimal sample-complexity bounds for single-copy state tomography, recovered as the special case $d_{\mathrm{in}}=1$. By contrast, coherent protocols with quantum memory achieve query complexity $Θ(d_{\mathrm{in}}^2 d_{\mathrm{out}}^2/\varepsilon^2)$. Hence, our results establish a rigorous learning separation between quantum process tomography with and without quantum memory.

</details>

**Authors:** Shingo Kukita, Kento Nawata, Yuichiro Matsuzaki  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.13422v1>

**Summary.** The paper tackles the problem of systematic error bias in Ramsey magnetometry caused by unknown frequency detuning. By designing and analyzing a specialized composite-pulse sequence, the authors successfully cancel this first-order detuning bias. This demonstrates a robust method to maintain high measurement precision close to the theoretical Standard Quantum Limit.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses systematic errors in quantum sensing protocols using coherent control techniques (composite pulses), which is highly relevant for improving precision measurements in AMO physics platforms like trapped ions or superconducting qubits.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to suppress systematic estimation bias in Ramsey magnetometry caused by an unknown detuning ($\delta$) between the actual and nominal spin frequencies.

**Main result.** A composite-pulse sequence was constructed that cancels the first-order detuning bias, allowing the measurement precision to approach the Standard Quantum Limit (SQL) even when $\delta$ is significant.

**Method.** The authors analyzed state evolution by expanding effects to first order in $\delta$ and designed specific pulse sequences whose Hamiltonian contributions cancel out the linear detuning error term.

**Model / system.** The system involves single-qubit Ramsey magnetometry of a DC magnetic field using spin probes. The dynamics are governed by an effective Hamiltonian that includes both the sensing field ($\Omega$) and the systematic detuning ($\delta$).

**Key observables.** Outcome probability ($P_0$), estimated frequency ($	ilde{\Omega}$), Mean-Square Error (MSE), and the bias term ($b$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Detuning ($\delta$), sensing field strength ($\Omega$), exposure time ($t$), and measurement count ($M$). The analysis focuses on the regime $\Omega t \ll 1$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on expanding effects to first order in $\delta$, and for some parts, it assumes depolarizing noise ($\gamma$) is known.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare single-pulse (error rising with detuning) versus composite-pulse protocols (error remaining flat), demonstrating the suppression of bias as detuning increases.

**Paper structure.** The paper first analyzes the standard single-pulse protocol, deriving the linear bias proportional to $\delta$. It then introduces and derives a composite-pulse sequence designed specifically to cancel this leading-order detuning contribution, restoring performance towards the SQL limit.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum sensing estimates a physical parameter encoded in the state of a probe; with independent spin probes the precision follows the standard quantum limit. Studies of sensing precision often assume that the parameters entering the model, such as the noise, are known. In practice these parameters are not always known, and a mismatch between the assumed and actual values induces a systematic error. Here we study single-qubit Ramsey magnetometry of a DC magnetic field under an unknown detuning between the actual and nominal spin frequencies: A first pulse puts the qubit into a superposition of its two states, the field to be sensed then adds a relative phase during an exposure stage, and a second pulse enables the readout. In our setting, the field acts only during the exposure stage, whereas the detuning acts throughout the whole protocol. We analyze how the detuning biases the estimate, preventing the total estimation error from following the standard quantum limit. We then construct a composite-pulse preparation and readout that exploits the difference in the intervals over which the field and the detuning act to cancel the detuning to first order. We evaluate the performance of this composite-pulse protocol and show that it suppresses the detuning-induced bias.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces the Measurement Plane, a modular, distributed framework designed to standardize and automate data collection across heterogeneous quantum networking testbeds. By separating user workflows from low-level hardware control into distinct software layers, it enables complex, multi-step quantum experiments remotely. This significantly reduces manual configuration effort while maintaining high experimental fidelity.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a crucial software infrastructure layer for realizing complex quantum network protocols, abstracting away device heterogeneity which is a major bottleneck in experimental quantum information science.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Quantum networking testbeds lack a distinct, standardized plane for coordinating distributed measurements and collecting experimental data across heterogeneous quantum devices.

**Main result.** The proposed Measurement Plane successfully coordinated remote nodes to execute complex experiments like coincidence measurement and polarization entanglement distribution with high visibility (up to 98%).

**Method.** A distributed framework using containerized microservices connected via publish-subscribe messaging was implemented, organizing functions into four distinct layers.

**Model / system.** The system is a software architecture designed for quantum networking testbeds, involving two remote nodes (Alice and Bob) connected by an optical network. It separates user workflows from device-specific control using capability abstraction.

**Key observables.** Visibility interference of up to 98% in polarization entanglement distribution; coincidence detection peaks at zero delay.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Two-node quantum networking setup; $\approx 1.4$ km optical fiber link.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The architecture assumes communication via a shared messaging infrastructure (NATS) and demonstrates feasibility rather than ultimate scale limits.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the layered architecture, the measurement lifecycle (Submission to Completion), the identifier hierarchy used for tracking, and experimental results showing coincidence histograms and polarization fringe visibility.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the Measurement Plane concept, details its multi-layered distributed microservice architecture (Application $ightarrow$ Coordination $ightarrow$ Capability $ightarrow$ Resource Agent), describes the identification scheme, and validates it through automated execution of remote quantum experiments.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum networking testbeds lack a distinct plane for coordinating distributed measurements and collecting experimental data across heterogeneous devices. To address this gap, we present the Measurement Plane, a dedicated plane that complements the data, control, and management planes rather than replacing or extending their pipelines. The contribution is presented as a distributed framework that organizes measurement functions into four layers: application, experiment coordination, capability, and resource agents. Our design separates user workflows from device-specific control.   We implemented the framework as containerized microservices connected through publish--subscribe messaging, and validated it on a two-node quantum networking setup connected by an optical network. The framework successfully coordinated remote nodes to execute coincidence measurement and polarization entanglement distribution experiments with visibility interference of up to 98 percent. This evaluation demonstrated the effectiveness of the framework for supporting complex, distributed quantum experiments, enabling online measurement and feedback, and significantly reducing manual configuration and execution effort.

</details>

**Summary.** This work theoretically characterizes NiS as a unique 3d altermagnetic semimetal where topology, magnetism, and lattice effects are intrinsically linked. By combining advanced DFT methods with transport theory, the authors predict large intrinsic spin Hall and anomalous Hall responses alongside giant magnetoresistance. The findings establish NiS as a promising material platform for realizing multiple quantum functionalities simultaneously.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter magnetism, the strong reliance on topological invariants (Berry curvature) and emergent quantum responses (AHC/SHC) connects deeply with modern theoretical approaches used in AMO physics for characterizing engineered quantum states.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Investigating the complex interplay between electronic topology, magnetism, and lattice dynamics in a specific magnetic semimetal.

**Main result.** NiS is identified as a model 3d platform exhibiting simultaneous carrier compensation, altermagnetic symmetry, large intrinsic spin Hall conductivity, and finite anomalous Hall response.

**Method.** The study combines first-principles DFT (LSDA+U+SOC), Berry-curvature analysis, semiclassical Boltzmann transport, and atomistic spin dynamics simulations.

**Model / system.** The physical system is hexagonal nickel sulfide (NiS), modeled as a carrier-compensated 3d altermagnetic semimetal with an A-type antiferromagnetic order. The electronic structure is analyzed using crystal field theory derived from the NiAs lattice symmetry.

**Key observables.** Intrinsic spin Hall conductivity ($\sigma^{\gamma}_{\alphaeta}$), anomalous Hall conductivity (AHC), nonsaturating magnetoresistance ($>10000\%$), and momentum-dependent spin splitting.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The magnetic transition temperature ($T_t \simeq 265	ext{K}$) and the magnitude of the spin splitting ($\sim 0.4$ to $1.0$ eV) are key parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on DFT calculations using the LSDA+U+SOC approach, assuming its validity for describing the magnetic ground state and electronic structure.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate band dispersions showing SOC-induced splitting (Fig. 1, 2), energy dependence of transport coefficients (SHC/AHC in Fig. 3), and the calculation of magnetic exchange interactions ($J_1, J_2, J_3$ in Fig. 5).

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by establishing the electronic structure via DFT, analyzing symmetry constraints leading to Dirac crossings (crystal field analysis), calculating transport properties using Berry curvature methods, and finally determining magnetic exchange interactions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We combine first-principles density-functional theory, Berry-curvature analysis, semiclassical Boltzmann transport, and atomistic spin dynamics to establish hexagonal NiS as a compensated 3d altermagnetic semimetal in which topology, magnetism, and lattice dynamics are intrinsically intertwined. The rotational coset symmetry of the NiAs lattice produces momentum-dependent spin splitting characteristic of altermagnetism. With spin-orbit coupling, gapped Dirac-like crossings generate intense Berry-curvature hot spots and nearly compensated electron-hole pockets. This leads to a large and anisotropic intrinsic spin Hall conductivity comparable to that of several 4d, 5d metals, a symmetry-allowed anomalous Hall response despite zero net magnetization, and nonsaturating magnetoresistance exceeding 10000 percent. On the magnetic side, first-principles determination of the exchange tensor reveals dominant long-range superexchange and sizable anisotropic interactions, quantitatively reproducing the experimental Neel temperature. Our results identify NiS as a model 3d platform in which carrier compensation, altermagnetic symmetry, Berry-curvature driven transport, and lattice-sensitive magnetism coexist within a single symmetry framework, offering a design principle for multi-functional quantum responses in correlated transition-metal compounds.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper explores the stability of Hatsugai-Kohmoto (HK) correlations in a 1D spinful chain when an onsite Hubbard repulsion is added. Using exact diagonalization, the authors find that this local interaction actively reinforces the HK physics rather than disrupting it. The results emphasize that the spatial structure of the repulsive term critically determines its effect on the correlated ground state.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides nonperturbative real-space insights into correlated electron physics, showing how local interactions can stabilize or modify exotic quantum phases like those described by the HK fixed point, which is relevant for understanding Mott insulators.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates how adding a finite onsite Hubbard repulsion modifies the established Hatsugai-Kohmoto (HK) physics in real-space observables and local spectra, moving beyond weak perturbation theory.

**Main result.** The onsite Hubbard interaction actively reinforces the HK-dominated regime, leading to a 'Hubbard-assisted strong-HK response' observed across spin correlations and spectral weight redistribution. This enhancement is highly sensitive to the spatial structure of the added repulsion compared to nearest-neighbor interactions.

**Method.** The research employs exact diagonalization (ED) calculations on an open chain system, supplemented by Lanczos continued-fraction methods for calculating local density of states (LDOS).

**Model / system.** The model is a half-filled one-dimensional spinful fermion chain governed by a Hamiltonian incorporating hopping, the HK interaction ($U_{	ext{HK}}$), and an onsite Hubbard repulsion ($U_{	ext{Hub}}$). Comparisons are made against nearest-neighbor (NN) interactions.

**Key observables.** Ground-state correlation functions (spin $\langle S \cdot S angle$, single-particle $\langle c^\dagger c angle$), Local Density of States (LDOS), and pairing correlations ($\Delta$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Interaction strengths ($U_{	ext{HK}}$, $U_{	ext{Hub}}$) relative to hopping ($t$); the system is studied in the half-filled limit, with comparisons made at finite doping.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Calculations are performed on finite open chains; the analysis relies on comparing results from different interaction types (onsite vs. nearest-neighbor).

**Figures summary.** Figures compare spin and single-particle correlations for HK-only, Hubbard-only, and mixed models across varying coupling strengths, showing how $U_{	ext{Hub}}$ shifts the system towards strong-HK behavior, while NN interactions show competing tendencies.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the effect of onsite repulsion by comparing it to the pure HK model, contrasting this with a nearest-neighbor interaction. Key diagnostics include correlation functions and impurity-induced spectral weight transfer.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Hatsugai-Kohmoto (HK) model has recently been identified as a fixed-point description of Mottness that is stable against perturbing local interactions. This raises a natural question beyond the weak-perturbation regime: how does a finite local repulsion modify HK physics in real-space observables and local spectra? We address this question in a half-filled one-dimensional spinful fermion chain, where the HK interaction is supplemented by an onsite Hubbard repulsion. Using exact diagonalization in an open chain, we compute ground-state correlation functions and site-resolved local spectra. We find that the Hubbard term does not drive the system away from the HK-dominated regime. Instead, at moderate $U_{\rm HK}$, a finite $U_{\rm Hub}$ promotes the emergence of behavior characteristic of the pure HK chain at larger $U_{\rm HK}$. This Hubbard-assisted strong-HK response is reflected consistently in the development of positive spin correlations, the suppression of single-particle coherence, the impurity-induced redistribution of local spectral weight, and the evolution of pairing correlations. As a control perturbation, we replace the onsite Hubbard interaction by a nearest-neighbor (NN) density interaction. In contrast to the Hubbard case, the HK and NN interactions display competing tendencies and do not reproduce the same strong-HK behavior. These results provide a nonperturbative real-space complement to the fixed-point stability of HK physics and show that the effect of an additional repulsive interaction depends sensitively on its spatial structure.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously examines Stoner transitions in 2D electronic materials by moving beyond mean-field approximations using a Diagrammatic Monte Carlo simulation. The study reveals that the nature of magnetic ordering—whether it occurs, and through what sequence of phases—is highly dependent on the number of valleys and the anisotropy of the underlying electron dispersion. It provides crucial insights into correlated magnetism in low-dimensional quantum materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter magnetism, the use of diagrammatic Monte Carlo techniques for strongly correlated electron systems provides sophisticated computational tools applicable to other many-body quantum problems where perturbative expansions fail.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates Stoner instabilities in two-dimensional electronic systems, going beyond the limitations of mean-field theory.

**Main result.** In one-valley systems, ferromagnetism only occurs if momentum transfer is cut off; for two-valley systems, the transition can be direct or proceed through an intermediate valley-polarized state, controlled by dispersion anisotropy.

**Method.** The authors employ a diagrammatic Monte Carlo (DiagMC) algorithm to treat both ladder and non-ladder renormalizations on equal footing in the susceptibility calculation.

**Model / system.** The system models 2D electronic systems with one or two valleys, described by Hamiltonians including kinetic terms and repulsive interactions. The analysis considers parabolic ($k^2$) and $k^6$ dispersions for single-valley cases, and elliptic dispersion for two-valley setups.

**Key observables.** Stoner transition point (instability), order parameters like valley polarization (VP) and ferromagnetism (FM), and the static magnetic susceptibility ($\chi_s$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dimensionless coupling constant $\lambda$, momentum cutoff $q^*$, and anisotropy factor $\eta$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is extending beyond mean-field theory by including higher-order diagrammatic corrections via the DiagMC method.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate phase diagrams for single-valley and two-valley systems showing symmetry-breaking phases (e.g., VP, SVP) versus interaction parameters ($q^*$).

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the mean-field context to applying the advanced DiagMC technique to calculate susceptibilities beyond lowest order, culminating in detailed phase diagrams mapping out different magnetic/valley ordered states.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Stoner instabilities for fermions with repulsive interaction have been well studied within the mean-field (ladder) approximation. In this study, we consider two-dimensional electronic systems with one or two valleys and discuss a Stoner transition beyond the ladder approximation. At weak coupling, the corrections to the ladder approximation come predominantly from the renormalization of the particle-hole vertex in the particle-particle channel, and the lowest-order corrections are logarithmically singular in the low-density limit. To investigate the problem beyond the lowest order, we apply the diagrammatic Monte Carlo algorithm and treat ladder and non-ladder renormalizations on equal footing. We find that in a one-valley system, a Stoner transition to a ferromagnetism occurs at low density only if there is a cutoff on the momentum transfer carried by the interaction. In a two-valley system, the restriction is less severe. Here we find either a direct Stoner transition into a spin- and valley-polarized state, or a set of two Stoner transitions via an intermediate valley-polarized state. The pathway is controlled by the anisotropy of the fermionic dispersion.

</details>
