**Summary.** This work characterizes optimal value readout in response-register quantum oracles by proving that the achievable probability is exactly determined by the normalized Rényi-1/2 effective Fourier support of the state. This establishes an exact information-theoretic measure, providing a tight complementarity theorem between phase and value measurements for Abelian groups.

**Why it may be interesting.** The rigorous mathematical connection between geometric properties (Fourier support) and operational limits ($P_{	ext{val}}$) provides a powerful, generalizable tool for analyzing quantum measurement protocols in structured settings.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper aims to provide an exact, quantitative characterization of the relationship between two complementary operational interpretations—value readout and phase kickback—for response-register quantum oracles.

**Main result.** It proves that for finite Abelian response groups, the optimal single-query value-readout probability is exactly equal to the normalized Rényi-1/2 effective Fourier support of the response state.

**Method.** The analysis uses information theory, linking optimal quantum state discrimination (covariant measurements) to properties derived from the Fourier-weight distribution of the response state via group theory concepts.

**Model / system.** The model is a response-register quantum oracle acting on a computational register and characterized by a finite Abelian response group $A$. The system's behavior is governed by the response state $|\etaangle$ and its associated Fourier-weight distribution $p = (p_\chi)$.

**Key observables.** Optimal single-query value-readout probability ($P_{	ext{val}}$), Rényi-1/2 effective Fourier support ($	ext{Seff}$), and target character phase fidelity ($\Phi$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimension of the response register, $d = |A|$, and the specific structure of the Abelian group $A$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The results are rigorously proven only for finite Abelian response groups; extensions to non-Abelian groups are suggested as future work.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the oracle's two interpretations (value readout vs. phase kickback), map out the achievable phase–value tradeoff region, and show the 'Effective Fourier-support landscape' relating $P_{	ext{val}}$ to the distribution $p$.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes a chain of correspondences: linking the operational limit ($P_{	ext{val}}$) through quantum state discrimination theory to an information-theoretic quantity ($	ext{Seff}$), which is defined via the R\'enyi-1/2 entropy of the Fourier weights.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Response-register quantum oracles admit two complementary operational interpretations: value readout and phase kickback. Although their computational equivalence is well understood, the quantitative relation between these two operational interpretations has lacked an exact characterization. We prove that, for finite Abelian response groups, the optimal single-query value-readout probability is exactly the normalized Rényi-1/2 effective Fourier support of the response state. This identity provides an exact information-theoretic characterization of optimal value-readout capability, gives the Rényi-1/2 effective Fourier support a direct operational interpretation in the oracle setting, and yields a tight phase--value complementarity theorem together with an explicit family of saturating response states.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically characterizes the G-center defect in Silicon, a promising platform for quantum computing. Using advanced multiconfigurational quantum chemistry methods, the authors calculate key properties like ZPL energy, Zero Field Splitting, and spin decoherence times. The results validate the use of complex electronic structure calculations to predict solid-state qubit performance.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses the theoretical modeling required for solid-state qubits by providing detailed predictions of optical transitions (ZPL) and spin coherence times ($T_2$) based on first principles quantum chemistry.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the spin and optical properties of G-centers defects in Silicon, which are critical components for potential quantum computing applications.

**Main result.** The theoretical calculations show excellent agreement with Zero Phonon Line energies and Zero Field Splitting components, and estimate a substantial spin decoherence time (1.465 ms).

**Method.** The research employs advanced quantum chemistry methods including Multiconfigurational Self-Consistent Field (MCSCF), CASSCF(6,6), NEVPT2 corrections, and Cluster Correlation Expansion (CCE) for coherence time.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the G-center defect embedded in Silicon. The calculations model this using various supercell sizes (up to 512 atoms) and focus on the electronic structure of the singlet ($S=0$) and triplet ($S=1$) manifolds.

**Key observables.** Zero Phonon Line (ZPL) energy ($\approx 0.969$ eV), Zero Field Splitting components ($D_{xx}, D_{yy}, D_{zz}$), theoretical spin decoherence time ($T_2$), and Transition Dipole Moment (TDM).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The active space size is systematically expanded from CAS(2,2) to CAS(6,6); the calculations are sensitive to cluster size convergence.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The multiconfigurational approach is necessary because single-configuration wavefunctions fail for describing the optical excitation process; approximations are made in calculating spin-vibrational coupling and decoherence rates.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the G-center structure, the detailed computational workflow (from supercell relaxation to CASSCF), and energy level diagrams showing relative positions of singlet and triplet states.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing the necessity of multiconfigurational methods for defect centers. It details a rigorous computational workflow involving geometry optimization, CASSCF calculations across increasing active spaces, and finally applying CCE/PYCCE to predict spin coherence times.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Understanding the properties of defects is imperative for proper use for variety of applications including quantum computing. In this paper, we use the multiconfigurational self consistent field (MCSCF) combined with DFT optimized geometry in order to investigate the spin and optical properties of G centers in Silicon. By utilizing quantum chemistry based methods, we show excellent agreement with the Zero Phonon Line and Zero Field Splitting Tensor components of the G center. We also calculate the theoretical spin decoherence time of the G centers using Cluster Correlation Expansion (CCE) methods.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously quantifies how realistic measurement imperfections degrade multipartite entanglement resources. By analyzing coherent misalignment and outcome flipping errors against various Bell inequalities, it establishes that secure key generation imposes stricter error tolerances than simply proving nonlocality. The findings offer crucial guidelines for robust device-independent quantum information tasks.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides quantitative benchmarks essential for experimentalists designing and interpreting measurements on multipartite entangled states, directly impacting the feasibility claims of quantum communication protocols.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study analyzes how practical measurement imperfections, specifically coherent angular misalignment and incoherent outcome flipping, degrade multipartite quantum nonlocality certified by Bell inequalities.

**Main result.** Secret-key generation imposes stricter constraints on measurement imperfections than the mere certification of multipartite nonlocality. Coherent errors lead to periodic violation windows, while key rate thresholds are determined by specific critical error probabilities.

**Method.** The authors employ systematic analytical derivations comparing ideal Bell values against degraded expectations using established inequalities (Mermin, Svetlichny, MABK) and connecting these bounds to asymptotic Devetak–Winter key-rate limits.

**Model / system.** The analysis focuses on $n$-partite GHZ states used as the resource for testing multipartite nonlocality. The imperfections are modeled mathematically as angular misalignments or probabilistic outcome flips.

**Key observables.** Degraded Bell expectation values ($S', S'_{Sve}, S'_{MK}$), key generation rates, and critical error thresholds ($	heta_{th}$, $p_{th}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of parties ($n$), angular misalignment ($	heta$ or $\delta_j$), and outcome flipping probability ($p$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the use of GHZ states and restricts comparisons to Bell inequalities with symmetric structures for certain error analyses.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the distinct interference patterns showing how the normalized violation ratio changes for full-system versus partial-system misalignment in $n=3$.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes two types of measurement errors (coherent vs. incoherent) against three major Bell inequalities, culminating in a comparison between the required error tolerance for nonlocality certification versus quantum key distribution.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Multipartite Bell nonlocality is a central resource for device-independent quantum information protocols, but its practical certification is inevitably affected by imperfect measurements. We analyze how coherent angular misalignment and incoherent outcome flipping affect Bell-value degradation and nonlocality thresholds in $n$-partite GHZ states based on the Mermin, Svetlichny, and Mermin--Ardehali--Belinskii--Klyshko (MABK) inequalities. Coherent misalignment produces periodic angular violation windows whose individual widths shrink with the number of parties. In contrast, incoherent outcome flipping yields a single critical outcome-flipping probability, which increases with $n$ for MABK and the odd-$n$ Mermin inequalities, but decreases with $n$ for the Svetlichny inequality. Connecting the degraded Bell values to asymptotic Devetak--Winter key-rate bounds under a convex-combination attack model shows that secret-key generation imposes stricter constraints on measurement imperfections than nonlocality certification. These results provide quantitative benchmarks for robust multipartite nonlocality certification and key-rate estimation under measurement imperfections.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study investigates how disorder affects electrical resistivity in Cu alloys doped with 3d metals. By comparing three magnetic models (DLM, FM, NM), the authors find that resistivity scales strongly with the momentum-space broadening of electronic states (BSF FWHM). This establishes a unified picture showing that scattering is governed by band structure broadening rather than just local electron density.

**Why it may be interesting.** Although focused on solid-state physics, the methodology involves analyzing spectral broadening and scattering rates derived from Green's functions, techniques relevant to understanding decoherence in quantum systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To determine the microscopic mechanism governing the resistivity increase in dilute Cu-3d transition-metal alloys at ambient temperatures.

**Main result.** Resistivity is governed by disorder-induced band broadening (measured via BSF FWHM) in momentum space, rather than solely by local density of states effects. The Double Local Moment (DLM) framework successfully reproduces experimental trends.

**Method.** First-principles calculations using the Korringa–Kohn–Rostoker coherent potential approximation (KKR-CPA) combined with the Kubo–Greenwood formalism to calculate electrical conductivity.

**Model / system.** The study investigates dilute solid-solution alloys of Copper (Cu) and 3d transition metals (X). Theoretical models compare Double Local Moment (DLM), Ferromagnetic (FM), and Non-Magnetic (NM) paramagnetic states.

**Key observables.** Electrical resistivity ($ho$), Full Width at Half Maximum (FWHM) of the Bloch Spectral Function (BSF) on the Fermi surface, Partial Density of States (pDOS) at the Fermi level ($E_F$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Ambient temperature regime ($T \gg T_K$); solute concentration ($	ext{Cu}_{0.99}	ext{X}_{0.01}$); power-law exponent $eta \approx 1.89$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the validity of the DLM framework at room temperature, contrasting with low-temperature Kondo regimes; it also notes that pDOS alone is insufficient to explain resistivity variations.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare $\deltaho$ vs. BSF FWHM (showing strong correlation) and show comparisons between different magnetic states' spectral functions and energy differences used for $T_C$ estimation.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically compares three theoretical descriptions of the paramagnetic state (DLM, FM, NM) using advanced electronic structure calculations to diagnose which physical quantity—pDOS or BSF FWHM—best correlates with measured resistivity trends across the 3d series.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The mechanism governing the resistivity in dilute Cu-3d transition-metal alloys at ambient temperature -- the regime relevant to most practical applications and distinct from the low-temperature, Kondo-screened regime addressed by earlier theoretical work -- is investigated using first-principles calculations based on the Korringa--Kohn--Rostoker coherent potential approximation combined with the Kubo--Greenwood formalism. The paramagnetic state is described within the disordered local moment (DLM) framework, corresponding to a local-moment paramagnet rather than a Pauli paramagnet. We show that the experimentally observed resistivity trends are reproduced only within the DLM description, while nonmagnetic and ferromagnetic states fail to capture the correct element dependence. Contrary to conventional interpretations based on the density of states at the Fermi level, the resistivity exhibits a strong correlation with the full width at half maximum (FWHM) of the Bloch spectral function (BSF) on the Fermi surface. This correlation reflects the disorder-induced lifetime broadening of electronic states, directly related to the scattering rate that governs electrical resistivity. A common power-law scaling between resistivity and BSF broadening is identified across different magnetic states. These results demonstrate that the resistivity is governed by disorder-induced band broadening in momentum space rather than by local density-of-states effects, providing a unified microscopic interpretation of the breakdown of Linde's rule in Cu-based alloys.

</details>

**Summary.** This lecture notes review the physics of the 2D electron Wigner crystal, detailing its formation from strong Coulomb correlations. It progresses from classical jellium models to modern quantum corrections, including how topological effects like Berry curvature introduce chiral terms into the spin Hamiltonian. The work is crucial for understanding correlated insulating states in advanced 2D materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** The discussion of topological modifications via the Berry phase introduces concepts relevant to effective Hamiltonians in condensed matter, which can be analogous to engineered gauge fields or synthetic magnetic fields studied in AMO contexts.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The lecture reviews the fundamental nature of the two-dimensional electron Wigner crystal (WC) phase, focusing on how modern effects like Berry curvature modify its description.

**Main result.** The WC state is governed by the competition between Coulomb energy and kinetic energy, and its properties are significantly modified by topological terms arising from Berry curvature in realistic materials.

**Method.** The analysis combines semiclassical treatments (jellium model) with rigorous quantum mechanical calculations of zero-point energies and spin Hamiltonians incorporating Berry phases.

**Model / system.** The primary system is a two-dimensional electron gas (2DES), modeled initially by the jellium approximation, but extended to include effects from moiré patterns and bilayer structures in modern 2D materials.

**Key observables.** Interaction parameter ($r_s$), quantum energy corrections ($\hbar\omega(\vec{q})$), chiral spin coupling term ($J_\chi$), and transport measurements like longitudinal resistance ($ho_{xx}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** $r_s = E_C/E_F$ (interaction parameter); the Berry phase ($\varphi$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The initial framework assumes a neutralizing background charge (jellium model), and approximations are used for quantum energy corrections, though these show remarkable accuracy.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate WC structures in triangular lattices, experimental images from MoSe2, phase diagrams showing microemulsion transitions, and transport measurements demonstrating the transition between WC and Quantum Hall states.

**Paper structure.** The notes are structured historically, moving from 'Old-fashioned physics' (jellium model for basic WC formation) to 'Newfangled physics' (incorporating Berry curvature effects on spin order and generalized WCs).

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

These are lecture notes created for a short lecture series at the 2026 CTEQ Summer School at Penn State. They are written in a conversational and informal style. The goal of these notes is to introduce and review a smattering of old and new ideas about the Wigner crystal (the solid phase of the two-dimensional electron system) in the context of recent experiments. Particular emphasis is given to the semiclassical description of the Wigner crystal, its quantum melting transition, its spin order, and the ways in which the Wigner crystal can be modified by Berry curvature.

</details>

### [Precoding-based protocols for entanglement assisted linear computation over a quantum many-to-one network](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13756v1)

**Authors:** Ruoyu Meng, Aditya Ramamoorthy  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.13756v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `entanglement & information structure` **3/5** · `non-equilibrium dynamics` **1/5**

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>
<div class="paper-figures-scroll">
<figure class="paper-figure-card">
<img src="2607.13756_figures/2607.13756_fig1.jpg" alt="Figure 1">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 1.</b> Fig. 1. A schematic diagram for a coding scheme under the noiseless quantum many-to-one model. Each Alicei encodes</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This paper develops advanced protocols for performing linear computations over a quantum network where multiple senders share entanglement. By allowing senders to precode inputs, they overcome limitations found in previous models, enabling the computation of more general transformations. Crucially, the authors prove that jointly computing several such functions can reduce the total required communication cost below the sum of individual costs.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on abstract quantum communication complexity, the concepts of resource quantification (communication cost) and achieving super-additive benefits are fundamental theoretical tools applicable to optimizing complex quantum information processing tasks.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work addresses computing a general linear combination of classical data vectors ($Y = \sum V_i W_i$) over a noiseless quantum many-to-one network.

**Main result.** They propose protocols that support a more general class of linear transformations than previously constrained, demonstrating subadditivity in the communication cost across multiple instances.

**Method.** The approach involves encoding classical inputs into local quantum subsystems and transmitting them through an entangled quantum channel to a single receiver for joint measurement and post-processing.

**Model / system.** The model is a noiseless quantum many-to-one network where $k$ senders share entanglement, and the computation aims to reconstruct a linear combination $\mathbf{Y}$ at Bob's location.

**Key observables.** Communication cost (total number of transmitted qudits) and the resulting output vector $\mathbf{Y}$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of senders ($k$), number of instances ($L$), dimensions of input/output spaces ($\mathbb{F}^{m_i}, \mathbb{F}^m$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The computation is assumed to be zero-error, and the analysis builds upon prior work concerning constraints like the Strong Self-Orthogonality (SSO) condition.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically illustrates the three stages of the coding scheme: entanglement distribution, local unitary encoding/transmission by $Alice_i$, and final measurement/post-processing at Bob.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from defining the general linear combination problem to developing protocols that relax prior constraints (like SSO), demonstrating cost improvements, and finally proving subadditivity across multiple instances.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

In this work, we consider the problem of computing a linear combination over a noiseless quantum many-to-one network. There are $k$ senders, Alice$_1$, $\ldots$, Alice$_k$, and a single receiver, Bob. Each Alice$_i$ has a data vector $W_i \in \mathbb{F}^{m_i}$, where $\mathbb{F}$ is a finite field. Bob wants to compute the linear combination $Y = V_1 W_1 + V_2 W_2 + \cdots + V_k W_k \in \mathbb{F}^m$, where $V_i$ is an $m \times m_i$ matrix over $\mathbb{F}$. The senders transmit quantum states to Bob through a noiseless many-to-one quantum network, but they are not allowed to communicate with each other. The senders share entanglement among themselves, while Bob does not share this entanglement. They encode their classical information $W_i$, $i=1,\ldots,k$, into their local subsystems and transmit them to Bob so that he can recover $Y$ through a quantum measurement and subsequent post-processing. The N-Sum Box protocol proposed by Allaix et al. (2025) considers this problem under certain constraints on the linear combination and the distribution of the data vectors among the senders.   We present protocols that support the computation of a more general class of linear transformations by giving the senders access to more qudits and allowing them to judiciously precode their input symbols. The communication cost of our schemes is at most that of the best-known prior results in this area and is strictly lower in certain cases. Finally, we demonstrate that the communication cost is subadditive across instances. Specifically, we identify two linear functions for which the total cost of computing them individually is strictly larger than the cost of computing them jointly.

</details>

**Summary.** This work addresses the difficulty of decoding quantum codes corrupted by correlated errors by developing an efficient multi-index tensor network approach. The authors overcome the challenges of non-factorizable probabilities and exponentially large tensors using index transformations and reparametrization techniques. They demonstrate that this new decoder achieves superior error correction thresholds compared to existing methods.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a sophisticated computational tool (tensor networks) to analyze performance limits in quantum error correction under realistic, complex noise conditions that go beyond simple independent errors.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is developing an efficient maximum likelihood decoder for quantum error correction under inter-qubit correlated noise models.

**Main result.** The proposed multi-index tensor network construction achieves a higher finite-size threshold than the widely used MWPM decoder in numerical simulations.

**Method.** The authors introduce additional binary indices and transformations to build a multi-index tensor network, followed by reparametrization and index classification to ensure efficient contraction of otherwise huge tensors.

**Model / system.** The analysis focuses on decoding the 2D surface code when subjected to correlated Pauli errors, specifically modeling noise combining independent single-qubit errors with nearest-neighbor correlated two-qubit interactions (ENN model).

**Key observables.** Finite-size threshold of the decoder; Coset probabilities $p(C(r'))$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Code distance ($d$); Noise parameters ($p_1, p_2$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method relies on reparametrization and index classification to decompose high-dimensional tensors into efficiently contractible forms.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the Nearest-Neighbor Error Model (ENN) structure; Figure 6 compares the estimated finite-size threshold vs. code distance for the proposed decoder against PyMatching 2.0.

**Paper structure.** The paper reviews surface codes and ML decoding, introduces the general correlated error model, details the multi-index tensor network construction using transformations, presents numerical simulations, and concludes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The maximum likelihood decoder based on tensor networks has proven highly successful for the 2D surface code, achieving the optimal decoding success rate. However, existing tensor network decoders are typically designed for independent single-qubit error models, and their performance under inter-qubit correlated error models remains unexplored. This is due to two major challenges. The first challenge lies in constructing the tensor network for correlated errors, since the same final Pauli error can arise from many different combinations of independent and correlated errors, preventing a direct factorization of the error probability. The second challenge is that even after a tensor network is constructed, it generally contains huge-dimensional tensors and is therefore not efficiently contractible. In this work, to address the first difficulty, we introduce additional binary indices and two transformations to construct a multi-index tensor network for maximum-likelihood decoding with correlated errors. To address the second difficulty, we use reparametrization, elimination, and index classification to decompose the huge-dimensional tensors into lower-dimensional tensors. This yields an efficiently contractible tensor network for error models satisfying the tractability conditions derived in this work. We perform numerical simulations for a representative correlated error model and show that the maximum-likelihood decoder implemented with our multi-index tensor network construction achieves a higher finite-size threshold than the widely used MWPM decoder.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper proposes that large-angle twisted van der Waals multilayers offer a viable route to achieving intrinsically three-dimensional fractional quantum Hall phases. By suppressing interlayer tunneling via twist angles, the system avoids metallic instabilities common in conventional 3D stacks. The resulting generalized Halperin states host exotic quasiparticles with irrational braiding statistics, opening new avenues for topological quantum information.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete materials platform (twisted van der Waals layers) for realizing exotic topological matter predicted in strongly correlated electron systems, linking theoretical concepts of irrational anyons to solid-state physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is realizing intrinsically three-dimensional (3D) fractional quantum Hall (FQH) phases that exhibit exotic properties like irrational anyons, moving beyond simple stacks of 2D liquids.

**Main result.** Large-angle twisted van der Waals multilayers stabilize generalized Halperin liquid states exhibiting non-foliated 3D topological order with quasiparticles possessing irrational braiding statistics.

**Method.** The study employs Monte Carlo calculations to compare the energies of a large set of competing trial wavefunctions, determining the ground state by minimizing the total energy functional.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled using twisted van der Waals multilayers (like graphene) under a strong magnetic field. The key mechanism relies on large twist angles suppressing interlayer tunneling while preserving strong Coulomb interactions.

**Key observables.** Topological order structure (non-foliated), quasiparticle braiding statistics (irrational vs. rational), and the relative stability of generalized Halperin states versus metallic phases.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Large twist angles ($	heta \gtrsim 10^\circ$), small interlayer tunneling ($t_{\perp} \approx 1.78$ meV), and specific filling fractions like $
u_L = 1/7$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the suppression of coherent interlayer tunneling due to momentum mismatch is the critical factor stabilizing the novel liquid phases.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the transition from dispersive 3D Landau levels favoring density waves (Fig. 1) and present phase diagrams comparing different twisted graphene regimes based on magnetic field and filling fraction (Fig. 3).

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the physical problem of achieving 3D FQH states, then details how large-angle twisting solves the metallic instability issue by suppressing tunneling, followed by extensive Monte Carlo calculations comparing candidate wavefunctions to identify the stable generalized Halperin liquid.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Three-dimensional fractional quantum Hall phases offer a route to intrinsically higher-dimensional topological order beyond simple stacks of two-dimensional quantum Hall liquids. Such phases can exhibit non-foliated, intrinsically three-dimensional entanglement structures, exponentially large topological degeneracies and quasiparticles with irrational braiding statistics. Their microscopic realization has remained elusive because Landau quantization in three dimensions generally leaves dispersive one-dimensional bands, favoring metallic and density-wave states over incompressible fractional liquids. Here we show that large-angle twisted van der Waals multilayers provide a practical route around this obstruction. Large twist angles suppress coherent interlayer tunneling through momentum mismatch, while the atomic-scale layer separation preserves strong interlayer Coulomb interactions. Using Monte Carlo calculations to compare the energies of an extensive set of 862 competing trial wavefunctions, we find that generalized Halperin states with quantum coherence extending across multiple consecutive layers are stabilized. In experimentally accessible magnetic-field regimes, these states replace the metallic spontaneous-interlayer-coherent phases that dominate conventional untwisted graphite-like multilayers. The resulting liquids realize non-foliated fractional quantum Hall order closely related to fractonic topological order, hosting quasiparticles with rational electric charges but irrational braiding statistics. Their large topological degeneracy and non-rational statistical phases may offer unconventional resources for quantum information storage and processing. Our results establish twisted van der Waals multilayers as a realistic materials platform for three-dimensional fractional Hall matter beyond conventional two-dimensional quantum Hall systems.

</details>

**Summary.** This work investigates the coupled thermodynamic and electronic properties of the half-Heusler material TbAuPb. By combining single-crystal measurements with advanced DFT calculations, the authors demonstrate that transport properties are highly sensitive to the material's magnetic state. The findings suggest that TbAuPb is a semimetal whose topology can be tuned by external magnetic fields.

**Why it may be interesting.** The strong coupling observed between charge transport anomalies (e.g., $	ext{AMR}$ symmetry changes) and magnetic phase transitions provides a rich platform for studying emergent quantum phenomena driven by magnetic order, relevant to correlated electron physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the interplay between magnetic ordering, electronic band topology, and charge transport properties in the half-Heusler plumbide TbAuPb.

**Main result.** TbAuPb exhibits semimetallic behavior whose magnetotransport properties are highly sensitive to its magnetic state. Theoretical calculations suggest it is a band inverted semimetal in the non-magnetic state, becoming topologically trivial upon field-induced ferromagnetism.

**Method.** The research combines experimental measurements (magnetization, specific heat, resistivity, magnetoresistance) with first-principles electronic structure calculations using DFT/MBJGGA+U functionals.

**Model / system.** The physical system is the single-crystal half-Heusler compound TbAuPb, which possesses a cubic MgAgAs-type structure and exhibits intrinsic magnetism due to partially filled 4f orbitals on Tb. The analysis covers both antiferromagnetic (AFM) and field-induced ferromagnetic (FM) magnetic phases.

**Key observables.** Electrical resistivity $ho(T)$, magnetoresistance (MR), angular magnetoresistance ($	ext{AMR}$), Hall resistivity ($ho_{xy}(B)$), specific heat $C(T)$, and electronic band structure/Density of States ($	ext{DOS}$) at the Fermi level.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Antiferromagnetic transition temperature ($T_N = 5 	ext{ K}$), critical magnetic field ($B_c = 5 	ext{ T}$), carrier mobilities ($\mu_n, \mu_p$), and lattice parameters ($a = 6.745(4) 	ext{ Å}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** DFT calculations utilize GGA/MB-JGGA functionals with SOC included; the analysis models charge transport using a two-band model fit at high temperatures.

**Figures summary.** Figures detail temperature dependence of magnetic susceptibility and specific heat, alongside $ho(T)$, MR vs. angle ($	heta$), and electronic band structures comparing nonmagnetic and ferromagnetic states.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically presents structural confirmation (XRD/EDX), followed by thermodynamic measurements ($C(T)$), then detailed magnetotransport studies ($ho(T)$, MR, AMR, Hall effect) across magnetic transitions, culminating in theoretical calculations of the electronic band structure and topological analysis.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Structural, thermodynamic and electrical transport properties of TbAuPb were investigated on single crystals. The compound was found to crystallize with the cubic MgAgAs-type structure characteristic of half-Heusler materials. It orders antiferromagnetically at TN = 5 K and undergoes a transition into a different antiferromagnetic phase emerging in high magnetic fields. Electrical transport in TbAuPb exhibits a multiband character, with a predominance of hole-like carriers. Angular magnetoresistance evolves systematically with applied magnetic field and changes its symmetry near the spin-reorientation transition, highlighting strong coupling between the charge transport and the magnetic order. The results of first-principles calculations indicate that TbAuPb is a band inverted semimetal in the non-magnetic state, which becomes topologically trivial in the field-induced ferromagnetic state.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents a microscopic constitutive theory to explain the nonlinear mechanical behavior of amorphous solids like metallic and polymer glasses. It successfully models the sequence from elastic loading through stress overshoot, yielding, and strain hardening by coupling nonaffine elasticity instability with structural relaxation dynamics. The results provide a unified theoretical framework validated against multiple experimental materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter mechanics, the underlying mathematical framework involving complex relaxation functions (stretched exponentials) and coupling between structural disorder (nonaffine effects) and macroscopic response shares conceptual links with modeling glassy dynamics in quantum or many-body systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Developing a microscopic constitutive theory for the nonlinear deformation of amorphous materials, specifically addressing stress overshoot, yielding, and strain hardening.

**Main result.** The model successfully reproduces the full stress-strain response across various glasses by unifying nonaffine elasticity instability with viscous structural relaxation dynamics. This establishes a unified framework linking these complex mechanical phenomena in amorphous solids.

**Method.** The theory couples nonaffine elasticity (derived from free energy considerations) to irreversible many-body relaxation processes, modeling the total stress as a sum of elastic, viscous, and hardening components.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on metallic and polymer glasses (e.g., polycarbonate, PMMA). The theory uses concepts like nonaffine displacements, strain-dependent coordination number, and stretched exponential relaxation forms to describe mechanical response.

**Key observables.** Stress-strain response curve, stress overshoot magnitude, yielding point, steady plastic flow regime, and strain hardening behavior.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Elastic modulus (E), viscosity ($\eta$), structural relaxation time ($	au_c$), viscous relaxation time ($	au_v$), and the stretched exponential exponent ($eta$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The total stress is assumed to be a linear sum of elastic, viscous, and hardening contributions. For polymers, finite chain extensibility is incorporated.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare theoretical predictions (solid lines) with experimental data (symbols) for MG, PC, and PMMA across various strain rates and temperatures, illustrating the characteristic sequence from loading to flow/hardening.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory by defining components of stress ($\sigma_{el}, \sigma_{visc}, \sigma_{hard}$), deriving constitutive equations based on nonaffine elasticity and relaxation dynamics, and then applying these models to quantitatively reproduce experimental data for multiple amorphous materials across different deformation regimes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We develop a microscopic constitutive theory for the nonlinear deformation of metallic and polymer glasses based on nonaffine elasticity coupled to irreversible many-body relaxation. The theory predicts the full stress--strain response, from linear elasticity through stress overshoot and yielding to steady plastic flow. We show that stress overshoot originates from the competition between a nonaffine elastic instability induced by strain-driven loss of mechanical connectivity at the atomic/molecular level, and viscous dissipation associated with structural relaxation. For polymer glasses, finite chain extensibility naturally accounts for strain hardening at large deformation. The stretched-exponential relaxation exponent is obtained independently from stress or modulus relaxation measurements and provides the primary dynamical input to the theory. Using a small set of physically meaningful parameters, the model quantitatively reproduces experimental stress--strain curves for metallic glasses, polycarbonate, PMMA, and epoxy resins over a broad range of strain rates. These results establish a unified microscopic framework linking relaxation dynamics, yielding, plastic flow, and strain hardening in amorphous solids.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes using Quantum Generative Adversarial Networks (QGANs) as a tool to test the security of Post-Quantum Cryptography. By training QGANs on cryptographic signature data, the authors demonstrate that near-term quantum hardware can learn these complex probability distributions. This suggests a viable pathway for utilizing quantum computation in cryptanalysis workflows.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on cryptography, the underlying mechanism—using variational quantum circuits to model complex probability distributions—is highly relevant to simulating many-body quantum states or learning Hamiltonians in condensed matter physics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is assessing the resilience of Post-Quantum Cryptography (PQC) protocols against potential attacks leveraging quantum computational capabilities.

**Main result.** The study confirms that near-term hybrid quantum-classical methods, specifically using QGANs, possess sufficient capability to learn and approximate the complex probability distributions underlying PQC signatures.

**Method.** A Quantum Generative Adversarial Network (QGAN) framework is employed. The generator prepares a quantum state representing the data distribution, while a classical discriminator network attempts to distinguish between real and generated samples.

**Model / system.** The model utilizes QGANs, which are hybrid quantum-classical architectures. The core task involves loading the probability distribution of hash-based digital signatures (like SPHINCS+) into a quantum state $|\psi_tangle$.

**Key observables.** Kullback-Leibler divergence ($D_{KL}(P \parallel Q)$) is used to quantify the approximation quality between the true and generated distributions.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of qubits ($n$), the structure/number of parameters in the quantum ansatz (e.g., N-local, real-amplitudes), and training loss metrics are key parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The approach is limited by current Noisy Intermediate-Scale Quantum (NISQ) technology; the results are based on simulation and do not constitute a full cryptanalytic attack.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the QGAN architecture, show sample data from PQC signatures, track training loss over epochs, and compare $D_{KL}$ divergence across different quantum ansatz types for both real and simple distributions.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem of PQC security assessment using quantum methods. It details the QGAN methodology, applies it to SPHINCS+ signatures, compares various quantum circuit ansatze (N-local vs. real-amplitudes), analyzes convergence via loss functions, and concludes with the potential implications for cryptanalysis.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The potential capabilities of quantum computers motivated the development of cryptographic protocols suitable for securing communication against adversaries with access to large fault-tolerant quantum computers. However, even though current quantum computers are limited in terms of size and precision, they can still be useful for finding loopholes and weaknesses in the post-quantum cryptographic protocols. In this work, we present an attempt to utilize the capabilities of Quantum Generative Adversarial Networks (QGANs), one of the promising architectures used in quantum machine learning, for this purpose. We describe an example application of QGAN architecture for the purpose of loading the probability distribution of the hash-based digital signatures into the memory of a quantum computer. Our results confirm that near-term hybrid quantum-classical methods possess capabilities required for this purpose. The presented approach can be used as a first step in the workflow, enabling the utilization of quantum computing for attacking post-quantum cryptographic primitives.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper proposes an advanced method to create reliable single-electron traps on solid neon, a promising platform for qubits. The authors use detailed 3D simulations to show that uncontrolled surface roughness causes trapping noise, but they demonstrate that selectively etching an underlying dielectric layer can deterministically engineer the potential minimum required for stable qubit operation.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses hardware realization challenges for solid-state quantum computing platforms by providing a theoretical blueprint for controlling nanoscale potential landscapes using material engineering.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is achieving deterministic, controlled single-electron trapping on solid neon surfaces for use as a quantum qubit, overcoming issues caused by uncontrolled surface roughness.

**Main result.** Engineered dielectric templates via selective etching provide a practical pathway to create reproducible potential minima that reliably capture electrons while suppressing unwanted surface bound states.

**Method.** The authors employed large-scale 3D Schrödinger–Poisson simulations to model the quantum electrostatics of electron confinement in patterned solid neon structures.

**Model / system.** The system is a levitating single-electron qubit on solid neon, modeled using a 3D finite-difference Schrödinger–Poisson framework. The potential landscape is controlled by engineering an underlying dielectric layer beneath the neon film.

**Key observables.** Lowest-energy bound states ($E_n$), orbital splitting ($\Delta E = E_2 - E_1$), probability density $|\psi(\mathbf{r})|^2$, and wavefunction localization.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Dielectric constant ($\varepsilon_r$), dielectric thickness ($H$), surface roughness parameters ($\sigma_{rms}$), and gate voltages (e.g., $V_{	ext{bottom}} = 1.0 	ext{ V}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The model assumes the validity of the single-particle limit for initial analysis, though future work aims to include spin dynamics.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the comparison between random roughness effects (causing unintended trapping) and the controlled potential landscape achieved by etching a dielectric trench, showing localized wavefunctions in the engineered geometry.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the promise of solid neon qubits to identifying surface morphology as the critical failure point. It then details simulations comparing uncontrolled roughness vs. engineered dielectric templates, culminating in demonstrating deterministic trapping via selective etching.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Levitating electron qubit on the surface of solid neon has recently emerged as a promising and intrinsically noise-resilient platform for quantum information processing. Their ultra-clean, inert environment suppresses conventional decoherence pathways associated with lattice disorder, charge traps, and nuclear-spin baths that limit coherence in semiconductor qubits. Yet, uncontrolled surface features such as bumps, valleys, and electrode-defined gaps can bind electrons unintentionally, contributing charge noise and inducing spin-orbit coupling mediated decoherence. To address this challenge, we propose an engineered interface in which a dielectric layer is deposited beneath the solid neon to provide an atomically smooth template, eliminating surface-roughness induced trapping. By selectively etching this dielectric layer at desired qubit locations, deterministic potential minima can be engineered to reliably capture electrons while suppressing unwanted surface bound states. We perform large-scale Schrodinger and Poisson simulation to compare the existing and proposed strategies of electron trapping on neon, obtaining good agreement with recent experimental measurements.

</details>

**Summary.** The authors present an advanced, modular Magneto-Optical Trap (MOT) designed specifically for high-precision quantum sensing using cesium atoms. By implementing diverging laser beams and a compact optical cage system, they achieve stable trapping of millions of atoms at temperatures below $10 \mu	ext{K}$. This demonstrates a significant engineering improvement over conventional MOTs, enhancing reliability for future quantum applications.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly addresses practical engineering challenges in quantum sensing platforms—namely, mechanical robustness and optical coupling efficiency—by modifying the standard MOT setup. The use of diverging beams is a key technical advance for field deployment.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a robust, modular Magneto-Optical Trap (MOT) optimized for high-precision quantum sensing and metrology applications.

**Main result.** The developed MOT successfully traps large numbers of cesium atoms ($\sim 4	imes10^8$) with low temperatures ($<10 \mu	ext{K}$) while maintaining long-term stability, even when using novel diverging laser beam geometries.

**Method.** The system employs a modular optical cage setup and utilizes diverging cooling laser beams instead of conventional collimated beams to improve robustness against reflections. Cooling is optimized via Polarization-Gradient Cooling (PGC).

**Model / system.** The physical platform is a Cesium Magneto-Optical Trap (MOT) adapted for stability and modularity. It can operate in standard or non-conventional diagonal magnetic field geometries.

**Key observables.** Atom number ($	ext{Cs}$ atoms), temperature ($T$), spatial widths ($\sigma_{X'}, \sigma_{Z'}$), and long-term stability over $>50$ hours.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Optimal MOT parameters include gradients near $6 	ext{ G/cm}$, intensity $\sim 1.1 	ext{ mW/cm}^2$, and detuning at $2\pi 	imes 12 	ext{ MHz}$. PGC duration is optimized around $6 	ext{ ms}$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The accuracy of temperature measurement via Time-of-Flight (TOF) method is limited below $10 \mu	ext{K}$; the diagonal MOT configuration is noted to be highly sensitive to parameter drift.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the overall setup using six optical cages, show temperature measurements via TOF fitting, detail optimization scans for 3D MOT parameters and PGC duration, and track long-term stability over $>52$ hours.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the motivation (need for robust MOTs), presents the novel diverging beam/modular optical system, optimizes loading and cooling stages (PGC), validates performance through temperature and atom number measurements, and concludes by testing stability in both conventional and non-conventional geometries.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Magneto-optical traps (MOTs) are a workhorse technology for neutral-atom quantum sensing, simulation, and computing. Here, we demonstrate a MOT optimized for inertial quantum sensing and precision metrology applications. Our MOT traps $4\times10^8$ cesium (Cs) atoms in a robust, modular system that achieves stable operation through two design features: (1) we use diverging cooling laser beams to reduce unwanted reflections and (2) optical elements are mounted in a fiber-coupled, compact, and modular cage rigidly attached to the vacuum chamber. Following polarization-gradient cooling (PGC), the system produces atom samples with temperatures below 10 $μ$K, similar to those achieved in conventional MOTs that use collimated laser beams. In addition, we observe trapping of $2\times10^7$ Cs atoms in a non-conventional MOT geometry, where the cooling laser beams are diagonal to the principal axis of the quadrupole magnetic field.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces an exact, group-theoretically derived algorithm for calculating observables in U(N) matrix models within their physically relevant gauge-invariant sector. By mapping the problem onto representations of the symmetric group, it provides explicit polynomial formulas for finite-N dynamics. This opens a new computational window into non-planar regimes of holographic quantum gravity models.

**Why it may be interesting.** While highly abstract, the techniques developed—using representation theory (Schur functions, symmetric groups) to solve complex many-body quantum field theories in a finite-N limit—are analogous to advanced methods used in strongly correlated electron systems or lattice gauge theories.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is developing an exact, systematic algorithm for computing observables within the gauge-invariant singlet sector of bosonic U(N) matrix models.

**Main result.** The authors present a group-theoretic framework that yields closed-form polynomial expressions in N for these matrix elements, allowing access to finite-N dynamics previously difficult to compute.

**Method.** The method relies on decomposing the Hilbert space using Schur polynomials and evaluating matrix elements via group-theoretic reductions involving cosets and character sums of the symmetric group.

**Model / system.** The system is a bosonic U(N) matrix model, fundamental in describing M-theory and D-brane dynamics through the gauge/gravity duality. The Hamiltonian involves quadratic and quartic terms in the matrices.

**Key observables.** Hamiltonian matrix elements, correlation functions of gauge-invariant observables (singlets), and the low-lying energy spectrum.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The rank N of the gauge group, the coupling constant g, and the mass parameter m.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to the gauge-invariant singlet sector. A major limitation noted is the difficulty in computing restricted characters for multi-matrix extensions.

**Figures summary.** Not specified by detailed notes; general structure involves mathematical derivations related to tensor decomposition and group theory.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from establishing the physical context (M-theory/D-branes) to defining the singlet basis using Schur polynomials, deriving explicit formulas for gauge generators, calculating correlators via Wick's theorem, and finally presenting the general algorithm structure.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Matrix models appear as fundamental descriptions of M-theory and D-brane dynamics, and via the gauge/gravity duality their gauge-invariant, or singlet, sector describes the purely gravitational degrees of freedom in the holographic dual. We present a new exact algorithm for computing observables of bosonic U(N) matrix models in the gauge-invariant singlet sector. This sector is spanned by an orthogonal basis of Schur polynomials (for a single matrix) and restricted Schur polynomials (for multiple matrices), which diagonalizes the free Hamiltonian and provides a natural truncation of the Hilbert space by excitation number. Matrix elements of the interaction Hamiltonian, or any gauge-invariant observable, are evaluated through a group-theoretic reduction to cosets and double cosets of suitable subgroups of the symmetric group, together with character sums on the symmetric group. The resulting entries are closed-form polynomials in the gauge-group rank N, assembled from group-theoretic data that are precomputed once and can be reused for any N and any coupling constants. We validate the one-matrix implementation against the exact mapping to N non-interacting fermions, demonstrating rapid convergence of the low-lying spectrum with the cutoff. The multi-matrix extension is outlined; its main bottleneck is the computation of restricted characters of the symmetric group, for which no algorithm comparable to the Murnaghan--Nakayama rule is currently known. The framework gives direct access to finite-N, finite-coupling dynamics of gauge-invariant states and opens a new computational window on the non-planar regime of holographic matrix models.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents QuPIV, a novel quantum algorithm designed to accelerate Particle Image Velocimetry (PIV) calculations by replacing classical FFT-based cross-correlation with multidimensional Quantum Fourier Transforms. The authors demonstrate the feasibility of this end-to-end process using experimental data, showing that advanced techniques like contracted amplitude amplification can maintain high accuracy for determining fluid velocity fields.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on fluid dynamics, the core methodology—developing complex, multi-stage quantum algorithms for signal processing using advanced techniques like contracted amplitude amplification—is highly relevant to developing general quantum computational primitives applicable across physics domains.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The authors propose a quantum algorithm, QuPIV, designed to replace the classical computation required for Particle Image Velocimetry (PIV) by leveraging quantum Fourier Transforms.

**Main result.** The end-to-end quantum pipeline demonstrates feasibility on experimental PIV data, showing that error sources like input preparation and processing can be analyzed individually, with performance comparable to classical methods.

**Method.** The methodology involves an end-to-end quantum circuit utilizing multidimensional Quantum Fourier Transforms (QFTs) for cross-correlation evaluation, coupled with novel state preparation and contracted amplitude amplification techniques.

**Model / system.** The application is PIV, a technique used to measure velocity fields in fluid dynamics by analyzing particle image correlations between two frames. The quantum computation operates on the correlation map derived from these images.

**Key observables.** Velocity vector ($\vec{u} = \Delta\vec{x}/\Delta t$), peak position error (Euclidean norm), and correlation map ($C_{map}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Image size ($N$ pixels), overlap percentage, number of amplification queries ($M$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the ability to efficiently load image data into qubits and relies on approximations like using binary inputs (sparsification) and analyzing error sources separately.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the QuPIV workflow from input images to velocity calculation; one figure compares quantum, classical, and analytical solutions for PIV challenge data, while another details individual error source analysis (input, output, processing).

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem, outlines the end-to-end quantum algorithm pipeline (state preparation $ightarrow$ QFT $ightarrow$ Amplitude Amplification), presents theoretical and numerical studies on synthetic/experimental data, and concludes with detailed error analysis.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Particle Image Velocimetry (PIV) is the prime image-processing technique to measure and visualize velocity fields of laminar and turbulent flows. The velocity field vectors are obtained with sub-pixelaccuracy by analyzing cross-correlations, empowered by Fast Fourier Transforms (FFT). Here, we present a quantum algorithm with multidimensional quantum Fourier Transforms, termed Quantum-based PIV (QuPIV), to replace the classical computation of up to millions of velocity vectors. Our end-to-end quantum algorithm includes a novel state preparation, modified amplitude amplification, and the output extraction. We enhance amplitude amplification by a contracted ground-state projector, which allows a significant reduction of the number of gates in the quantum circuit. We justify the end-to-end capability with numerical studies on all stages of the algorithm on both synthetic and experimental data.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a general 'foliated' framework to implement Measurement-Based Quantum Computing (MBQC) using qudits. It constructs resource graph states by extending 2D error-correcting codes across time layers, allowing fault-tolerant computation for various code types. The results confirm the method's applicability and show that performance metrics like error thresholds improve with higher qudit dimensions.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly impacts quantum computing theory by extending established QEC concepts from qubits to qudits, which is crucial for realizing high-capacity photonic quantum information processing.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop a general framework for constructing fault-tolerant Measurement-Based Quantum Computing (MBQC) using qudits by 'foliating' any Pauli-based quantum error-correcting code.

**Main result.** The authors provide a generalized construction applicable to various code types (stabilizer, subsystem, dynamical) and demonstrate that the error correction threshold increases with the qudit dimension $d$.

**Method.** The method involves constructing a time-extended graph state ($\Lambda$) by foliating the 2D code structure across multiple time layers. Fault tolerance is achieved via sequential X-basis measurements on ancilla qubits, requiring careful calculation of 'detectors' to track stabilizer values.

**Model / system.** The system utilizes qudits (dimension $d$, where $d$ is prime) encoded within a graph state structure that evolves over discrete time steps ($t$). This framework is particularly relevant for photonic platforms where high-dimensional states are natural resources.

**Key observables.** Error correction thresholds, stabilizer generators of the graph state, and detector observables (which must commute with all measurements).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The qudit dimension $d$ (prime), the number of data/ancilla qubits ($N, T$), and the error model (photon loss dominant).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The framework initially focuses on CSS-type codes, though generalizations to non-CSS and dynamical types are discussed. For photonic implementation, fusion-based computation is suggested due to potential limitations in deterministic gates.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the qudit toric code unit cell, the process of foliating perfect codes across time layers, and detailed diagrams showing how detectors span multiple time steps ($t=0$ to $t=4$) for both CSS and non-CSS cases.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from defining the general framework for qudit graph states and measurement protocols (MBQC). It then details the construction of detectors by analyzing stabilizer generators across time layers, providing specific examples like the qudit toric code and generalized honeycomb codes to validate the method.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a framework for foliating any Pauli-based quantum error-correcting code over prime-dimensional qudits. For any such code, we obtain a qudit graph state that can be measured to perform fault-tolerant measurement-based quantum computing. Such a paradigm is of interest in platforms such as photonics, where measurement-based protocols are natural and high-dimensional states are readily available. We discuss several examples for arbitrary prime dimension $d$, such as the qudit toric code (stabilizer, CSS), the $d$-dimensional perfect $[[5,1,3]]$ code (stabilizer, non-CSS), and a straightforward $d$-dimensional generalization of the CSS honeycomb code (dynamical, CSS). Under a simple error model, we numerically calculate thresholds for the foliated qudit toric code and demonstrate that they are comparable to the non-foliated version.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces 'half dualization' to construct non-invertible particle-vortex duality defects on a lattice. By applying this technique to the charge-$n$ XY model, it demonstrates that these defects are fundamentally non-invertible due to underlying $\mathbb{Z}_n$ symmetries. Crucially, the authors show that introducing gauge fields into the system resolves this non-invertibility as the theory flows to its infrared limit.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful general mathematical tool (half dualization) for analyzing topological defects and duality structures in lattice field theories, which is highly relevant for understanding critical phenomena and phase transitions in condensed matter systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The research introduces 'half dualization' as a general method to construct non-invertible duality defects by performing a duality transformation only in a subregion of spacetime.

**Main result.** Half dualization successfully constructs a non-invertible particle-vortex duality defect for the charge-$n$ XY model, and this non-invertibility is shown to be resolved (flowing to an invertible defect) when the system is gauged or Higgsed in the infrared limit.

**Method.** The core method involves dividing the lattice into two regions ($L_1, L_2$) separated by a cut ($\Sigma$), performing the duality transformation only on $L_2$, and analyzing the resulting interface coupling term (the defect).

**Model / system.** The primary model is the (2+1)-dimensional Villainized charge-$n$ XY model on a cubic lattice, which is later generalized to include $\mathbb{Z}_n$ gauge fields. The Hamiltonian involves cosine interactions dependent on the charge $n$.

**Key observables.** Non-invertibility of the duality defect (tested by fusing it with its reverse), and the flow of the defect's nature (non-invertible to invertible) in the infrared limit.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The charge exponent $n$, coupling constants ($K, K', \kappa$), and the regime defined by comparing $K'$ to $n^2K$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method is general and dimension-independent; specific analyses rely on approximations like the Villainization or assuming certain couplings are small enough to retain $\mathbb{Z}_n$ structure.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the construction of the duality defect across a codimension-one cut. Figure 2 schematically shows the phase diagram of the gauged model, illustrating the flow from non-invertible to invertible defects.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces half dualization using the XY model to find a non-invertible defect. It then applies this technique to the $\mathbb{Z}_n$-gauged version of the model, showing how gauging causes the defect to flow towards an invertible state in the IR.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce half dualization as a general principle to construct non-invertible duality defects. The construction performs a duality transformation only in a subregion of spacetime, leaving an interface between the original theory and its dual. A non-invertible duality defect can be hosted on this interface. Half dualization is applicable whenever a local duality transformation is available, and does not depend on the spacetime dimension. We demonstrate the construction procedure in the (2+1)-dimensional Villainized charge-$n$ XY model on a cubic lattice. Half dualization yields a non-invertible particle-vortex duality defect whose fusion with its orientation reverse produces a (1+1)-dimensional $\mathbb{Z}_n$ gauge theory on the fusion surface. We then apply half dualization to the $\mathbb{Z}_n$-gauged XY model relevant to the 3D XY$^\ast$ transition. In the gauged model, the half dualization interface hosts a gauge covariant duality wall, rather than a genuine gauge invariant duality defect. It flows to an invertible duality defect in infrared when the $\mathbb{Z}_n$ gauge theory is confined or Higgsed.

</details>

**Summary.** This thesis explores the dual potential of machine learning—both classical and quantum—for analyzing complex datasets in particle physics. It develops Quantum Extreme Learning Machines (QELMs) for quantum data analysis and applies deep convolutional networks to classify neutrino events from water Cherenkov detector images, highlighting ML's role in modern fundamental physics research.

**Why it may be interesting.** It bridges advanced ML techniques with fundamental physics data analysis, providing insights into when quantum information processing might yield non-trivial computational advantages over classical methods in structured physical measurements.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work investigates applying machine learning, both classical (deep learning) and quantum (QELMs), to analyze complex, high-dimensional data arising in fundamental physics experiments like neutrino detection.

**Main result.** CNNs effectively classify complex neutrino events from detector images, while QELM analysis suggests that useful feature generation relies on local entanglement build-up over a finite time scale rather than requiring fully scrambled quantum states.

**Method.** The paper employs Quantum Extreme Learning Machines (QELMs) for quantum data processing and Convolutional Neural Networks (CNNs), including residual networks, for classical image classification in neutrino physics.

**Model / system.** The systems include water Cherenkov detectors used in neutrino experiments (e.g., Hyper-Kamiokande) and the theoretical framework of QELMs operating on N-qubit states governed by fixed unitary evolution.

**Key observables.** Classification accuracy for event discrimination, entanglement growth, and the characteristic transition time ($t^*$) in quantum dynamics.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Number of qubits (N), evolution time (t), and detector charge/timing information from PMTs.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The QELM analysis suggests that a physically meaningful intermediate regime exists where local dynamics are sufficient for feature extraction, not requiring generic quantum advantage.

**Figures summary.** Not specified by the notes; the focus is on methodology and results across two distinct applications (QML vs. Neutrino Imaging).

**Paper structure.** The thesis is structured in two main parts: the first develops QELMs, analyzing encoding strategies and quantum dynamics; the second applies deep learning to classify complex event topologies from simulated water Cherenkov detector images.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

This thesis investigates the application of machine-learning methods in the context of quantum computing and neutrino physics, with particular emphasis on the construction of effective representations for complex, high-dimensional data. The first part of the work is devoted to Quantum Extreme Learning Machines (QELMs), a hybrid quantum--classical framework in which classical data are encoded into quantum states and processed through fixed quantum dynamics, while learning is performed by a classical readout layer. Within this framework, we analyze the role of encoding strategies, feature-reduction methods, Hamiltonian structure, and measurement, with particular focus on the relationship between quantum dynamics, expressivity, entanglement, and classical simulability. The second part of the thesis concerns the application of deep learning to the analysis of images produced by water Cherenkov detectors in neutrino physics. Convolutional architectures, including residual networks, are developed for the classification of complex events in realistic simulated datasets, showing that such models can effectively extract relevant information from detector data. Taken together, these results highlight the potential of machine learning, in both its classical and quantum forms, as a powerful framework for the analysis of complex data in fundamental physics, while also outlining relevant challenges and directions for future research.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces the concept of passage speed derived from the transition susceptibility, offering a method to characterize quantum motion independent of net probability flow. By applying this technique to relativistic systems, the authors show that this passage speed correctly predicts local dispersion velocities and establishes the reduced Compton wavelength as a fundamental length scale governing relativistic quantum dynamics.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a novel diagnostic tool ($v_p$) to measure intrinsic quantum motion that is robust even when standard probability current measures vanish (e.g., standing waves), linking it directly to fundamental scales like the Compton wavelength.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To characterize quantum motion independently of probability transport by analyzing how a quantum state responds to weak local perturbations.

**Main result.** The inverse transition susceptibility defines a passage speed that reproduces the local dispersion velocity in both propagating and evanescent regions, establishing a relativistic bound related to the reduced Compton wavelength.

**Method.** The methodology involves calculating the transition susceptibility ($\chi$) of quantum states subjected to weak localized couplings ($J$), defining the passage speed as $v_p = \chi^{-1}$.

**Model / system.** The analysis considers general quantum states, extending from non-relativistic Schrödinger equations to relativistic Klein-Gordon waveguides coupled transversely. The system is probed by applying weak local perturbations.

**Key observables.** Passage speed ($v_p$), transition susceptibility ($\chi$), and the local dispersion velocity.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The coupling strength ($J$), energy ($\epsilon$), wave number ($k$), decay constant ($\kappa$), and the reduced Compton wavelength ($\lambda_C/c$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** For relativistic extensions, an auxiliary two-stream model is introduced for evanescent states. The definition of $v_p$ depends on the placement of the coupling evaluation window.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the general coupling scheme; Figure 2 shows the ratio $v_p/v$ for a standing wave at various coupling positions ($x_c$). Figure 3 plots $v_p/c$ versus the Lorentz factor ($\gamma$) for both propagating and evanescent regimes.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the concept of passage speed via susceptibility in general settings (including standing waves). It then extends this framework to a relativistic model involving coupled Klein-Gordon waveguides, deriving specific formulas for $v_p$ in both propagating and decaying regimes.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We characterize quantum motion based on the susceptibility of a quantum state to weak local state conversion. The inverse susceptibility can be interpreted as a passage speed characterizing local motion independently of probability transport. For individual WKB branches, it reproduces the magnitude of the local dispersion velocity in both propagating and evanescent regions. A relativistic extension yields a passage speed bounded by the speed of light, reaching this bound at the finite energy corresponding to the center of the mass gap, where the evanescent decay length equals the reduced Compton wavelength. This identifies the reduced Compton wavelength as the shortest stationary evanescent length scale compatible with relativistic quantum motion.

</details>
