**Summary.** The study uses Raman spectroscopy and DFT calculations on $	ext{Na}_{0.5}	ext{CoO}_2$ to investigate the sodium-ion lattice dynamics. They identify a temperature $T^* \sim 300	ext{ K}$ where sodium ions undergo a dynamical-to-static crossover, suggesting this structural freezing is crucial for establishing the subsequent electronic and magnetic order in the cobaltate layers.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a direct spectroscopic probe of ion dynamics that precedes electronic ordering in a strongly correlated material, offering insights into how structural degrees of freedom can gate emergent quantum phases.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the temperature-driven dynamical-to-static crossover of sodium ions within the zig-zag ordered phase of $	ext{Na}_{0.5}	ext{CoO}_2$. The goal is to link this ion dynamics to emergent electronic and magnetic orders in the cobaltate layer.

**Main result.** The authors detected two novel sodium phonon modes appearing below $T^* \sim 300	ext{ K}$, interpreting this anomaly as a crossover where gradual sodium ion freezing sets the stage for subsequent CoO$_2$ ordering.

**Method.** A combination of polarization-resolved Raman spectroscopy and first-principles DFT calculations (including Hubbard U corrections) was employed to analyze lattice dynamics.

**Model / system.** The physical system is $	ext{Na}_{0.5}	ext{CoO}_2$, a layered cobaltate compound known for complex temperature-dependent electronic and magnetic transitions ($	ext{AFM}$ transition at $87	ext{ K}$).

**Key observables.** Sodium phonon mode frequencies, linewidth broadening vs. temperature, in-plane resistivity $ho(T)$, and magnetic susceptibility $\chi(T)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** $T^* \sim 300	ext{ K}$ (crossover temperature), $T_{	ext{S}} \sim 460	ext{ K}$ (zigzag ordering temperature), Hubbard U parameter ($2.5	ext{ eV}$ chosen for best fit).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the observed phonon anomalies at $T^*$ represent a true dynamical-to-static crossover, and that DFT calculations within the harmonic approximation capture the essential physics despite discrepancies in high-frequency modes.

**Figures summary.** Figures show temperature dependence of Raman spectra across various geometries; Figure 1 illustrates $ho(T)$ and $\chi(T)$ showing transitions near $300	ext{ K}$; phonon dispersion plots confirm Na-dominant low-energy modes.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically presents experimental measurements (Raman, transport) to identify anomalous temperature regimes ($T^*$), followed by theoretical calculations (DFT/Phonopy) to assign physical origins to the observed lattice vibrations and link them mechanistically to known magnetic transitions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We employ polarization-resolved Raman spectroscopy combined with first-principles calculations to study the sodium-ion lattice dynamics in a sodium zig-zag ordered cobaltate compound Na$_{0.5}$CoO$_2$. We detect two sodium phonon modes for the first time, and their mode frequencies are consistent with first-principles phonon calculations based on an orthorhombic unit cell. We find that they appear below around $T^*\sim300\pm50$K with large linewidth broadening, much lower than the sodium zig-zag ordering temperature $T_\text{S}\sim460$K, and then narrow at lower temperatures. We interpret the sodium-phonon anomalies occurring at $T^*$ as a dynamical-to-static crossover involving mainly the motion of sodium ions. Our results suggest that the gradual freezing of the sodium ions and the well-defined static sodium-zigzag order below $T^*$ set the stage for the emergent electronic and magnetic orders in the CoO$_2$ layer of Na$_{0.5}$CoO$_2$.

</details>

### [Classical and quantum mechanics across representations: an operational reading of the Wigner Weyl correspondence](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13135v1)

**Authors:** Samuel Schlegel, Borivoje Dakić, Flavio Del Santo  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.13135v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks
**Topic relevance:** `Keldysh / 2PI / non-Gaussian methods` **1/5** · `analog quantum simulation` **1/5** · `correlated / nonlocal dissipation` **1/5** · `driven-dissipative phase transition` **1/5** · `entanglement & information structure` **1/5** · `methods for driven-dissipative` **1/5** · `non-equilibrium dynamics` **1/5**

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>

<img src="2607.13135_figures/2607.13135_fig2.jpg" alt="Figure 2">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 2.</b> FIG. 2. The entanglement hierarchy of Ref. [13] located on the classical/quantum state spaces of Fig. 1. Representational entanglement (RE): a classical density f ≥0 whose Hilbert- space image ˆρf is not positive, so covariance witnesses are ap- plied outside their domain. Hybrid entanglement (HE): states that are positive and entangled yet Wigner-positive, repro- ducible by a classical phase-space model at the quadrature level (C ∩Q). Genuine entanglement (GE): positive, entan- gled, and Wigner-negative, admitting no classical phase-space description.</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously compares classical and quantum mechanics by analyzing their equivalent representations in phase space and Hilbert space via the Wigner-Weyl correspondence. It argues that while the mathematical mapping is systematic, genuine physical distinctions—such as noncommutativity—are robust features, whereas artifacts like negativity are often merely consequences of choosing a specific representation language.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a deep structural comparison between classical and quantum descriptions, which is foundational for understanding the limits of semi-classical approximations used widely in AMO physics (e.g., Wigner function analysis).

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To systematically compare classical and quantum mechanics across different mathematical representations (phase space vs. Hilbert space) to distinguish between representation-dependent artifacts and robust, physically significant structural differences.

**Main result.** The core physical structure is governed by noncommutativity, which manifests as the $\hbar$-dependent Moyal $\star$-product in phase space, rather than being tied to a preferred mathematical language (PS or HS).

**Method.** The authors use the Wigner-Weyl correspondence to map between classical phase-space distributions and quantum operators, developing comparisons across states, kinematics, dynamics, and measurements.

**Model / system.** The paper analyzes general classical and quantum mechanical theories, focusing on how their descriptions translate when moving between phase space ($\Gamma$) and Hilbert space. The comparison is formalized using the Wigner transform and Weyl quantization map.

**Key observables.** Noncommutativity, $\hbar$-dependent $\star$-deformation of the algebra, positivity constraints (negativity in Wigner function), outcome statistics, and state-update rules.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Planck's constant ($\hbar$), which dictates the transition from classical Poisson brackets to quantum Moyal brackets.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes that the Weyl map provides an invertible translation at the level of symbols, but it explicitly notes that this map fails to preserve physical constraints like positivity.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically shows the partial overlap between classical and quantum state spaces under the WW correspondence. Figure 2 illustrates a hierarchy of entanglement (RE $ightarrow$ HE $ightarrow$ GE) based on positivity requirements.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by establishing the mathematical machinery (WW map, Moyal product), comparing formalisms across different physical processes (kinematics, dynamics, measurement), and finally developing an operational hierarchy to distinguish genuine quantum features from representational artifacts like negativity or entanglement types.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The physical content of a theory is not intrinsically tied to any single mathematical formalism. Both classical and quantum mechanics admit equivalent representations, notably in phase space and in Hilbert space, related by the Wigner-Weyl correspondence. While this correspondence has long been studied in mathematical physics, its foundational and operational implications are often left implicit. Here we give a systematic account of what changes, and what does not, when classical and quantum theories are expressed in each other's native language. This representational viewpoint separates artifacts (such as the appearance of non-positivity or negativity under certain maps) from robust structural distinctions that persist across representations, in particular noncommutativity and its $\hbar$-dependent $\star$-deformation of the classical algebra. We develop the comparison at the level of states, kinematics, and dynamics, and extend it to measurement by formulating both outcome statistics and state-update rules within the same framework.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces StreamingQEC, a novel system simulator designed to model the resource demands of fault-tolerant Quantum Error Correction (QEC) pipelines. It moves beyond simple circuit simulation by explicitly modeling classical resource contention across CPUs, GPUs, and interconnects. By using advanced techniques like certified recurrence, it achieves massive speedups while accurately predicting performance bottlenecks critical for building large-scale quantum computers.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides crucial system-level performance data that bridges the gap between theoretical quantum circuit design and practical hardware implementation constraints, which is vital for scaling any physical quantum computer.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge is understanding resource contention and queuing across diverse classical resources (controller, compute, accelerator) during the continuous hybrid pipeline of fault-tolerant Quantum Error Correction (QEC). Existing simulators fail to model these system-level interactions.

**Main result.** The proposed simulator achieves a 24.0x speedup over explicit simulation while accurately reproducing performance metrics across thousands of calibrated configurations, revealing critical bottlenecks like transfer limits and decoder stalls.

**Method.** It introduces StreamingQEC, which uses an explicit Discrete-Event Simulation (DES) as a reference, supplemented by an auto staged-fluid mode for fast screening and a certified recurrence mechanism for state compression.

**Model / system.** The system models the classical workload induced by protected logical quantum computation intervals ($\mathcal{I}_g$), which involves sequential QEC stages: readout, syndrome transport, decoding, feedback, and control. This is mapped onto a resource-constrained pipeline involving CPUs, GPUs, and dedicated decoders.

**Key observables.** Simulated completion time (makespan), stage wait/busy times, queue pressure, backlog metrics, and resource utilization across various interconnects (PCIe, NVLink).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Microsecond-scale QEC cycle timings; specific performance profiles derived from 9,998 measured decoder-runtime events.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The simulation abstracts away quantum state evolution and logical error rate validation. The recurrence mechanism relies on matching scheduling states and metric contributions for compression.

**Figures summary.** Figures summarize the system model mapping protected computation to resource-bound QEC stages, while tables detail grounded decoder/runtime evidence (e.g., Surface MWPM, qLDPC) and link performance parameters.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the need for system-level modeling in fault-tolerant QEC pipelines. It then details StreamingQEC's three execution modes (DES, staged-fluid, recurrence). The core is presenting the methodology for grounding runtime profiles from extensive decoder measurements and applying this to analyze resource bottlenecks.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Fault-tolerant quantum computing requires a continuous hybrid quantum error correction (QEC) pipeline comprising measurement readout, syndrome transport, decoding, feedback, and control. Existing QEC simulators primarily evaluate circuits, noise models, decoders, and protocol-level outcomes. System architects, however, must also understand how these workloads contend for and queue across controller, compute, accelerator, and communication resources during protected logical execution. We introduce StreamingQEC, a system-level simulator that translates fault-tolerant logical workloads into resource-constrained streaming-QEC pipelines. An explicit discrete-event simulation provides the reference execution semantics. An automatic staged-fluid mode enables faster approximate design-space exploration, while a certified recurrence mechanism compresses repeated transitions only when their scheduling state and metric contributions match those of the explicit execution trace. We assemble a decoder-runtime dataset containing 9,998 measurements, of which 8,174 are used to fit performance profiles. Recurrence reproduces the reported explicit-simulation metrics across 35 calibrated-profile configurations, as well as additional workload and cadence validation cases. For a 16-job anchor workload, it preserves 59,743,936 decoding events while achieving a 24.0x host-side speedup, and recurrent simulations scale beyond 1.22 billion events. Across 17 reference configurations, the automatics taged-fluid mode yields a mean makespan error of 2.60% and a worst-case error of 6.45%. Design-space studies reveal transfer-limited resource matching,decoder-driven pipeline stalls, and saturation of dedicated resources under microsecond-scale QEC cycles.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces two novel numerical techniques, Filon and Controlled Filon methods, designed to efficiently simulate the time evolution of quantum systems with highly oscillatory behavior. By adapting Filon quadrature for oscillatory integrals, the authors drastically reduce the required number of simulation timesteps compared to established methods like Hermite integration. This makes accurate classical control simulations feasible for superconducting qubits.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful algorithmic tool for simulating complex quantum dynamics where standard integrators fail due to rapid oscillations, directly impacting computational feasibility in quantum control theory.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core challenge is achieving fast and accurate classical simulation of quantum systems whose dynamics are governed by highly oscillatory time-dependent Hamiltonians.

**Main result.** The developed Controlled Filon method significantly reduces the computational cost compared to standard methods, outperforming benchmarks like the Hermite method by factors up to 500x for simulating gates like CNOT.

**Method.** The authors adapt Filon quadrature, traditionally used for oscillatory integrals, into two specialized time-stepping schemes (Filon and Controlled Filon) tailored for solving linear systems of ODEs arising from quantum evolution.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on controlled quantum systems modeled by the time-dependent Schrödinger equation ($rac{d}{dt}\psi(t) = -iH(t)\psi(t)$), specifically tested using superconducting transmon qubits and CNOT gate simulations.

**Key observables.** Accuracy of simulation (convergence rate) for quantum state evolution, particularly when simulating controlled gates like the CNOT gate.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The time step size ($\Delta t$), the oscillation frequencies ($\omega_j$), and the order ($s$) of the interpolation polynomial used in the quadrature rule are critical parameters.

**Assumptions / limitations.** A key assumption is that the envelope function $f(t)$ varies more slowly than the carrier wave $e^{i\omega t}$ when $|\omega| > 0$. The methods are derived assuming specific mathematical structures related to the Hamiltonian evolution.

**Figures summary.** Figure 2.1 compares convergence for the Rabi oscillator, showing that Filon/Controlled Filon achieve target accuracy with much larger time steps ($\Delta t$) than the Hermite method.

**Paper structure.** The paper first defines the problem (highly oscillatory quantum simulation), then details the adaptation of Filon quadrature theory into two numerical methods, followed by presenting and analyzing these methods through numerical simulations on physical systems like the Rabi oscillator and CNOT gate.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Fast and accurate classical simulation of quantum systems is a central challenge in the design and control of quantum computers, but the highly oscillatory dynamics of these systems severely limit the efficiency of standard numerical methods. To address this, we adapt Filon quadrature for oscillatory integrals into two numerical methods, called Filon and Controlled Filon, for solving linear systems of ODEs with highly oscillatory solutions. We tailor both methods for efficient implementation in controlled quantum systems, and the Controlled Filon method additionally accounts for the oscillatory structure of the control pulses. We show by numerical experiments that these methods significantly reduce the computational cost of accurately simulating systems of superconducting transmon qubits by decreasing the number of timesteps needed to reach a given level of precision, with only a modest increase in the cost per timestep. For a realistic simulation of the dynamics of a CNOT gate, the Controlled Filon method is the most efficient method tested at every target accuracy, outperforming the best Hermite method by up to 6x and the Hermite method of the same order by up to 500x.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces HybridQC, a novel simulator designed to model the entire topology of tightly integrated hybrid quantum-classical compute units (HCUs). It moves beyond simulating isolated components by treating controllers and communication links as first-class resources in a configurable graph. The work demonstrates that system performance is governed by complex resource contention and scheduling limits, providing a systematic framework for predicting scaling bottlenecks before physical hardware deployment.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on classical simulation infrastructure, the detailed analysis of resource contention, scheduling policies, and bottlenecks in hybrid quantum systems provides crucial insights into the practical limitations that any future quantum algorithm implementation must overcome.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The performance of hybrid quantum-classical applications is increasingly bottlenecked by classical control, communication overhead, and scheduling rather than the quantum computation itself.

**Main result.** System scaling is highly non-uniform; balanced hardware scaling provides only modest speedups, while workload growth in specific dimensions (like circuit count) can lead to exponential runtime increases.

**Method.** The authors developed HybridQC, a topology-aware discrete-event simulator that models the entire coupled system rather than individual components.

**Model / system.** The core model is the Hybrid Compute Unit (HCU), represented as a configurable graph containing classical processors, memory, controllers, QA devices, and DQC devices connected by explicit communication links. The simulation tracks resource occupancy across this entire topology.

**Key observables.** Makespan (end-to-end time), Controller Critical-Path Share (CPF), resource utilization, queueing delays, and scaling factors for various workloads.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Workload dimensions (e.g., input data size, circuit count, shot count) and hardware topology parameters (link bandwidth, controller capacity).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The simulator assumes that architectural changes can be modeled by editing the resource graph, allowing for predictive analysis of future integrated systems.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the configurable HCU topology and show how different workloads exhibit bottleneck migration patterns (e.g., from QA to DQC or controllers) as resources scale.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces HybridQC by defining the HCU graph model, details its layered architecture (Workload $ightarrow$ Runtime-profile $ightarrow$ Topology $ightarrow$ Simulation), calibrates it against real hardware measurements, and then presents extensive workload scaling studies comparing cloud vs. integrated execution semantics.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Hybrid quantum-classical application performance is increasingly limited by classical control, host-to-QPU communication, and scheduling rather than quantum execution. Existing simulators and runtime interfaces analyze individual kernels but fail to address system-topology questions, such as controller bottlenecks, diminishing returns of QPU capacity, or resource contention under heterogeneous workloads. We introduce HybridQC, a topology-aware discrete-event simulator for tightly coupled hybrid compute units (HCUs). HybridQC models HCUs as configurable graphs of classical processors, memory, controllers, quantum annealing (QA) and digital quantum computing (DQC) devices, and communication links. It decomposes jobs into typed, directed acyclic graphs of stages, ranging from input preparation to classical postprocessing, executed under interchangeable scheduling policies. Calibrated with live measurements from D-Wave (Advantage 1 and 2) and IBM (Kingston, Marrakesh, and Fez) processors, HybridQC distinguishes physical QPU occupancy from cloud wall-clock latency. The models achieve mean absolute percentage errors of 3.92%-8.04% for D-Wave QPU access time and 5.26%-19.01% for IBM quantum-seconds measurements. Workload experiments reveal that a balanced 10x HCU scaling improves makespan by only 2.19x-3.42x, while altering scheduling policies shifts makespan by up to 1.80x for a 20-job workload. Scalability varies heavily by workload dimension: a 100x input data increase yields a 306 s median runtime, whereas a 100x joint increase in circuit count, shot count, and circuit depth drives runtime to 4.806x10^7 s on an unchanged HCU. HybridQC offers a systematic framework for evaluating the topology, scheduling, and scaling limits of hybrid architectures prior to physical deployment.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper addresses the challenge of local minima trapping in quantum annealing by introducing diagonal catalysts. These catalysts are constructed using graph path patterns and modify the system's Hamiltonian to enforce a better correlation between low energy and physical proximity to the solution. The results show that these engineered landscapes significantly boost optimization performance across various problem instances.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a concrete, mathematically derived mechanism to engineer quantum annealing Hamiltonians, which is highly relevant for understanding how engineered couplings can guide quantum computation away from local minima traps.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** Quantum annealing performance is limited by population trapping in local minima far from the true solution on the energy landscape.

**Main result.** The authors introduce diagonal catalysts that reshape the energy landscape, significantly improving the correlation between low energy and proximity to the ground state.

**Method.** They construct catalysts using a mathematical framework based on propagating patterns along self-avoiding paths on the coupling graph. These catalysts are added to the problem Hamiltonian during quantum annealing.

**Model / system.** The system is modeled by an Ising spin system undergoing adiabatic evolution governed by $H(s) = -(1 - s)\sum X_i + s (H_P + C(m))$, where $H_P$ is the original problem Hamiltonian and $C(m)$ is the constructed catalyst.

**Key observables.** Top-band mass ($P_{0.95}$), probability mass at small Hamming distance ($\delta \leq 2$), and paired gain relative to baseline anneals.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Catalyst order ($m=2$ to $m=5$), sweep time ($T=2, 6, 10$), and graph regularity (3-, 4-, 5-regular graphs).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The constructed catalyst is typically denser than the original problem Hamiltonian, and there is a trade-off between catalyst order and fidelity to the true ground state.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate how catalysts rescale energy bands (improving energy–distance correspondence) and show probability redistribution on both energy and Hamming distance for various catalyst orders and sweep times.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops a mathematical framework connecting energy and Hamming distance, derives the construction method for diagonal catalysts via path propagation, and then benchmarks these catalysts across multiple graph families using quantitative diagnostics in energy and Hamming space.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum annealing is often limited by population trapped in local minima many spin flips from the solution. We introduce a mathematical framework to understand the connection between energy and Hamming distance in optimization problems. Using this, we build ZZ-catalysts from ground-state patterns of small frustration-free subproblems that make configurations far from the solution less energetically competitive. On sparse problems they multiply the near-solution probability at short sweeps, with gains persisting on fully-connected models and tunable via subproblem choice.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper details the design and characterization of novel 'sail-like' $	ext{Si}_3	ext{N}_4$ membranes for use in ultra-sensitive optomechanical accelerometers. By employing Bayesian optimization, the authors engineered resonators that significantly boost the quality factor times mass product while lowering the resonance frequency. The resulting monolithic device demonstrates sensitivity capable of resolving microgravity accelerations, advancing fundamental physics sensing capabilities.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant as it pushes the boundaries of mechanical sensing by combining advanced materials science ($	ext{Si}_3	ext{N}_4$), optimization theory (Bayesian methods), and quantum/optomechanical measurement techniques to reach fundamental force detection limits.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is developing highly sensitive optomechanical accelerometers by engineering strained membrane resonators to achieve a superior combination of low resonance frequency and high Q-mass product.

**Main result.** The authors designed 'sail-like' $	ext{Si}_3	ext{N}_4$ membranes that significantly increase the $Q 	imes m$ product while lowering the fundamental frequency, achieving sensitivities sufficient to resolve microgravity accelerations.

**Method.** The study combines theoretical modeling (deriving noise equations based on geometry and stress) with computational optimization using Bayesian methods to guide the design of optimal membrane geometries.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a strained $	ext{Si}_3	ext{N}_4$ membrane resonator, optimized into a sail-like shape. The final device integrates this sail onto a nanoribbon acting as an inertial reference plane for monolithic cavity optomechanical sensing.

**Key observables.** Thermal acceleration noise ($S_{	ext{th}}^a$), resonance frequency ($f$), Quality factor ($Q$), and displacement/acceleration imprecision ($	ext{S}_{	ext{imp}x}, 	ext{S}_{	ext{imp}a}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Room temperature thermal noise of $40\;	ext{n}g_0/\sqrt{	ext{Hz}}$; achieving $\mu	ext{g}_0/\sqrt{	ext{Hz}}$ resolution over a 4 kHz bandwidth.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on modeling the mechanical response using continuum mechanics equations and assumes that thermal noise is the limiting factor, although discrepancies with measured Q-factors were noted.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare $Q 	imes m$ vs frequency for different membrane types, detail the Bayesian optimization process used to find optimal parameters, and show experimental characterization of resonance frequencies and quality factors.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from identifying the need for improved resonator design (low $f$, high $Qm$) to applying Bayesian optimization on sail geometries. It then presents measurements on optimized single sails and finally demonstrates a monolithic double-membrane setup for acceleration sensing.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Strained membrane resonators have emerged as a promising platform for optomechanical accelerometry; however, the desired combination of low frequency and high $Q$-mass product requires a rethinking of their dissipation dilution engineering. Applying Bayesian optimization to a Si$_3$N$_4$ membrane, we discover a class of sail-like trampoline resonators in which the frequency is decreased by an order of magnitude while preserving the $Q$-mass product. We demonstrate centimeter-scale sails with kHz frequencies, $Q\sim10^7$ and $Q\times\text{mass}\sim$ 10 g. Vertically integrating a 7 kHz device with a nanoribbon, we realize a monolithic cavity optomechanical accelerometer with a room temperature thermal noise of $40\;\text{n}g_0/\sqrt{\text{Hz}}$, sufficient to resolve $μg_0/\sqrt{\text{Hz}}$ ambient vibration over a bandwidth of 4 kHz with a displacement imprecision of $10^{-14}\;\text{m}/\sqrt{\text{Hz}}$. Cryogenic arrays of sail membranes may be attractive for new physics searches and distributed quantum sensing experiments.

</details>

**Authors:** Kevin Bogner, Aysajan Abidin, Dave Singelee, Bart Preneel  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** quantum information and computing · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.13464v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

<div class="figure-strip-hint">↔ Scroll figures horizontally</div>

<figure class="paper-figure-card">
<img src="2607.13464_figures/2607.13464_fig1.jpg" alt="Figure 1">
<figcaption class="paper-figure-caption"><b>Fig 1.</b> Figure 1: Reduced mafia-fraud SDP game. The adversary’s comb S first interacts with the prover via the pre-ask channel Nω, then answers the verifier’s challenge ρω. The branch-dependent triple (Nω, ρω, Πω), with Πω the verifier’s accept operator, combines into a single tester operator Wω that captures everything outside the adversary’s control on branch ω. The acceptance probability is then Tr(WωS), a linear function of the comb S; this linearity is what makes the optimization an SDP.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously analyzes the security of Quantum Distance-Bounding protocols by modeling them as one-round fraud games. Using Semidefinite Programming, they compute exact attack success probabilities for discrete variables, demonstrating that Mafia-Fraud resistance can distinguish between different QDB designs. This work advances the theoretical understanding of physical distance verification in quantum communication.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides rigorous mathematical tools (SDP) to analyze security bounds in quantum communication protocols, which is foundational knowledge for quantum cryptography and secure quantum networking.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper evaluates the security of Quantum Distance-Bounding (QDB) protocols by isolating and studying one-round distance-fraud (DF) and mafia-fraud (MF) games.

**Main result.** For discrete variables, SDPs allow for exact computation of MF attack values, showing that MF clearly separates studied QDB protocols. The overall resistance depends on whether an attacker can use early information to answer fresh challenges.

**Method.** The authors reduce the one-round DF and MF games to convex optimization problems solvable exactly using Semidefinite Programming (SDP) for discrete variables, while employing calibrated Gaussian models for continuous variables.

**Model / system.** The study focuses on QDB protocols that use timed quantum communication in a fast phase to bound physical distance. The analysis covers both discrete-variable and continuous-variable constructions.

**Key observables.** Success probability of the best single-round attack ($p_\star^{MF}$ or $p_\star^{DF}$) for various QDB protocols.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The one-round nature of the fast phase, which simplifies the optimization problem compared to full quantum position verification.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to a single round; for continuous variables, it uses a finite-grid approximation due to intractable non-convexity.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the flow diagrams of specific QDB protocols (e.g., QDB 2017, E91 QDB) detailing verifier/prover actions across multiple rounds.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the framework by isolating one-round games; it then details the SDP formulation for discrete variables and Gaussian estimation for continuous variables, presenting results via tables comparing calculated attack values to prior work.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum distance-bounding (QDB) protocols let a verifier check that a prover is both genuine and physically nearby. During a timed fast phase of quantum communication, the verifier measures round-trip times to obtain an upper bound on the prover's distance. For a uniform comparison, we isolate the fast phase and study one-round distance-fraud (DF) and mafia-fraud (MF) games. For discrete-variable QDB, we show that these games reduce to convex optimization problems and can therefore be solved exactly with semidefinite programming; each MF value comes with an explicit attack achieving it and a matching certificate that no attack does better. This contrasts with quantum position verification, where an attack is split between two separated parties, so its optimization is nonconvex and analyses rely on relaxations. In our MF game, the cooperating pair collapses to a single sequential strategy, which keeps the game convex and its exact value computable. Across the discrete-variable protocols we examine, the best one-round DF attack succeeds with the same probability ($1/2$) for every protocol, whereas MF clearly separates the protocols. For continuous-variable QDB, we report estimated attack success probabilities from a calibrated Gaussian attack model. The benchmark covers protocols whose fast phase itself authenticates the prover; designs that follow Brands and Chaum and instead bind the fast phase with a final authenticated message, like the earliest QDB proposal, fall outside it and are treated separately. Of the four protocols studied, two had no previously known one-round attack values, and we report the first ones; for the other two, we find MF attacks with higher success probability than previously reported. Overall, one-round MF resistance depends on whether an attacker can use information revealed early by the prover to answer a fresh challenge from the verifier.

</details>

**Summary.** This work analyzes the failure points in approximating quantum information measures, specifically the CHSH functional, using the NPA hierarchy. It establishes that the required level of approximation undergoes a sharp phase transition at a critical tilt parameter. The failure mechanism is mathematically characterized as an obstruction arising from noncommutative geometry rather than simple polynomial degree limitations.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a deep mathematical understanding of when quantum bounds can be rigorously proven using semi-definite programming techniques (moment problems), linking computational limitations to fundamental algebraic structures.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper investigates the phase transition governing the exactness of the NPA hierarchy when approximating the quantum maximum of a doubly-tilted CHSH functional.

**Main result.** It proves that the hierarchy is exact in the supercritical regime ($\alpha, eta \ge 1$) but fails to be exactly certifiable near the critical line due to an obstruction rooted in noncommutative geometry.

**Method.** The analysis employs advanced mathematical tools including moment matrix theory, rational certificates, second-order perturbation analysis, and geometric conditions like the boundary Hessian.

**Model / system.** The model is based on optimizing the doubly-tilted CHSH functional ($B_{\alphaeta}$) for quantum measurements, analyzed via its connection to the NPA hierarchy of moment problems.

**Key observables.** The quantum maximum value $c_Q(s)$, the overshooting coefficients $a_k$, and the required level of approximation $k$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The tilt parameter $s = 2 - \alpha - eta$, which controls proximity to the critical line $\alpha + eta = 2$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on comparing noncommutative moment structures against commutative algebraic geometry bounds (Positivstellensatz).

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the asymptotic behavior of $c_Q(s)$, analyzes the quadratic overshoot of NPA levels, proves exactness in the supercritical region via rational certificates, and identifies the phase transition mechanism using geometric conditions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Gigena et al. [npj Quantum Inf. 11, 82 (2025)] proved the exact quantum maximum of the doubly-tilted CHSH functional $B_{αβ}=α\langle A_0\rangle+β\langle B_0\rangle+\mathrm{CHSH}$ and observed that the NPA level required to reach it grows without evident bound toward the critical line $α+β=2$. We quantify the mechanism on the symmetric slice $s=2-α-β$: (i) the quantum value leaves the local bound cubically, $c_Q=4-s+s^3/6-s^4/36+O(s^5)$; (ii) each NPA level overshoots quadratically, $c_k(s)=4-s+a_k s^2+O(s^3)$, with the almost-quantum coefficient computed exactly, $a_{1+AB}=3/64$; (iii) the divergence of the required exact level is equivalent to positivity of the single sequence $(a_k)$ - proven for every $k$ in the companion paper. We prove the supercritical side completely: for all $α,β\ge 1$ and every level the hierarchy is exact, via three explicit rational certificates realizing an affine identity. The hierarchy's exactness thus undergoes a phase transition at the critical line. On the subcritical side we certify the first four levels in exact arithmetic (rational pseudo-moments beating $c_Q$, confirmed by Sturm's theorem). We identify the exact mechanism: rescaled to the critical corner, the limiting obstruction is the Motzkin polynomial, the classical nonnegative-but-not-sum-of-squares form, so the finite-level failure sits in the restricted-certificate regime. The phase boundary has a precise geometric reading via Nie's finite-convergence theorem and Marshall's boundary Hessian condition: a self-tested optimum is finitely NPA-certifiable whenever its boundary Hessian is nondegenerate (contact order two), which holds for the single tilt and fails exactly at the doubly-tilted cubic touch. Three verified errata in the published polynomial system of Gigena et al. are documented.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents Group Reservoir Computing, a novel method for synthesizing quantum gates by translating the complex unitary evolution problem into solving nonlinear ordinary differential equations in Wei–Norman coordinates. By using an Echo State Network trained on these coordinates, it bypasses computationally expensive full-system simulations while mathematically guaranteeing that the resulting gate remains perfectly unitary.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful machine learning framework that respects fundamental physical constraints (unitarity via WN decomposition), offering a computationally efficient alternative to standard optimal control methods for quantum hardware implementation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is efficiently synthesizing quantum gates by finding time-dependent control fields that drive a quantum system to a desired unitary transformation.

**Main result.** The proposed method achieves high fidelity (mean 0.94) on single-qubit gates and significantly accelerates the convergence of gradient-based optimization techniques by providing an accurate warm start.

**Method.** It combines Lie theory (Wei–Norman decomposition) to map the unitary evolution problem onto a set of scalar nonlinear ordinary differential equations, which are then solved using Reservoir Computing (specifically, an Echo State Network).

**Model / system.** The system is a controlled quantum evolution governed by a Hamiltonian $H(t) = H_0 + \sum u_k(t) X_k$, aiming to synthesize unitary gates on finite-dimensional hardware platforms.

**Key observables.** Gate fidelity ($F$), convergence iteration count, and the coordinate error ($\| \xi - \hat{\xi} \|$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The time interval $[0, T]$, the control class $V$, and the structure of the underlying Lie algebra.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key assumptions include assuming Lipschitz continuity for the dynamics in a compact set K and that the surrogate vector field error is bounded ($\|\mathbf{f} - \hat{\mathbf{f}}\| \le \varepsilon$).

**Figures summary.** Figures compare convergence speed (iteration counts) between warm-start, cold-start, and plain gradient ascent methods for standard gates; another figure shows fidelity vs. input scaling.

**Paper structure.** The paper first introduces the problem and the WN decomposition to reduce the dynamics to scalar ODEs. It then details using Reservoir Computing (ESN) to learn the control-to-coordinate map, followed by theoretical error bounding and empirical validation showing convergence speed improvements.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Quantum gate synthesis is essential for implementing quantum algorithms on real hardware, yet existing methods are often computationally demanding. Here, we introduce a novel approach based on reservoir computing, which we name Group Reservoir Computing, an efficient machine-learning paradigm for learning temporal dynamics whose training reduces to a single linear regression, to reduce the resources required. The method is grounded in the Wei--Norman decomposition, which provides a compact description of the evolution. We prove that the reconstructed dynamics always remain unitary by construction and derive formal error bounds that establish the theoretical validity of the strategy. On the standard single-qubit gate set the trained network produces a control pulse in a single pass, with mean fidelity 0.94 across the eight benchmark gates; used to warm-start gradient-based optimization, it roughly halves the number of iterations that plain gradient ascent needs to reach a target fidelity, so that the relevant figure of merit is the time to reach that threshold rather than the final accuracy after a fixed budget. Owing to its general formulation, the method applies to any finite-dimensional hardware platform; the route to multiqubit synthesis is discussed in the closing section.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper theoretically proves that axial phonons can acquire a d-wave angular momentum texture in orbital altermagnets using only electronic structure effects (via the molecular Berry curvature). This bypasses the need for spin-orbit coupling, opening new avenues to engineer exotic phonon functionalities like angular-momentum Seebeck effects.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work connects orbital magnetism (a strongly correlated electron phenomenon) directly to lattice dynamics via Berry curvature, providing a mechanism for engineering quantized angular momentum transport in materials without needing relativistic SOC terms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To theoretically demonstrate that axial phonons can host a d-wave angular momentum texture in orbital altermagnets without relying on spin-orbit coupling.

**Main result.** The authors show the electronic state's d-wave orbital magnetic moment texture is transferred to the lattice vibrations (phonons) via the molecular Berry curvature, enabling new platforms for engineering axial phonons.

**Method.** The study employs the Born-Oppenheimer approximation and analyzes phonon dynamics by diagonalizing an effective Hamiltonian derived from electron-phonon coupling mediated by the Molecular Berry Curvature (MBC).

**Model / system.** The system is an orbital altermagnet on a checkerboard lattice, modeled with a minimal tight-binding Hamiltonian exhibiting d-wave loop-current order that breaks time-reversal symmetry.

**Key observables.** Axial phonons carrying finite angular momentum; the $d$-wave orbital magnetic moment texture ($m_{z,k}$); and measurable effects like angular-momentum Seebeck and splitter effects.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The analysis is restricted to the insulating regime (V=0) and relies on the presence of d-wave symmetry breaking in the electronic state.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key approximations include using the Born-Oppenheimer approximation, assuming a minimal tight-binding model, and utilizing Slater determinant forms for basis states.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the orbital almagnet structure on the lattice, the momentum-resolved orbital magnetic moment texture ($m_{z,k}$), and the calculated Molecular Berry Curvature ($	ext{G}_{\alpha\kappaeta}(\mathbf{k})$) in momentum space.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from establishing the electronic model and its d-wave texture to calculating the MBC using the BO approximation. It then applies this MBC to derive the phonon Hamiltonian, culminating in the diagonalization of the effective Hamiltonian to predict measurable phonon splitting effects.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We theoretically demonstrate that axial phonons, lattice vibration quanta carrying finite angular momentum, can host a $d$-wave angular momentum texture in orbital altermagnets in the absence of spin-orbit coupling. We consider a minimal electronic tight-binding model with $d$-wave loop-current order that breaks time-reversal symmetry. Within the Born-Oppenheimer approximation, we incorporate electron-phonon coupling via the molecular Berry curvature and show that the underlying $d$-wave orbital magnetic moment texture of the electronic state is transferred to the phonons without requiring the relativistic spin-orbit coupling. Our results expand the range of platforms available for engineering axial phonons and point to functionality unique to $d$-wave textures, including angular-momentum Seebeck and splitter effects, corresponding to longitudinal and transverse angular-momentum currents driven by a temperature gradient.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper rigorously proves that for a specific Bell inequality functional near its critical point, no finite level of the NPA hierarchy can exactly capture the true quantum maximum value. This is shown by constructing explicit mathematical counterexamples demonstrating an unavoidable overshoot at every finite relaxation level. It establishes fundamental limitations on using polynomial moment methods to characterize quantum correlations.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides rigorous mathematical proof regarding the limitations of polynomial relaxations (like those used in semidefinite programming bounds) when applied to fundamental quantum correlations, suggesting inherent gaps between classical/algebraic bounds and true quantum mechanics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To determine if any finite level of the Navascués–Pironio–Acín (NPA) hierarchy is exact for the doubly-tilted CHSH functional near a critical tilt.

**Main result.** The authors prove that no finite NPA level is exact in any neighborhood of the critical point because the quantum value leaves the local bound only cubically, causing all finite levels to strictly overshoot.

**Method.** The proof uses a primal construction involving analyzing moment curves and structural laws derived from relation lattices. It combines advanced algebraic techniques with computer-assisted verification using exact rational arithmetic.

**Model / system.** The analysis concerns the Bell scenario quantified by doubly-tilted CHSH functionals, which are related to quantum correlations measured via moments of Pauli operators in a specific limit (the critical tilt).

**Key observables.** The level-$k$ NPA value ($c_k(s)$) compared against the true quantum maximum value ($c_Q(s)$), and the coefficients $a_k$ characterizing the deviation.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The small parameter $s$, which measures the distance from the critical tilt ($\alpha + eta = 2$), and the NPA level index $k$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The proof relies on one external input: the published quantum value of Gigena et al., which was cross-checked by the authors.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops structural laws (L1, L2, L4) governing the NPA hierarchy using relation lattices and moment tensors. It then uses these structures to construct a feasible moment curve that demonstrates an unavoidable overshoot of the quantum bound near criticality.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Gigena, Panwar, Scala, Araujo, Farkas and Chaturvedi [npj Quantum Inf. 11, 82 (2025)] determined the quantum maximum of the doubly-tilted CHSH functionals, observed that the Navascues-Pironio-Acin level needed for exactness grows without evident bound toward the critical tilt, and asked whether any finite level suffices. We answer this in the negative. For the symmetric critical family $B_s=(1-s/2)(\langle A_0\rangle+\langle B_0\rangle)+\mathrm{CHSH}$ we prove: for every NPA level $k\ge 2$ there are an explicit rational $g_k>0$ and an $s^*_k>0$ with $c_k(s)\ge 4-s+g_k s^2$ on $(0,s^*_k]$; since the quantum value leaves the local bound only cubically, every finite level strictly overshoots on an interval: no finite level is exact on any neighbourhood of the critical point. Unconditionally $a_2>1/39$, $a_3>1/188$, $a_4>1/641$. The proof is a primal construction: an exactly feasible moment curve at each level, built from level-uniform structural laws and one level-independent signed witness -- a closed-form class function $y^*$ with $N_k^TΓ(y^*)N_k=u_k u_k^T$ at every level. The mechanism forces the sign: for $k\ge 3$ no quantum state and no smooth curve of quantum models can realize the gain direction, so the overshoot lives strictly in the non-quantum part of the NPA tangent cone. The proof is computer-assisted in the strict sense: finite exact-integer verifications with proven degree bounds are constituent parts of the argument; the chain has been re-verified against independent implementations, including a symbolic per-regime proof of the witness identity and a clean-room implementation written from the paper text alone. The one external input is the published quantum value of Gigena et al., cross-checked to twelve digits.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces Quantum Topological Data Encoding (QTDE), a general framework that encodes the topological structure of datasets into quantum states. It uses the combinatorial Laplacian as the generator for unitary quantum evolution, creating representations superior to classical methods. This advances quantum machine learning by providing a robust way to process high-dimensional data carrying complex geometric information.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges algebraic topology with quantum computation by providing a concrete mechanism to embed complex relational data structures into the quantum domain using spectral properties of graph Laplacians.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is developing a method to encode rich geometric and topological structure from classical datasets into quantum states for machine learning applications.

**Main result.** Quantum Topological Data Encoding (QTDE) successfully maps simplicial complexes to quantum states, showing that these representations outperform baselines derived from direct comparison of classical Laplacian descriptors.

**Method.** The framework uses topology-driven quantum evolution, governed by the combinatorial Laplacian matrix ($L_k$), to generate quantum states $|\Psi(T)angle$. This is generalized using Quantum Singular Value Transformation (QSVT) for tunable spectral filtering.

**Model / system.** The model involves mapping a dataset's structure (represented as a simplicial complex $K$) into a quantum state in a Hilbert space. The dynamics are dictated by the unitary evolution operator $U_k(t) = e^{-i L_k t}$, where $L_k$ is the combinatorial Laplacian.

**Key observables.** The primary observables include the resulting quantum state $|\Psi(T)angle$, the fidelity kernel $K = |\langle \Psi(T_1)|\Psi(T_2)angle|^2$, and explicit feature vectors derived from expectation values.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Key parameters involve the Laplacian dimension ($k$), time evolution parameter ($t$), and polynomial coefficients ($	heta$) used in QSVT.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The method assumes that guiding quantum encoding via the topology itself is beneficial, rather than just extracting invariants; explicit representations rely on selecting a finite set of observables $\{M_j\}$ and times $\{t_j\}$.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the QTDE workflow (Classical data -> Laplacian -> Evolution -> Quantum Representation) and compare performance metrics like fidelity kernels ($K_{ij}$) and cosine similarity matrices derived from different spectral filters.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces QTDE, details the use of the combinatorial Laplacian as a Hamiltonian generator for unitary evolution, proposes both implicit (kernel-based) and explicit (feature vector) representations, validates these methods on classification tasks, and discusses future directions like hardware implementation and complexity guarantees.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Many datasets encountered across a wide range of domains possess rich geometric and topological structure that is difficult to capture using conventional vector-based representations. Quantum machine learning offers the possibility of processing high-dimensional data in Hilbert spaces, but its practical success depends critically on how classical data is encoded into quantum states. We introduce \emph{quantum topological data encoding} (QTDE), a general framework for encoding topological information into quantum states via topology-driven quantum evolution. Our method generalises an existing topology-driven quantum encoding framework to higher-dimensional data. We test the proposed method on clique-complexes classification tasks, and provide preliminary evidence that topology-driven quantum representations can capture discriminative information beyond that available through direct comparisons of classical topological descriptors. The proposed quantum representations consistently outperform a baseline based on direct comparisons of the combinatorial Laplacians describing the underlying topological structure. We indicate several areas of application where the framework can be used to provide a more efficient and reliable data representation.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a framework to disentangle the geometric movement of probability amplitude from the constructive interference effects in quantum optimization. It shows that simple mixing alone is insufficient for finding solutions; true advantage requires engineering phases so that multiple computational paths interfere constructively at the target state. This offers a powerful diagnostic tool for designing robust quantum algorithms.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a rigorous analytical tool to diagnose *why* quantum algorithms fail or succeed in constrained settings, separating hardware/mixing limitations from algorithmic phase control requirements.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is separating geometric effects (amplitude transport) from quantum interference when analyzing constrained optimization algorithms.

**Main result.** Quantum sampling advantage requires coherent phase alignment among computational paths, allowing for a certified success probability independent of the Hilbert-space dimension or search space size.

**Method.** The authors develop a framework that analytically separates amplitude transport (geometry) from constructive interference (phase control) within an alternating quantum circuit structure.

**Model / system.** The model involves encoding constrained optimization problems (like routing/scheduling) into product spaces, governed by a mixing operator and subsequent diagonal phase layers.

**Key observables.** Target amplitude / Success Probability; Shell-resolved absolute path mass; Coherent interference contribution.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Circuit depth (number of alternations); Problem size; Feasible-set cardinality.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The separation relies on the assumption that the one-step transfer kernel factorizes across coordinates, and specific phase alignment conditions must be met for advantage.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates a layered process: Constraints define the space $ightarrow$ Mixer generates path mass (Geometry) $ightarrow$ Diagonal phases determine constructive contribution (Coherence) $ightarrow$ Measurement yields classical results.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds by first defining the constrained product space, then developing a framework to decompose the dynamics into geometric transport and coherent interference components, culminating in deriving conditions for achieving certified success probabilities.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We study the separation of geometric effects from quantum interference in quantum optimization algorithms. Constrained optimization problems such as routing, assignment, and scheduling are often encoded as product spaces of local variables, together with global feasibility penalties. The central algorithmic question we address is how a constraint-preserving mixing operator transports quantum amplitude across an exponential search space in the presence of local and global constraints. We develop a framework that separates three effects that are usually intermixed: amplitude transport, coherent interference among transported amplitudes, and problem-dependent classical postprocessing. We show that the mixing operator alone does not have a target-seeking ability. Concretely, the normalized distribution induced by its amplitude transport moves toward the distance profile of a uniformly random configuration. Thus, quantum sampling advantage may only arise when the phases of the many computational paths reaching a target configuration are sufficiently aligned for their amplitudes to reinforce. We show that, when the cost phases are engineered so that these paths add coherently, a number of circuit alternations growing only logarithmically with problem size suffices to convert the sum of their absolute contributions into a lower bound on the target amplitude, yielding a certified success probability independent of the ambient Hilbert-space dimension, the search-space size, or the feasible-set cardinality. We develop applications to problem-specific transpilation diagnostics, scalable hardware probes, constraint-induced classical maps of quantum-generated samples, the attribution of solution quality between the quantum distribution and classical post-processing in hybrid quantum-classical workflows and connections to distance-partitioned product spaces from classical coding theory.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper tackles optimizing irrigation scheduling in water-scarce regions by formulating it as a QUBO problem. The authors successfully map the complex hydrological dynamics and operational constraints onto a 2-local Ising Hamiltonian suitable for QAOA. They emphasize that their primary contribution is providing a physically grounded, data-complete encoding rather than claiming quantum computational superiority.

**Why it may be interesting.** While not directly about AMO physics, the rigorous process of translating complex physical constraints (like hydrology) into a mathematically structured Hamiltonian is highly relevant for developing quantum algorithms for real-world scientific modeling beyond simple toy models.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper formulates rotational irrigation scheduling, a complex combinatorial optimization problem, into a QUBO suitable for quantum computation.

**Main result.** They successfully map the physical constraints onto a 2-local Ising Hamiltonian, demonstrating that classical solvers can solve instances up to 150 variables quickly, and they claim no quantum advantage is demonstrated.

**Method.** The core method involves linearizing soil-water balance dynamics and constructing a cost function composed of stress, budget, spatial, and timing penalties, which are then mapped to an Ising Hamiltonian for QAOA execution.

**Model / system.** The system models water delivery in agricultural zones over time, governed by soil moisture memory. The optimization is framed as minimizing a QUBO/Ising Hamiltonian derived from hydrological equations using observed field data from Uzbekistan.

**Key observables.** Optimal irrigation schedule (binary decision vector $x_{z,d}$), total cost function value, and performance metrics comparing exact solvers vs. QAOA outputs.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Problem size scales up to 584 variables; the optimization is constrained by a finite water budget ($K$); quantum hardware tested includes the ibm_fez backend.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Key assumptions include linearizing the soil-water balance (Assumption 1) and using symmetric quadratic penalties for stress, which are necessary to achieve a 2-local Hamiltonian structure.

**Figures summary.** The notes do not detail figure contents, but mention benchmarking tables (Table II, Table III) detailing parameters for small, medium, and large instances tested across various solvers.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the physical problem, derives the QUBO formulation by linearizing dynamics, details the mapping to a 2-local Ising Hamiltonian, benchmarks performance against classical methods (MIQP), and finally tests execution on quantum hardware using both standard QAOA and an XY-mixer variant.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Rotational irrigation scheduling in water-scarce Central Asia is a densely coupled combinatorial problem: soil-moisture memory links each irrigation decision to all later days within a zone, field adjacency couples zones on overlapping window days, and rigid canal rotations quantize water delivery in time. We formulate it as a Quadratic Unconstrained Binary Optimization (QUBO) by linearizing the root-zone water balance, so the quadratic crop-stress objective generates the physical couplings as 2-local Ising interactions with no higher-order terms; only the water-budget constraint requires an artificial all-to-all penalty, which we certify with an instance-adaptive weight bound an order of magnitude tighter than generic prescriptions. Every instance is built from observed data for a cotton district in Khorezm, Uzbekistan: NASA POWER meteorology, FAO-56 Penman--Monteith evapotranspiration, SoilGrids~2.0 hydraulics, measured capillary fluxes, and documented canal-rotation windows that enter as qubit-count reductions. We benchmark four tiers -- exact solvers, matched-budget heuristics, ideal-statevector quantum approximate optimization algorithm (QAOA), and noise-model plus IBM Heron execution -- and add a scaling study on soil instances up to 584 variables. Exact branch-and-bound proves optimality in seconds through 150 variables, and heuristic degradation at fixed evaluation budget is repaired by scaling the budget, so no classical scalability wall appears at deployment-relevant sizes, and none is claimed. On hardware, the informative signal is optimum-sampling enrichment over uniform sampling. We claim no quantum advantage; we deliver a physically grounded, data-complete, reproducible encoding of a societally critical scheduling problem for the quantum-utility era.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper details the formal construction of Shor's algorithm to attack RSA-2048 and P-256 within the Lean proof assistant. Using an agentic workflow, the authors derive quantitative resource estimates (qubits, gates) for these attacks by building complex reversible quantum circuits. It represents a major step toward machine-checked verification of quantum cryptanalysis.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on cryptography, the rigorous formalization process—using AI agents to build complex quantum circuits in a proof assistant—represents a significant methodological advance for verifying any large-scale quantum algorithm.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper formalizes quantum attacks, specifically Shor's algorithm, against established cryptographic systems like RSA-2048 (integer factorization) and P-256 (discrete logarithms). It aims to provide machine-checked proofs of the required resources for these attacks.

**Main result.** The authors derived specific resource estimates—including logical qubits, Toffoli gates, and circuit depth—for breaking both RSA-2048 and P-256, presenting quantitative results in a formal proof assistant (Lean).

**Method.** They employ agentic formalization within the Lean proof assistant to build complex quantum circuits for modular arithmetic and point addition. This involves combining theoretical cryptographic reductions with rigorous, machine-checkable circuit construction.

**Model / system.** The model is abstract quantum computation, utilizing reversible quantum circuits (e.g., controlled operations) implemented in a formal framework. The targets are the mathematical structures underpinning RSA and elliptic curve cryptography.

**Key observables.** Logical qubits, Toffoli gates, maximal circuit depth, success probability ($\ge 2/3$), and classical arithmetic operation counts.

**Important parameters / regimes.** RSA-2048 modulus size (2048 bits) and P-256 curve parameters (NIST standard).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The reported resources are for logical qubits and Toffoli gates, neglecting physical overheads like noise or error correction. The process assumes a clear separation between formal checking guarantees and scientific judgment.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate dependency maps showing how the final theorems depend on foundational components like reversible modular addition circuits and resource accounting steps.

**Paper structure.** The work is structured around developing terminal propositions in Lean for both RSA-2048 (factor recovery) and P-256 (scalar recovery). This involves building necessary arithmetic primitives first, then assembling them into the final attack algorithms while meticulously tracking all required resources.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Large language models are increasingly assisting with demanding formal theorem-proving tasks, particularly when grounded in machine-checked libraries such as Lean. Agentic systems further amplify this process by searching, reusing, and extending existing formal developments to uncover new discoveries. In quantum computing, Shor's algorithm and its variants present such a demanding case for Lean formalization. In this work, we formalize this algorithm family in Lean through agentic formalization: software agents analyze sources, write Lean code and repair proofs, with human review of the scientific claims and machine checking of the resulting formal proofs. Our formalization develops the mathematical foundations for analyzing quantum attacks in two cryptographic settings: a 2048-bit modulus in the RSA-2048 and the standardized elliptic curve over a 256-bit prime field (P-256). To support these analyses, the formalization ranges from quantum algorithms for order finding to reversible quantum circuits for modular and elliptic-curve arithmetic. Based on [Quantum 5, 433] and [ASIACRYPT 2017, 241--270], we formalize the logical resource estimates for RSA-2048 and P-256, respectively, and provide additional estimates of classical operations. We expect the results pave the way for broader machine-checked quantum cryptanalysis and represent a step toward AI-assisted design and verification of quantum algorithms.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper rigorously proves that the free-particle solution can be derived from the bound state solutions of the finite square well by taking the width to infinity. It uses Fourier transforms to map the discrete energy spectrum onto a continuous one, showing the mathematical consistency between confined and unconfined quantum systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a fundamental, rigorous demonstration of how continuum limits are handled in quantum mechanics, which is crucial for understanding effective Hamiltonians or open system approximations where boundaries become negligible.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The work systematically demonstrates the mathematical transition from the discrete, quantized energy spectrum of a finite square well to the continuous spectrum characteristic of a free particle.

**Main result.** By applying Fourier analysis and taking the limit where the confining width approaches infinity, the bound state solutions correctly recover the plane-wave form of the free particle wave function.

**Method.** The core method involves solving the time-independent Schrödinger equation for both finite and infinite wells, followed by mapping the resulting discrete summation structure onto a continuous integral representation using Fourier transform techniques.

**Model / system.** The model is a quantum particle confined by a potential $U(x)$, comparing the free particle case to the finite and infinite square well potentials. The governing equation is the time-independent Schrödinger equation.

**Key observables.** Energy spectrum ($E$): transitioning from discrete eigenvalues ($E_n$) to a continuous band; wave functions ($\psi$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Well width ($L$), potential height ($U_0$), and the limit $L 	o \infty$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies on treating the transition from a discrete sum (Fourier Series) to a continuous integral (Fourier Transform), which requires careful handling of limits.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 schematically shows the potential profiles for finite and infinite wells. Figure 2 illustrates how the energy level spacing ($\Delta E$) decreases as $L$ increases, leading toward the continuum limit.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses by first establishing solutions for the free particle (continuous spectrum), then solving the finite square well problem (discrete spectrum), and finally executing the mathematical procedure to show that the continuous limit of the discrete solutions yields the free-particle plane wave.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The free-particle and square-well potentials are two of the most emblematic problems in quantum mechanics, illustrating essential concepts such as matter waves, energy quantization, and bound states. It is therefore natural to consider how the free-particle solutions emerge from the square well as the width approaches infinity. In this work, we present a systematic procedure to demonstrate this transition by applying a Fourier transform to the wave equation.

</details>

### [Inherent interpretability provides inherent value in quantum machine learning](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13827v1)

**Authors:** Kaitlin Gili, Zachary P. Bradshaw  
**Type:** theory · **Category:** other · **PDF:** <https://arxiv.org/pdf/2607.13827v1>  
**Analysis basis:** full PDF text, analyzed in chunks

Hamiltonian choices offer a bottom-up route to the kernel function design of GPs – in contrast to the top-down design of an explicit spectral distribution in RFF.</figcaption>
</figure>

**Summary.** This paper argues that the true value of Quantum Machine Learning lies in its inherent mathematical structure—its interpretability—rather than just its performance relative to classical methods. It demonstrates this by comparing how quantum Fourier models and Random Fourier Features can both approximate GP kernels, showing that quantum tools offer unique mechanisms for principled model design. This reframes QML research to value the underlying physical or mathematical components of quantum circuits.

**Why it may be interesting.** It bridges advanced topics like algebraic topology and group theory (used to constrain models) directly into the practical methodology of machine learning, offering theoretical frameworks for building physically motivated quantum algorithms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The field of Quantum Machine Learning (QML) needs a value proposition beyond merely matching classical ML performance, especially given that model interpretability is crucial for real-world deployment.

**Main result.** The value of QML models should be characterized by their inherent mathematical structure and interpretability offerings, rather than solely by task performance metrics.

**Method.** The authors compare quantum Fourier models against Random Fourier Features (RFF) in the context of designing interpretable Gaussian Process (GP) kernels for uncertainty quantification.

**Model / system.** The work analyzes parameterized quantum circuits (QNNs) and uses Bayesian linear regression within a GP framework. It compares feature mapping techniques derived from classical spectral distributions (RFF) versus those arising naturally from quantum information tools (Quantum Fourier Models).

**Key observables.** Model interpretability, uncertainty quantification ($\sigma^2_{	ext{pred}}$), and the ability to encode domain-specific inductive biases.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The spectral distribution $p(\omega_z)$ controlling RFFs; the Hamiltonian families $\{H^{(l)}_j\}$ used in quantum feature mapping.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The argument assumes that demonstrating inherent interpretability benefits justifies the use of potentially resource-intensive quantum computation for ML tasks.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 visually contrasts the bottom-up kernel design approach using Hamiltonian families (Quantum Fourier Models) against the top-down spectral distribution choice in RFFs.

**Paper structure.** The paper establishes the need for interpretability in QML, provides a detailed mathematical comparison between Quantum Fourier Models and RFFs via GP kernels, and concludes with a review of various quantum information tools (symmetry, topology) that enable inherently interpretable model design.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The field of quantum machine learning (QML) evolved to value models believed to most directly rival those providing utility in classical ML, namely large-scale neural networks. Although more recently, classical ML has been learning a hard lesson with respect to deploying un-interpretable neural networks in the wild: model interpretability matters for domain-adapted co-design and human adoption. We adopt this larger ML perspective to argue that quantum ML model value can be found through the characterization of its inherent interpretability offerings -- i.e. its mathematical structure that contributes meaningfully to desired model behavior for the specific ML task. To support our perspective, we provide a motivating example of a characterization with quantum Fourier models and random Fourier features (RFF) as approaches to approximate Gaussian process (GP) kernels for uncertainty quantification tasks in ML. The top-down and bottom-up complementarity of the two mathematical constructions reveals that quantum Fourier models offer different tools than RFFs for principled GP kernel design and interpretable discovery for uncertainty quantification with real-world data. To showcase the rich variety of inductive biases enabled by quantum information tools, we review examples from the QML literature -- including symmetry, metric geometry, and topology -- that can be used to design inherently interpretable ML models for specific tasks. We hope this framing encourages the QML community to value the inherent components and mechanisms of quantum models separately from task performance, as inherent interpretability might be the reason that a quantum model, and potentially a quantum computer, gets used in practice for ML.

</details>
