**Summary.** This paper presents the Jacobi Qudit Algorithm (JQA) for finding the eigenvalues and eigenvectors of unknown unitary operators acting on qudits. It achieves this by mapping the iterative Givens rotation steps into sequential, experimentally accessible single-parameter variational optimizations. The work is significant because it establishes a structured quantum method that mirrors classical numerical efficiency while being naturally suited for qudit architectures.

**Why it may be interesting.** It provides a concrete blueprint for implementing complex numerical linear algebra routines directly on hardware (qudits), offering a structured path from classical theory to quantum circuit realization.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is developing a quantum algorithm for spectral decomposition of unknown unitary operators, specifically realizing the Jacobi diagonalization procedure on qudits.

**Main result.** The proposed structured quantum algorithm preserves the convergence behavior of classical Jacobi methods and achieves expected quadratic scaling ($O(d^2)$) in elementary operations with dimension $d$.

**Method.** It implements Givens rotations using two sequential, experimentally accessible single-parameter quantum variational optimizations rather than explicit operator reconstruction or controlled unitaries.

**Model / system.** The system is a qudit (dimension $d$) undergoing unitary evolution governed by an unknown unitary operator. The method mimics the classical Jacobi algorithm for normal matrices.

**Key observables.** Convergence of the off-diagonal norm ($	au(M)$), eigenvalues extracted via interferometry, and the number of required Jacobi sweeps.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The dimension $d$ of the qudit space; the target accuracy $\epsilon$; and the structure of the unitary operator $M$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumption is that elementary two-parameter Givens rotations can be decomposed into two sequential single-parameter variational optimizations, which are experimentally feasible.

**Figures summary.** Figure 3 compares the average number of Jacobi sweeps required for different accuracies ($\epsilon$) across dimensions $d$, showing the scaling behavior of the JQA compared to generic methods.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem by relating it to classical Jacobi diagonalization, details the qudit-native quantum realization using sequential variational optimizations for Givens rotations, discusses eigenvalue extraction via interferometry, and concludes with complexity analysis and future directions like parallelization.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Jacobi diagonalization is a long-established numerical algorithm for the spectral decomposition of Hermitian and, more generally, normal matrices. In this work, we develop a qudit-native quantum realization of the Jacobi diagonalization algorithm for unknown unitary operators. The proposed framework avoids explicit reconstruction of the operator and controlled-unitary operations. Each elementary two-parameter Givens rotation is implemented through two sequential single-parameter quantum variational optimizations that are directly accessible experimentally. An interferometric protocol is further introduced for extracting the eigenvalues of the diagonalized unitary, up to an overall global phase. Numerical simulations on ensembles of Haar-random unitary matrices demonstrate that the proposed algorithm preserves the characteristic convergence behavior of the classical Jacobi method and exhibits the expected quadratic scaling in the number of elementary operations with the dimension $d$, comparable to its classical counterpart. The results establish the proposed algorithm as a structured quantum numerical linear algebra algorithm naturally suited to qudit architectures and provide a bridge between classical iterative matrix algorithms and their quantum realizations.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper details a method to construct robust binary CSS quantum LDPC codes by utilizing pair-partition constructions based on circulant permutation matrices. The authors derive necessary algebraic conditions that guarantee structural integrity, leading to the explicit reporting of several high-performance code examples with rigorously verified distances.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides advanced theoretical tools for constructing quantum error-correcting codes with desirable properties (high rate, high girth), which is fundamental knowledge for building fault-tolerant quantum computation architectures.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is constructing binary Calderbank–Shor–Steane (CSS) quantum low-density parity-check (LDPC) codes using circulant permutation matrices (CPMs).

**Main result.** The authors report specific, high-rate examples of such codes, including the (J,L)=(4,12)-regular girth-six code [[372,130,16]] with rate 0.349.

**Method.** Construction relies on imposing linear paired-difference equations derived from a pair partition array onto CPM exponents to guarantee CSS orthogonality and structural properties like high Tanner girth.

**Model / system.** The model is the quantum error correcting code itself, defined by two parity check matrices, $H_X$ and $H_Z$, which are constructed using CPMs parameterized by column weight J, row weight L, and prime lift size P.

**Key observables.** Code parameters $[[n, k, d]]$, rate ($1 - 2J/L$), Tanner girth (at least six), and minimum distance $d$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Column weight J, row weight L, prime lift size P.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The final distance claims require independent verification via exhaustive low-weight exclusion and explicit non-stabilizer witnesses, separate from the algebraic construction guarantees.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the pair-partition array $(M_{ij})$, the mixed-distinctness condition using sample values in $\mathbb{F}_{53}$, and details on variables/constraints for specific parameter choices.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops by first establishing necessary algebraic conditions (Theorem 1) guaranteeing structural properties like orthogonality and girth. These conditions are then implemented in a systematic search algorithm to find concrete examples, culminating in rigorous distance verification procedures.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We construct binary Calderbank--Shor--Steane (CSS) quantum low-density parity-check (LDPC) codes from circulant permutation matrices (CPMs). The construction is parameterized by column weight J, row weight L, and prime lift size P. A J x J array of pair partitions imposes linear paired-difference equations on the CPM exponents. These equations give CSS orthogonality. The main finite examples reported here are the (J,L)=(4,12)-regular girth-six code [[372,130,16]] with rate 0.349, and the (J,L)=(4,14)-regular girth-six code [[518,228,16]] with rate 0.440. We also record (J,L)=(3,8)-regular girth-six instances [[472,122,14]] and [[488,126,14]], with lift sizes P=59 and P=61, respectively. The stated distances are established for the fixed matrices by exhaustive low-weight exclusion together with explicit non-stabilizer witnesses.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces Parallel Quantum Feature Augmentation (PQFA), a novel hybrid framework that enhances multimodal feature representations by passing the fused classical output through parallel shallow quantum circuits. By demonstrating superior performance and parameter efficiency compared to purely classical augmentation techniques, PQFA establishes quantum readout as an effective tool for post-fusion learning in complex AI tasks.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work directly applies variational quantum algorithms (VQAs) in a hybrid setting for complex data classification, offering a structured way to test quantum advantage in machine learning tasks beyond simple kernel methods or state preparation.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge addressed is enhancing multimodal classification by improving representations *after* they have been fused, rather than redesigning the entire fusion backbone.

**Main result.** PQFA consistently outperforms both unaugmented baselines and MLP augmentation methods on benchmark datasets while using significantly fewer parameters, demonstrating effective post-fusion quantum feature augmentation.

**Method.** The method involves amplitude-encoding the classical fused feature into multiple parallel shallow variational quantum circuits (PQCs) whose measurement readouts are concatenated with the original classical representation for final prediction.

**Model / system.** It employs a hybrid quantum-classical framework. Classical components use frozen RoBERTa and ViT encoders followed by cross-attention, pooling, and adaptive gated fusion to yield $\mathbf{z}_C$. This is then augmented by parallel PQCs acting on the fused feature.

**Key observables.** Classification accuracy improvement over baselines; stable predictive performance across simulated noise levels; parameter efficiency compared to classical alternatives.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The number of augmentation parameters (PQFA uses $\approx 2.2\text{K}$ vs. $24.0\text{K}$ for MLP-Aug); the depth and width of the variational quantum circuits.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The performance gains are attributed to the learned nonlinear transformation provided by the quantum readouts, not merely increased feature dimensionality or trainable parameters.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the overall architecture: Text/Image $\rightarrow$ Encoders $\rightarrow$ Cross-Attention $\rightarrow$ Pooling $\rightarrow$ Adaptive Gating ($\mathbf{z}_C$) $\rightarrow$ Parallel Quantum Augmentation (PQCs) $\rightarrow$ Concatenation $\rightarrow$ Classifier.

**Paper structure.** The paper first establishes the need for post-fusion enhancement. It details the PQFA architecture, which processes fused features through parallel quantum circuits. Evaluation involves controlled comparisons against unaugmented and MLP baselines, followed by ablation studies (missing modalities, feature diagnostics) to prove the necessity of the quantum component.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Most multimodal learning methods improve how heterogeneous representations are aligned and fused, while post-fusion enhancement remains less explored. We propose Parallel Quantum Feature Augmentation (PQFA), a hybrid quantum-classical framework that applies multiple shallow variational quantum circuits to fused multimodal features. Text and image representations extracted by frozen RoBERTa and ViT encoders are processed through bidirectional cross-attention, attentive pooling, and adaptive gated fusion. The fused feature is then amplitude-encoded into parallel quantum circuits, whose measurement readouts are concatenated with the classical representation for prediction. We evaluate PQFA on MM-IMDb and N24News through controlled comparisons using the same encoders, fusion backbone, data splits, projection dimension, and augmentation output width. PQFA consistently outperforms both the fusion backbone without quantum augmentation and a width-matched MLP augmentation baseline, while using approximately 2.2K augmentation parameters compared with 24.0K for the MLP branch. Missing-modality experiments further show improved robustness when textual or visual inputs are incomplete, with particularly clear gains when the more informative textual modality is severely degraded. Controlled ablations and feature-space analyses indicate that the improvement cannot be reproduced by random feature mappings, increased classical width, or untrained quantum transformations. Quantum-state diagnostics additionally show stable predictive performance across the tested simulated noise levels and distinct branch-specific transformations of the encoded states. These results establish PQFA as an effective and parameter-efficient strategy for post-fusion augmentation in hybrid quantum-classical multimodal learning.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper details a highly resource-optimized quantum algorithm for solving the Elliptic Curve Discrete Logarithm Problem (ECDLP). The key innovation is a novel, space-efficient reversible modular inversion circuit that drastically reduces qubit requirements compared to prior methods. This advancement provides a concrete, improved blueprint for implementing Shor's algorithm in practice.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on cryptography, the detailed quantum arithmetic circuits developed—especially the modular inversion and register-sharing techniques—represent advanced theoretical tools applicable to any quantum computation requiring efficient large-number arithmetic in condensed matter or AMO simulations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to develop a quantum algorithm for solving the Elliptic Curve Discrete Logarithm Problem (ECDLP) while significantly reducing the required quantum resources, particularly logical qubits.

**Main result.** The authors present a space-efficient construction achieving resource requirements of $3n+6\lfloor \log_2 nfloor+ O(1)$ logical qubits for an $n$-bit prime field curve, representing substantial improvements over previous state-of-the-art estimates.

**Method.** The improvement hinges on developing a new space-efficient reversible modular inversion circuit derived from the Extended Euclidean Algorithm (EEA), which addresses the dominant space bottleneck in point addition circuits.

**Model / system.** The work operates within the standard quantum circuit model, applying Shor's algorithm structure to ECC over prime fields. The core components involve implementing controlled affine point addition and modular inversion using techniques like register-sharing and location-controlled arithmetic.

**Key observables.** Logical qubits (space complexity), Toffoli gate counts, and CNOT gate counts are the primary resources quantified for the algorithm's implementation.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The resource costs scale with $n$, the bit-length of the prime field ($n=256$ bits is used as a key example).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes the feasibility of implementing complex reversible modular arithmetic circuits, particularly refining EEA state representation using length registers and location-controlled arithmetic.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the scaling of Toffoli and CNOT gate counts for modular inversion and point addition as a function of bit size $n$, alongside comparative tables showing qubit savings over prior works.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds by first identifying ECDLP as the target problem, then focusing on optimizing the bottleneck subroutine (modular inversion via EEA refinement), followed by constructing the full space-efficient controlled point addition circuit necessary for Shor's algorithm implementation.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The Elliptic Curve Discrete Logarithm Problem (ECDLP) is a fundamental problem in cryptography, and reducing the resource requirements of quantum algorithms for solving ECDLP is an important goal. In this work, we present a space-efficient quantum algorithm for solving the ECDLP over prime fields, achieving an implementation with only $3n+6\lfloor \log_2 n \rfloor+O(1)$ logical qubits and $919n^3/\log_2 n+O(n^2)$ Toffoli gates, where $n$ is the bit-length of the prime. For a 256-bit prime-field curve, our construction requires only 835 logical qubits, reducing the previous best estimates of 1098 and 1175 logical qubits by Chevignard et al. [EUROCRYPT 2026] and Babbush et al. [ArXiv Preprint 2026], respectively.   The key to our improvement is a new space-efficient reversible modular inversion circuit, which addresses the dominant space bottleneck in affine-coordinate point addition. Starting from the extended Euclidean algorithm (EEA), we refine the register-sharing technique of Proos and Zalka by introducing length registers and location-controlled arithmetic to compactly store and update intermediate variables. We further optimize the reversible update procedures and construct the corresponding controlled arithmetic circuits, resulting in a modular inversion circuit implemented by only $2n+6\lfloor \log_2 n \rfloor+O(1)$ logical qubits and $195n^2+O(n\log_2 n)$ Toffoli gates. This modular inversion circuit together with mid-circuit measurements and classical feed-forward operations provides a space-efficient controlled affine point-addition circuit and a complete implementation of Shor's algorithm for ECDLP.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper presents 'Two-Tower Matrix Multiplication,' a novel quantum subroutine to compute the product of many matrices ($W = M_0 \cdots M_{K-1}$). It achieves an unprecedented circuit depth that is independent of the number of matrices $K$, offering a major algorithmic speedup over classical and prior quantum methods. This breakthrough makes complex linear algebra operations more feasible for near-term quantum hardware.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a significant algorithmic improvement for fundamental linear algebra tasks in quantum computing, directly impacting the feasibility of complex quantum machine learning kernels and simulations that rely on matrix products.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The paper addresses the computational challenge of matrix chain multiplication, $W = M^{(0)}\cdots M^{(K-1)}$, which is crucial in scientific computing.

**Main result.** They introduce a quantum subroutine called Two-Tower Matrix Multiplication that encodes the product state with circuit depth independent of the chain length $K$.

**Method.** The method utilizes a two-layer, parallel structure involving specialized quantum state preparation operators ($	ext{SP}(M)$ and $	ext{SP}(M)^\dagger$) to compute the matrix product amplitude.

**Model / system.** The work is framed within the QRAM-based model for quantum linear algebra subroutines. It operates on encoding matrices into quantum states, allowing computation of the full product statevector.

**Key observables.** The signal weight $\Omega_W = rac{\|W\|^2_F}{\prod_{k=0}^{K-1} \|M(k)\|^2_F}$ is a key diagnostic used to track the useful amplitude carried by the final quantum state.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Circuit depth achieves $O(\max_k 	ext{polylog}(P_k P_{k+1}))$, which is independent of $K$. The qubit count scales as $\Theta\left(\sum_{k} \log P_kight)$.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The polylogarithmic depth relies on the assumption that each matrix $M(k)$ can be loaded into a quantum state in polylogarithmic time (QRAM model).

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the core components: $	ext{SP}(M)$, $	ext{SP}(M)^\dagger$, and $	ext{MSP}(M)$ for small matrices, and show the general Two-Tower circuit structure for $K$ matrices.

**Paper structure.** The paper introduces the problem, details the quantum state preparation operators, derives the efficient two-tower circuit structure by showing its equivalence to sequential methods, analyzes the complexity (depth vs. qubits), and discusses applications like chain-vector multiplication and signal weight decay.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Matrix chain multiplication -- computing $\mathcal{W} = M^{(0)}\cdots M^{(K-1)}$ where $M^{(k)} \in \mathbb{R}^{P_k \times P_{k+1}}$ -- arises in scientific computing, machine learning, and graph analysis. Despite the importance of this problem, for chains of distinct matrices, the classical number of operations grows linearly with the chain length $K$ and polynomially in the matrix dimensions. We present \emph{Two-Tower Matrix Multiplication}, a quantum subroutine that encodes the product $\mathcal{W}$ of the $K$ matrices into a quantum state in circuit depth $\mathcal{O}(\max_{k} \mathrm{polylog} (P_k P_{k+1}))$, which is independent of~$K$ within the QRAM-based state-preparation model, whereas the qubit count is $\mathcal{O}\bigl(\sum_{k} \log P_k \bigr)$; the total gate count remains linear in $K$, so the gain is in the circuit depth. The construction interleaves state-preparation operators across two layers; within each layer, all operators act on disjoint registers and execute in parallel. This subroutine can be specialized for the chain-vector case, which computes the product of $K-1$ matrices applied to a vector. We prove the correctness of the subroutine for all $K$ and provide two implementations using the Qiskit and QCLAB frameworks. The subroutine is applicable to any downstream quantum algorithm that operates on a matrix encoded in the statevector, including norm estimation, graph-matrix powers, linear system solving, and quantum machine learning kernels.

</details>

</details>

### [Realization and manipulation of spiral charge density waves in a two-dimensional metal](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13878v1)

**Authors:** Lili Zhou, Ruizi Zhang, Chen Si, Zhaoteng Dong, Mengya Ren, Keru Guo, Can Zhang, Jizheng Wu, Fudi Zhou, Huixia Yang, Yaxin Zhao, Guoyuan Yang, Xiaolong Xu, Yuanxiao Ma, Xiao Kong, Yu Zhang, Yeliang Wang

**Summary.** The research uses strain engineering on NbSe2 to overcome the challenge of competing, disordered Charge Density Wave (CDW) orders. By creating controlled strain environments within material wrinkles, the authors successfully stabilize and manipulate distinct CDW phases, such as transforming a 3x3 order into a highly stable 4x1 stripe. This establishes strain as a powerful tool for controlling correlated electronic states in 2D materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work demonstrates how external mechanical tuning (strain) can act as a powerful knob to selectively stabilize specific correlated electronic phases (CDWs), offering an analogy to controlling quantum states using external fields in AMO physics or superconducting circuits.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The central challenge is disentangling and controlling competing Charge Density Wave (CDW) orders, which are often nearly degenerate and intertwined by disorder in real materials.

**Main result.** Strain engineering can lift the near-degeneracy of CDW states in NbSe2, transforming an intrinsic 3x3 order into stable, controllable unidirectional 4x1 or 2x2 phases. Furthermore, these strain networks allow for the creation and manipulation of chiral spiral textures.

**Method.** The study combines experimental measurements using Scanning Tunneling Microscopy (STM) with theoretical calculations like Density Functional Theory (DFT) to map out phase stability under controlled strain fields.

**Model / system.** The physical system is NbSe2, a layered Transition Metal Dichalcogenide (TMD). The research focuses on the interplay between lattice strain (induced via van der Waals interactions in wrinkle networks) and the electronic ground state characterized by competing CDW superlattices (3x3, 4x1, 2x2).

**Key observables.** CDW superlattice order ($3 	imes 3$, $4 	imes 1$, $2 	imes 2$), local strain fields (compressive/tensile), CDW transition temperatures, and the development of chiral spiral textures.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Strain magnitude (uniaxial compression vs. biaxial tension $> 2.5\%$) and temperature (stability up to 70 K).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The ability to stabilize strain networks using vdW interactions, and that the observed CDW transitions are primarily governed by lattice strain lifting electronic degeneracy.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate STM images showing distinct CDW superstructures in different wrinkle regions; schematics detail how compressive/tensile strain drives the transition from 3x3 to 4x1 or 2x2 orders, and show evidence of chiral textures and voltage-induced melting.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically maps out the phase diagram by correlating local strain fields (measured via STM topography) with the resulting stable CDW order, providing both experimental visualization and DFT confirmation for each transition pathway.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Nearly degenerate charge-density-wave (CDW) states play a central role in the competition among collective phenomena. In real materials, however, these states are often intertwined by disorder, hindering their disentanglement and control. Here we show that strain can lift this near-degeneracy and spatially separate distinct CDW states in NbSe2. Using van der Waals (vdW) interactions, we stabilize a micron-scale strain network that produces spatially inhomogeneous strain fields. Within this landscape, the intrinsic 3 * 3 CDW superlattice of pristine NbSe2 transforms into an isolated unidirectional 4 * 1 order under 1D-confined compression, and into a 2 * 2 order under biaxial tension. The 4 * 1 CDW has a multiband origin and exhibits markedly enhanced thermal stability, persisting up to 70 K. At strain-network nodes, it further develops into chiral spiral textures, which can be melted by voltage pulses. These results establish strain as a powerful approach to disentangle, stabilize and manipulate competing electronic orders.

</details>

### [Fundamental Relation between Conductance of Biomolecules and the Fukui Function](http://arxiv.org/abs/2607.13309v1)

**Authors:** Gabor Vattay

(b) In single- probe setups, multi-point coupling to the substrate or solution leads to a short-circuit limit (Gsub →∞), leaving the conduc- tance bottlenecked solely by the tip: GT ≈Gtip = 2e2</figcaption>
</figure>
</div>

**Summary.** This paper develops a rigorous DFT-based theory showing that the electrical conductance of biomolecules is fundamentally determined by their local chemical reactivity, quantified by the Fukui function. By analyzing open quantum system dynamics under specific approximations, the authors establish an equivalence between electronic transport pathways and chemically active sites. This has major implications for designing bioelectronic sensors.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work bridges quantum chemistry (reactivity descriptors like Fukui functions) with condensed matter transport physics (conductance), providing a powerful theoretical tool for bioelectronics.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To establish a fundamental, quantitative link between the finite-temperature electronic conductance of biomolecules and their local chemical reactivity descriptor (the Fukui function).

**Main result.** The thermal conductance ($G_T$) is shown to be directly proportional to the local chemical reactivity quantified by the finite-temperature Fukui function ($f_T(r)$), establishing a link between electronic transport and chemistry.

**Method.** The derivation uses Density Functional Theory (DFT) within an open quantum system framework, employing a double perturbation expansion under the fast-thermalization condition to simplify complex many-body dynamics.

**Model / system.** The study models biomolecules, such as proteins, coupled to metallic leads. The theoretical machinery involves deriving the master equation for the single-particle density matrix from the Mermin grand potential.

**Key observables.** Conductance ($G_T$), finite-temperature Fukui function ($f_T(r)$), and local electronic structure profiles $Z(r)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Fast-thermalization condition ($	au_{rlx} \ll 	au_{esc}$), temperature ($k_B T$), applied voltage, and coupling strengths ($\Gamma$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation critically relies on the fast-thermalization condition; it also assumes linear response approximations around equilibrium.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 provides empirical validation in PTP1B, showing how ligand binding suppresses the spatial profile of $f_T(r)$ along the WPD loop, correlating with pathway disruption. Figure 2 schematically illustrates two-terminal vs. single-probe transport models.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically derives the conductance from first principles (Mermin potential $ightarrow$ Master Equation) and then applies approximations (double perturbation expansion under fast thermalization) to show that the complex electronic response projects out, leaving a result governed solely by $f_T(r)$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The finite-temperature conductance of a molecule coupled to metallic leads is derived entirely within the framework of density functional theory (DFT) and its time-dependent extension for open quantum systems. Starting from the Mermin grand potential, the foundational Kohn-Sham equations, the Fukui function, and the open-system master equation for the single-particle density matrix are systematically formulated. The non-equilibrium electron-phonon dissipator is obtained from the partial trace over the phonon bath. By applying Wick's theorem for non-interacting fermions, a fully exchange-symmetric collision integral is obtained that strictly preserves Pauli exclusion at the operator level. Performing a double perturbation expansion, initially in the applied voltage (linear response), and subsequently in the molecule-lead coupling (weak coupling), it is demonstrated that under the fast-thermalization condition, the complex exchange-correlation self-consistent field response is analytically projected out by the diagonal structure of the slow Liouvillian mode. Consequently, the thermal conductance is governed by the finite-temperature Fukui function, the central reactivity descriptor of conceptual density functional theory. This condition is satisfied in proteins, whose wave functions are extended and multifractal due to quantum criticality at the Anderson metal-insulator transition. This derivation establishes a fundamental link between electronic transport and chemical reactivity, identifying conducting paths with reactive sites. It opens new technological avenues connecting drug design to conductance experiments and also provides a foundation for designing next-generation bioelectronic sensing and computing architectures.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical paper establishes a mechanism by which charge-neutral quasiparticles, such as magnons, can exhibit an electric quadrupole moment via the Aharonov-Casher effect. By treating this response semiclassically, the authors calculate the $	ext{AC-EQ}$ tensor and demonstrate its relevance in magnetic materials, showing it is comparable to nuclear moments.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work extends the concept of orbital magnetism from charged particles to neutral excitations like magnons, predicting measurable electric quadrupole responses that are sensitive to magnetic structure and spin texture.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary goal is to elucidate the physical manifestation of Orbital Angular Momentum (OAM) for charge-neutral particles in solids, as its magnetic signature is not straightforward.

**Main result.** The authors demonstrate that a charge-neutral particle with a magnetic moment exhibits an Aharonov-Casher induced electric quadrupole ($	ext{AC-EQ}$) when coupled to the electric field gradient in crystalline solids. These $	ext{AC-EQ}$ components can reach magnitudes comparable to typical nuclear moments.

**Method.** The theory employs a semi-classical framework, deriving the $	ext{AC-EQ}$ tensor as a linear response of thermodynamic potentials (like the grand potential) to spatial derivatives of the electric field.

**Model / system.** The analysis is developed for charge-neutral particles, using magnons in magnetic systems like ferromagnetic pyrochlore and helical langasite as concrete examples. The formalism involves effective electromagnetic potentials derived from the particle's magnetic moment coupling.

**Key observables.** AC-induced electric quadrupole ($	ext{AC-EQ}$), which decomposes into scalar, toroidal dipole, and reduced quadrupole components; Polarization ($	ext{P}_\alpha$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Magnetic moment ($\mu$), crystalline solid structure (pyrochlore, langasite), and the coupling strength derived from the Aharonov-Casher effect.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The derivation relies on a semi-classical approximation and assumes weak electric fields/slow variations in external potentials.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the intuitive picture of how angular motion generates specific components of the $	ext{AC-EQ}$ tensor, depending on the relative orientation of the magnetic moment ($\mu$) and the rotation vector ($oldsymbol{\omega}$).

**Paper structure.** The paper develops the theory by first establishing the effective potentials (Eqs. 11, 12) and then deriving the $	ext{AC-EQ}$ tensor ($Q_{\alphaeta}$) as a response function (Eq. 19). It decomposes this tensor into irreducible components (Rank-0, Rank-1, Rank-2), providing physical interpretations for each component.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

For charged particles, their orbital angular momentum (OAM) in solid have a direct magnetic manifestation as an orbital magnetization. For charge-neutral particles, however, the physical manifestation of the OAM in solid remains unclear. Here, we show that a charge-neutral particle carrying a magnetic moment couples to electric-field gradient through the Aharonov-Casher (AC) effect, thereby exhibiting the electric quadrupole in crystalline solids. This AC-induced electric quadrupole (AC-EQ) contains the scalar, toroidal dipole, and reduced quadrupole components, which are conjugate to the divergence, circulation, and shear of the electric field, respectively. As representative examples, we calculate the AC-EQ of magnons in two magnetic systems. In ferromagnetic pyrochlore, Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interaction (DMI) induces a sizable AC-EQ, whereas in helical Fe langasite the AC-EQ emerges from the helical spin configuration even in the absence of DMI. In both systems, the unit-cell-integrated AC-EQ components reach magnitudes comparable to typical nuclear electric quadrupole moments.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically analyzes how magnetic dynamics induce an 'emergent induction' in Weyl semimetals. It derives the magnetoelectric response by separating contributions into intraband (dissipative) and interband (topological) parts. The key finding is that these systems inherently behave as emergent inductors due to strong magnetoelectric coupling, particularly when the chemical potential lies within the topological energy gap.

**Why it may be interesting.** The concept of emergent induction mediated by topological band structures (Weyl nodes) provides a mechanism linking magnetism, topology, and macroscopic electromagnetic responses, which is relevant to advanced spintronic/topological materials research.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates emergent electromagnetic responses, specifically 'emergent induction,' arising in magnetic Weyl semimetals due to underlying magnetic dynamics.

**Main result.** The magnetoelectric response is shown to have distinct intraband and interband contributions; the system generally acts as an emergent inductor due to this coupling.

**Method.** Theoretical derivation using Boltzmann transport theory for intraband effects and the Kubo formula for interband contributions, applied to effective two-band models.

**Model / system.** The physical system is a magnetic Weyl semimetal, modeled using effective two-band Hamiltonians that incorporate time-dependent magnetization acting as an emergent vector potential. The analysis applies this formalism to polar Weyl ferromagnets.

**Key observables.** Magnetoelectric (ME) response ($\langle M	au angle$ and $\mathcal{C}_I$), inductance ($L$, $L_{	ext{long}}$, $L_{	ext{Hall}}$), and conductivity components ($\sigma_{DC,O}$ and $\sigma_{DC,Hall}$).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Relaxation time ($	au$), chemical potential ($\mu$), Weyl node separation/Fermi arc length, and the magnitude of magnetization dynamics.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis relies on separating the ME response into dissipative intraband and non-dissipative interband parts, and uses approximations regarding the relative magnitudes of different conductivity components in later sections.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the chemical potential dependence of both the Fermi surface term ($\mathcal{C}_{O}$) and the interband effect term ($\mathcal{C}_I$), as well as the $\mu$ dependence of conductivity and inductance for specific models.

**Paper structure.** The paper develops a general theory by deriving ME responses from transport equations, separates contributions into intraband/interband terms, applies this to polar Weyl ferromagnets to calculate inductance components ($L_{	ext{long}}, L_{	ext{Hall}}$), and concludes with generalized expressions for the inductive response.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We theoretically study emergent electromagnetic responses in Weyl semimetals. Focusing on magnetic Weyl semimetals, we develop a general theory of emergent induction driven by magnetic dynamics. We show that magnetoelectric (ME) responses in Weyl semimetals give rise to emergent induction mediated by magnetization dynamics. Using effective two-band models for magnetic Weyl semimetals, we derive a formula for the ME response that includes both intraband and interband contributions. The resulting formula shows that the intraband contribution is proportional to the relaxation time $τ$, whereas the interband contribution is associated with the separation of the Weyl nodes. Applying the general formula to a model of polar Weyl ferromagnets, we demonstrate that the dynamics of the toroidal moment is closely related to the emergent inductive response in polar Weyl ferromagnets, as recently discovered by Suzuki et al. [Y. Suzuki et al. arXiv:2607.12322]. The chemical-potential dependence of the inductance indicates that the emergent electromagnetic response is enhanced in the energy range of the Weyl dispersion, reflecting the topological nature of Weyl semimetals.

</details>

**Summary.** The paper introduces Tensor-Network Finite Elements, a novel framework that unifies the solution of diverse operator equations—from PDEs to delay equations. It achieves this by translating continuous differential operators into structured tensor forms and minimizing the resulting residual error within a constrained TN variational space. This offers a general, powerful computational tool for modeling complex physical systems.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a powerful computational bridge between established numerical analysis (FEM) and modern quantum many-body simulation techniques (TNs), offering a general toolset for solving complex physical models beyond standard quantum chemistry or condensed matter simulations.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The core problem is developing a unified framework to solve analytic Operator Equations (OEs), including PDEs, IDEs, and MDEs, by connecting Finite-Element (FE) discretization with Tensor Network (TN) variational representations.

**Main result.** The authors establish a direct connection between the FE numerical formalism and TN variational algorithms, allowing highly non-linear partial differential equations to be reformulated as weighted-residual optimization problems over TN degrees of freedom.

**Method.** The method involves discretizing OEs using FE methods, which generates multilinear interaction tensors. These are then approximated and optimized within a Tensor Network ansatz (like MPS or PEPS) by minimizing a weighted residual norm subject to the TN manifold constraint.

**Model / system.** The framework is general, applying to various OEs such as nonlinear diffusion equations, initial-value problems, integro-differential equations, and memory/delay equations. The underlying mathematical structure involves mapping continuous operators onto discrete tensor representations.

**Key observables.** Controlled error reproduction of conventional solutions (e.g., 1D diffusion), preservation of boundary conditions (Neumann).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Bond dimensions ($\sigma$), maximum bond degree ($M$), time step size ($\Delta t$), and the order of basis expansion ($B$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The primary assumptions include assuming locality of correlation to use MPS/PEPS ansatzes, and that the solution admits a compact tensor-network representation for computational tractability.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates the contraction of interaction kernels in TNs. Figure 2 shows the TN representation of an operator's left-hand side. Figures 7 and 8 demonstrate numerical convergence tests on nonlinear diffusion problems using both standard methods and the proposed TN variational approach.

**Paper structure.** The paper builds from general FE discretization theory to handling non-linearity by upgrading the Hilbert space to Fock space. It then shows how the resulting large coefficient tensors are compressed via TN ansatzes, culminating in optimization procedures tested on specific diffusion examples.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Operator equations (OEs) underpin quantitative modeling across science and engineering. Finite-element (FE) methods discretize continuous OEs into finite-dimensional algebraic systems, whereas tensor networks (TNs) provide flexible variational representations of correlated discrete systems. Here, we develop a framework that connects FE with TN for analytic OEs. The power of this method comes from its ability to convert highly non-linear partial differential equations into linear matrix equations. In particular, we show that FE discretization induces a hierarchy of multilinear interaction tensors, through which differential, integral, nonlinear, memory, and delay equations can be expressed within a common algebraic structure. The resulting systems are reformulated as weighted-residual optimization problems over TN degrees of freedom. Matrix-product-state calculations for one-dimensional linear and nonlinear diffusion reproduce conventional solutions with controlled error while preserving continuity and Neumann boundary conditions. The framework provides a common variational language for analytic OEs and establishes a direct connection between FE numerical formalism and TN variational algorithms, offering a general foundation for TN-based and quantum-inspired approaches to solving OEs.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper introduces a novel, low-cost 3D-printed platform for conducting room-temperature quantum transport measurements using break junctions. By analyzing conductance decay in the tunneling regime, the authors systematically extract the apparent work function ($\phi$) of contacts across different environments. This establishes an accessible and reproducible framework for nanoscale research on reactive materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on solid-state electronics, the rigorous statistical analysis of quantum transport signatures ($\phi$ distribution) provides valuable insights into surface physics and interface phenomena relevant to open quantum systems modeling.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** To develop an accessible, low-cost methodology for performing quantum transport measurements on reactive materials at room temperature.

**Main result.** The study successfully resolved the conductance quantum in highly reactive contacts and established that the apparent work function distribution follows a non-central chi-square distribution, matching theoretical predictions accounting for atomic roughness.

**Method.** Measurements were performed using a custom 3D-printed break junction setup to characterize current decay (tunneling regime) across various environments (vacuum, glycerol).

**Model / system.** The system uses a Mechanically Controllable Break Junction (MCBJ) housed in a PLA vacuum chamber. Quantum transport is analyzed using the Landauer formalism, relating conductance decay exponentially to the apparent work function ($\phi$).

**Key observables.** Conductance quantum ($G_0$), Apparent Work Function ($\phi$), Statistical distribution of $\phi$ (non-central chi-square).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Room temperature operation, high vacuum capability ($1.4 	imes 10^{-4}$ mbar), and the dependence on environmental adsorbates.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis assumes linearity in the tunneling regime for work function extraction and that the PLA platform is structurally sound for pressure differentials up to 1 atm.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate hardware setups, vacuum pump-down curves, conductance histograms showing $1G_0$ peaks across environments, and statistical fits of $\phi$ distributions using non-central chi-square models.

**Paper structure.** The paper details the low-cost experimental platform construction, presents measurements on reactive metals (Cu) in various atmospheres to test environmental robustness, and then uses Au as a benchmark to systematically extract and statistically analyze the apparent work function ($\phi$) from tunneling decay traces.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We present a low-cost, 3D-printed high-vacuum platform integrating a mechanically controllable break-junction system and a custom logarithmic amplifier for room-temperature quantum transport measurements. Using copper as a highly reactive test case, we successfully resolve the $1G_0$ conductance quantum under both high vacuum and anhydrous glycerol, demonstrating the effectiveness of these environments against rapid atmospheric oxidation. In parallel, utilizing gold as a robust benchmark, we systematically extract the apparent work function ($φ$) from thousands of tunneling traces across ambient air, vacuum, and glycerol. Our analysis demonstrates that the statistical distribution of $φ$ rigorously follows a non-central chi-square distribution. The obtained gold work functions match existing literature across all environments. Although lower than bulk values, they perfectly align with theoretical models accounting for atomic-scale roughness, apex geometry, and environmental adsorbates. Ultimately, this methodology establishes an accessible and reproducible framework for systematic nanoscale research on reactive materials.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically analyzes the bound states trapped in a line-shaped vortex within a 2D topological superconductor. By solving the Bogoliubov-de Gennes equations using elliptical coordinates, the authors predict the existence of a zero-energy Majorana state and quantized excited states. The findings provide crucial theoretical predictions for detecting topologically protected quantum phenomena in superconducting materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work provides a detailed theoretical framework for predicting robust zero-energy modes (Majorana fermions) in superconducting topological systems, which is highly relevant to realizing topological quantum computation platforms.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates zero-energy fermion bound states trapped within a line-shaped vortex core of a two-dimensional topological superconductor.

**Main result.** A zero-energy Majorana state is predicted to exist, alongside equally spaced excited states whose energies are determined by solving the Bogoliubov-de Gennes equations in elliptical coordinates.

**Method.** The analysis involves solving coupled differential equations derived from the BdG formalism using specialized techniques suited for line defects, including matching solutions across different radial regimes.

**Model / system.** The system is a 2D electron gas on a topological insulator surface, subjected to proximity-induced s-wave superconductivity and Rashba spin-orbit coupling. The geometry necessitates the use of elliptical coordinates $(\mu, 
u)$ due to the line-shaped vortex.

**Key observables.** Zero-energy Majorana state ($E_0=0$), quantized excited state energies ($E_m$), and the Local Density of States (LDOS) measured via STM.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The superconducting gap $\Delta(r)$ vanishes along the line defect ($\mu=0$); parameters include Fermi velocity ($v_F$) and coupling strengths.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Approximations are made in solving differential equations, particularly concerning the decay function $K(	ilde{ho})$ and neglecting higher-order terms in perturbative matching procedures.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate elliptical coordinates defining the vortex geometry; one shows the decay function $K(	ilde{ho})$; another tracks the convergence of excited state energy $E_1$; and a final figure demonstrates the characteristic asymmetry in the LDOS spectrum for different spin components.

**Paper structure.** The paper progresses from setting up the physical model and Hamiltonian in elliptical coordinates, to solving the BdG equations using Bessel/Hankel functions across matching boundaries ($	ilde{ho}_c$), culminating in deriving quantized energy expressions via perturbative matching conditions.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Fermion bound states in the core of a line-shaped vortex of a two-dimensional topological superconductor are investigated. The superconducting pairing potential, described in terms of elliptical coordinates, vanishes along a line defect with the two foci at the endpoints. The superconductivity is induced into a topological insulator via proximity effect with a type II s-wave superconductor. The spin and the momentum are perpendicularly locked by the strong spin-orbit coupling via Rashba interaction. A zero-energy Majorana state arises from the Berry phase together with a sequence of equally spaced fermion exitations. By solving the Bogoliubov-de Gennes equations using the method employed by Caroli, de Gennes and Matricon we calculate the energies, the wave-functions and spin-polarization of the bound states. An analytic expression for the local density of states within the vortex is obtained.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper theoretically examines graphene nanohelicoids by modeling their electronic structure using a tight-binding approach that incorporates anti-chiral symmetry. Key findings show that the band gap oscillates periodically with width, and the topological invariant (Zak phase) alternates between trivial and nontrivial states. This work provides deep insight into how geometric curvature dictates the fundamental quantum electronic properties of 2D materials.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter topology, the concept of engineered geometric constraints leading to protected electronic states (like those derived from symmetry) is highly relevant to designing quantum materials for novel quantum devices.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates the electronic properties and topological characteristics of graphene nanohelicoids (GNHs), which are structures where the honeycomb lattice is curved onto a helicoidal surface.

**Main result.** The band gap size in GNHs exhibits periodic closing and reopening as the width changes, accompanied by an alternating Zak phase that switches between trivial and nontrivial values.

**Method.** The authors employed the tight-binding approximation to derive effective one-dimensional lattice models from the 3D helical structure, supplemented by mathematical derivations involving recurrence relations for associated polynomials.

**Model / system.** The physical system is Graphene Nanohelicoids (GNHs). The model uses an effective 1D tight-binding Hamiltonian exhibiting anti-chiral symmetry ($E_v(k)=-E_c(k+\pi)$), contrasting with standard chiral symmetries.

**Key observables.** Band structure, Zak phase, band gap size, and the spectral relation $E_v(k) = -E_c(k + \pi)$.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Structure width ($W$), edge termination (zigzag vs. armchair), and momentum shift ($\pi$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis primarily uses the tight-binding approximation, considering only intralayer coupling, and relies on deriving analytic models for the effective 1D chain.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the GNH lattice model, compare band structures under anti-chiral vs. chiral constraints, and show how gap dependence varies with width or radius changes for zigzag and armchair edges.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically analyzes the electronic structure by first establishing the effective 1D model and symmetry properties (anti-chiral). It then details results based on edge termination (zigzag vs. armchair), concluding with advanced mathematical derivations concerning determinants and topological invariants using recurrence relations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We introduce graphene nanohelicoids, geometric analogues of graphene nanoribbons, in which the honeycomb lattice is embedded on a helicoidal surface. Starting from the three-dimensional helical structure, we construct effective one-dimensional lattice models with band structures characterized by a momentum-shifted particle-hole relation $E_v(k)=-E_c(k+π)$ that reflects an anti-chiral symmetry arising from the nonsymmorphic symmetry. A systematic investigation of graphene nanohelicoids using the tight-binding approximation reveals a number of trends upon varying width and edge orientation, for instance, alternating transitions between semiconducting and metallic regimes. As the structure width varies, the band gap periodically closes and reopens, accompanied by an alternating Zak phase that switches between trivial and nontrivial. We derive an analytic tight-binding model and introduce a continuous deformation of the graphene nanohelicoids that explains the origin of width-dependent band inversion and alternating Zak phase.

</details>

**Summary.** This paper presents the discovery of a previously unknown family of multihump-multivalley solitons (MHMVSs) using the Darboux transformation method on birefringent optical fibers. The research establishes that these solutions possess distinct topological characteristics, notably linking the virtual monopole field to both intensity zeros and poles in the complex plane.

**Why it may be interesting.** This work is highly relevant for theoretical physicists in quantum optics and open quantum systems as it provides new exact analytical solutions (MHMVSs) and reveals novel topological features within a coupled nonlinear wave system framework.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study aims to systematically derive and analyze multihump-multivalley soliton (MHMVS) families existing on a plane wave background within birefringent optical fibers.

**Main result.** The authors obtained exact solutions for MHMVSs, including arbitrary configurations (AMHMVSs), demonstrating a previously unknown soliton family and revealing that the virtual monopole field is equally determined by intensity zeros and poles.

**Method.** Exact solutions were derived using the Darboux transformation method applied to the two-component Fokas-Lenells equations. Stability was confirmed via numerical simulations under weak white noise.

**Model / system.** The physical system modeled is birefringent optical fibers, governed by the integrable two-component Fokas-Lenells equations (CFL equations). This system is an extension of the Manakov model incorporating self-steepening and spatio-temporal coupling effects.

**Key observables.** Multihump-Multivalley Solitons (MHMVSs), Arbitrary MHMVSs (AMHMVSs), virtual monopole field, intensity profiles (e.g., cube, star, hexagonal patterns).

**Important parameters / regimes.** Birefringence parameters ($\eta$), background wave amplitude ($a_s$), and spectral parameters ($\kappa$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The analysis is restricted to solitons propagating on a plane wave background, contrasting with previous work limited to vanishing backgrounds.

**Figures summary.** Figure 1 illustrates example intensity structures of MHMVSs on the plane wave, showing various patterns like cube, star, hexagonal, and triangular configurations.

**Paper structure.** The paper systematically derives solutions using the Darboux transformation method, classifies fundamental solutions via phase diagrams, analyzes the topological structure related to the virtual monopole field, and confirms stability through numerical simulations.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

We obtain a family of multihump-multivalley solitons (MHMVSs) on a plane-wave background in birefringent optical fibers governed by the two-component Fokas-Lenells equations, with exact solutions derived via the Darboux transformation method. The fundamental solutions are systematically classified through their phase diagrams, and higher-order configurations are identified as well. Notably, the construction extends to solitons with arbitrary MHMV structures, a class of solutions previously unreported in two-component integrable systems. Numerical simulations confirm the robustness of these solutions under weak white noise. Furthermore, analysis of their topological structure reveals that the virtual monopole field is determined equally by the intensity zeros and poles of MHMVSs in the complex plane, a feature that has remained unrecognized in previous studies of nonlinear wave topological phases. These findings reveal a previously unknown soliton family on a plane-wave background along with a distinct topological feature within the two-component framework, thereby enriching the broader understanding of topological phases of nonlinear waves.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study uses DFT calculations on the Cu/WO3 interface to demonstrate a robust spin-orbit coupling characterized by both linear and a remarkably large cubic Rashba term. The giant cubic parameter arises from orbital hybridization between W and Cu, establishing this light-metal oxide system as a novel platform for studying nonlinear spintronic phenomena.

**Why it may be interesting.** The discovery of a giant, tunable cubic Rashba effect in a light-metal/oxide platform opens new avenues for spintronics research beyond traditional heavy metal interfaces.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates emergent Rashba spin-orbit interaction (SOI) at interfaces between light metals and heavy-element oxide insulators, focusing on the interplay between linear and cubic Rashba effects.

**Main result.** The Cu/WO3 interface exhibits a robust Rashba splitting, yielding a giant intrinsic cubic Rashba parameter ($	ilde{\alpha}_R \approx -1.93 	ext{ eV}\cdot	ext{\AA}^3$) driven by W-Cu orbital hybridization.

**Method.** First-principles Density Functional Theory (DFT) calculations were employed to model the electronic structure and spin splitting at the heterostructure interface.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is the Cu(001)/WO3(001) heterostructure, analyzed using slab models incorporating Spin-Orbit Coupling (SOC). The analysis examines how Rashba parameters change with Cu thickness and surface orientation.

**Key observables.** Rashba spin-splitting magnitude ($\Delta E(k)$), intrinsic linear ($\alpha_R$) and cubic ($	ilde{\alpha}_R$) Rashba parameters, and orbital contributions to the interface state.

**Important parameters / regimes.** The giant cubic Rashba parameter $	ilde{\alpha}_R \approx -1.93 	ext{ eV}\cdot	ext{\AA}^3$ and the linear parameter $\alpha_R \approx 0.49 	ext{ eV}\cdot	ext{\AA}$ are key quantitative results.

**Assumptions / limitations.** The calculations use a simplified cubic phase for WO3, avoiding complex room-temperature monoclinic structures, while retaining necessary symmetry for surface analysis.

**Figures summary.** Figures illustrate the structural model and electronic band structure/spin textures, showing momentum-resolved spin splitting ($\Delta E(k)$) confirming both linear and cubic Rashba contributions in the interface state.

**Paper structure.** The paper proceeds by establishing the physical model (Cu/WO3 heterostructure), calculating the electronic properties using DFT+U with SOC, extracting the energy splitting dependence on momentum to fit $\alpha_R$ and $	ilde{\alpha}_R$, and finally analyzing the thickness dependence to isolate the intrinsic cubic term.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

Harnessing Rashba spin-orbit interaction and related spintronic functionalities has traditionally relied on metallic surfaces or interfaces containing elemental heavy metals. Here, using first-principles calculations and Cu(001)/WO$_3$(001) as a model heterostructure, we show that interfacing a light metal, Cu, with a band insulator, WO$_3$, yields an interface state that exhibits a robust Rashba spin splitting arising from the interplay between linear and cubic Rashba effects. The spin splitting is driven by the strong spin-orbit coupling of W atoms and enabled by W-Cu orbital hybridization at the interface. The cubic Rashba contribution asymptotically grows with Cu thickness and can be explained in terms of cross-coupling between the vacuum/Cu and Cu/WO$_3$ interfaces. This interfacial cross-coupling, however, diminishes at larger Cu thicknesses, allowing us to extract the intrinsic cubic Rashba parameter, which has a giant value of approximately -1.93 eV $Å^3$. In contrast, the linear Rashba parameter is only weakly affected by this cross-coupling and varies from approximately 0.30 to 0.49 eV Å. We further show that sizable linear and cubic Rashba effects persist across several interface geometries and Cu surface orientations, including (110) and (111). Our work identifies the Cu/WO$_3$ interface as a novel light-metal/heavy-element-based oxide platform for exploring the rich spectrum of Rashba physics, including linear and nonlinear spin-orbit phenomena.

</details>

**Summary.** This theoretical study investigates how doping A-sites in perovskite electrolytes like BaCeO3 can boost performance for SOFCs. The authors show that using small, highly electronegative dopants simultaneously improves proton conductivity by facilitating oxygen vacancies and enhances chemical stability by weakening detrimental bonds with acidic gases.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on materials science, the underlying physics involves defect chemistry, ionic/covalent bond characterization, and energy landscape mapping using advanced computational techniques applicable to complex many-body systems.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The primary challenge addressed is improving the chemical stability of BaCeO3-based electrolytes used in solid oxide fuel cells (SOFCs), as current doping strategies often fail to enhance both proton transport and stability simultaneously.

**Main result.** The study demonstrates that A-site doping with small-radius, high-electronegativity ions like Ca enhances performance by weakening A-O bonds (aiding vacancy formation) and inducing lattice contraction (improving thermal stability).

**Method.** The research relies heavily on first-principles calculations using DFT (VASP/PBE functional) to model structural changes, calculate defect energies, and map proton migration pathways.

**Model / system.** The system under investigation is the perovskite electrolyte structure, specifically comparing pristine BaCeO3 with Ca-doped variants like Ba0.875Ca0.125CeO3.

**Key observables.** Proton concentration/diffusion barriers, oxygen vacancy formation energy (E_vac), lattice contraction (tolerance factor change), and adsorption energies for acidic gases (CO2, SO2).

**Important parameters / regimes.** The key physical parameters are the ionic radii and electronegativities of the dopants, which govern the electronic and structural changes.

**Assumptions / limitations.** Calculations assume the Born-Oppenheimer approximation is valid for migration barriers, and the PBE functional may slightly overestimate lattice constants compared to experimental values.

**Figures summary.** Figures compare supercell structures of doped vs. pristine materials, show band structure/DOS comparisons, and illustrate structural changes related to doping effects.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a logical progression: identifying the stability limitation -> proposing A-site doping as a solution -> using first-principles methods to quantify how small radius (lattice contraction) and high electronegativity (weakened bonding) synergistically improve both proton transport and chemical resilience.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The practical application of BaCeO$_3$-based electrolytes is limited by their poor chemical stability in proton-conducting solid oxide fuel cells. Commonly employed B-site doping strategies typically improve proton transport with limited improvement in stability. Recent experiments show that A-site Ca doping can simultaneously enhance both properties. Here, through first-principles calculations and mechanistic analysis of Ca-doped BaCeO$_3$, we identify the synergistic roles of small-radius, high-electronegativity A-site dopants in governing proton transport and chemical stability in perovskite electrolytes. We show that the higher electronegativity of A-site dopant weakens the A-O ionic bonding, facilitating oxygen-vacancy formation and enhancing proton uptake by increasing the basicity. This weakened A-O interaction also suppresses the formation of impurity phases and reduces the adsorption strength of acidic gases such as CO$_2$ and SO$_2$. The lattice contraction induced by the smaller ionic radius improves thermal stability and can enhance proton diffusion in systems where proton transfer is the rate-limiting step. Furthermore, we find that Ca surface segregation can mitigate grain-boundary resistance effects. Our results demonstrate the advantages of A-site Ca doping in Ba-based electrolytes, clarify the mechanisms by which small-radius, high-electronegativity dopants influence proton transport and chemical stability, and provide guidance for the design of high-performance proton-conducting electrolytes.

</details>

**Summary.** This work theoretically analyzes the T-phase $	ext{MoH}_2$ monolayer, establishing it as a stable candidate for ambient-pressure superconductivity. Using first-principles calculations, the authors predict strong electron-phonon coupling ($\lambda=1.04$) leading to a conventional superconducting transition temperature of $14.4 	ext{ K}$. The findings position 2D hydrides as promising materials for low-dimensional quantum phenomena.

**Why it may be interesting.** While focused on condensed matter superconductivity, the rigorous application of advanced quantum many-body theory (Eliashberg equations) to a novel 2D material platform is methodologically rich for theoretical physicists.

<details markdown="1"><summary>Detailed structure</summary>

**Main problem.** The study investigates structural stability and phonon-mediated superconductivity in two-dimensional (2D) transition-metal hydrides to find ambient-pressure alternatives to high-temperature superconductors.

**Main result.** The T-phase of $	ext{MoH}_2$ is confirmed as the stable ground state, predicting a conventional superconducting critical temperature ($T_c$) of $14.4 	ext{ K}$ at ambient pressure.

**Method.** The research employs first-principles calculations (DFT) combined with Density Functional Perturbation Theory (DFPT) and solving the anisotropic Migdal-Eliashberg equations.

**Model / system.** The system modeled is the hexagonal molybdenum dihydride ($	ext{MoH}_2$) monolayer, comparing its T-phase (octahedral) to a less stable H-phase (trigonal prismatic).

**Key observables.** Superconducting transition temperature ($T_c$), electron-phonon coupling parameter ($\lambda$), structural energy difference, and elastic constants.

**Important parameters / regimes.** Ambient pressure; $\lambda = 1.04$; $T_c = 14.4 	ext{ K}$; Energy stabilization of T-phase over H-phase ($0.198 	ext{ eV}$).

**Assumptions / limitations.** The superconducting pairing mechanism is assumed to be conventional and mediated by phonons; the calculation uses a fixed Coulomb pseudopotential ($\mu^*=0.1$).

**Figures summary.** Figures detail structural comparisons (T vs H phase), thermal stability via AIMD, electronic structure (Fermi surface dominated by Mo $d$-orbitals), phonon dispersion/EPC spectral function ($\alpha^2F(\omega)$), and momentum-resolved superconducting gap closing.

**Paper structure.** The paper follows a logical progression: first establishing the ground state structural stability (T-phase vs H-phase); second, confirming mechanical and thermal robustness via elastic constants and AIMD; third, calculating electronic properties (metallicity) and phonon spectra; finally, applying the Eliashberg formalism to predict $T_c$.

</details>

<details markdown="1"><summary>Abstract</summary>

The realization of ambient-pressure, high-temperature superconductivity in hydrogen-rich materials remains a major pursuit in condensed-matter physics. While bulk hydrides require extreme pressures to stabilize, two-dimensional (2D) transition-metal hydrides offer a promising alternative to bypass these compression constraints. In this work, we investigate the structural stability, electronic properties, and phonon-mediated superconductivity of a hexagonal molybdenum dihydride (MoH2) monolayer using first-principles calculations. Total-energy evaluations reveal that the octahedral T-phase is energetically more favorable than the previously reported trigonal prismatic H-phase by 0.198 eV, establishing the T-phase as the true ground-state configuration. Consequently, we systematically evaluate the lattice dynamics and superconducting properties of this ground-state T-MoH2 monolayer within the frameworks of density functional perturbation theory (DFPT) and the anisotropic Migdal-Eliashberg formalism. The transition metal-hydrogen vibrational networks induce strong electron-phonon coupling (EPC), yielding an integrated coupling parameter of λ= 1.04. Solving the anisotropic Eliashberg equations predicts a conventional superconducting transition temperature (Tc of 14.4K) at ambient pressure, characterized by a moderately gap distribution (Δ= 2.07-3.01meV at 5.0 K). Our findings highlight the T-MoH2 monolayer as a structurally, mechanically, and thermally stable platform for exploring low-dimensional conventional superconductivity under ambient conditions.

</details>
